## I / I

===Original===
Miriam left the gaslit hall and went slowly upstairs. The March twilight lay upon the landings, but the staircase was almost dark. The top landing was quite dark and silent. There was no one about. It would be quiet in her room. She could sit by the fire and be quiet and think things over until Eve and Harriett came back with the parcels. She would have time to think about the journey and decide what she was going to say to the Fräulein.

Her new Saratoga trunk stood solid and gleaming in the firelight. Tomorrow it would be taken away and she would be gone. The room would be altogether Harriett’s. It would never have its old look again. She evaded the thought and moved clumsily to the nearest window. The outline of the round bed and the shapes of the may trees on either side of the bend of the drive were just visible. There was no escape for her thoughts in this direction. The sense of all she was leaving stirred uncontrollably as she stood looking down into the well-known garden.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆（Miriam）离开煤气灯照亮的门厅，慢慢走上楼去。三月的暮色铺在楼梯平台上，楼梯里却几乎黑透了。顶层平台一片漆黑，静悄悄的。四下没有人。她房间里会很安静的。她可以坐在炉火边，安安静静地把种种事情想一遍，等伊芙（Eve）和哈丽特（Harriett）带着买的东西回来。她会有时间想想这趟旅程，想好要对弗劳莱恩小姐（the Fräulein）说些什么。

她那只崭新的萨拉托加大衣箱（Saratoga trunk）结结实实立在炉火光里，闪闪发亮。明天它就会被搬走，她也要走了。这间房间就全归哈丽特了。它再也不会是从前的样子了。她避开这个念头，笨拙地挪到最近的窗前。依稀望得见圆形花坛的轮廓，还有车道拐弯两侧山楂树（may trees）的影子。朝这个方向想，思绪是无处可逃的。她站在那里俯瞰那座熟得不能再熟的花园，正在抛下这一切的感觉不由自主地翻涌起来。

===Original===
Out in the road beyond the invisible lime trees came the rumble of wheels. The gate creaked and the wheels crunched up the drive, slurring and stopping under the dining room window.

It was the Thursday afternoon piano-organ, the one that was always in tune. It was early today.

===Chinese===
外面路上，看不见的椴树那一边，传来车轮的辘辘声。大门吱呀一响，车轮碾着沙石上了车道，拖沓着，在饭厅窗下停住了。

是星期四下午的手摇风琴，那台总是调得准准的。今天来得早了些。

===Original===
She drew back from the window as the bass chords began thumping gently in the darkness. It was better that it should come now than later on, at dinnertime. She could get over it alone up here.

She went down the length of the room and knelt by the fireside with one hand on the mantelshelf so that she could get up noiselessly and be lighting the gas if anyone came in.

The organ was playing “The Wearin’ o’ the Green.”

It had begun that tune during the last term at school, in the summer. It made her think of rounders in the hot school garden, singing classes in the large green room, all the class shouting “Gather *ro*ses while ye may,” hot afternoons in the shady north room, the sound of turning pages, the hum of the garden beyond the sunblinds, meetings in the sixth form study.﻿﻿ … Lilla, with her black hair and the specks of bright amber in the brown of her eyes, talking about free will.

===Chinese===
低音和弦开始在黑暗里轻轻擂动，她从窗边退了回来。它现在来，总比晚些时候、等到晚饭时来好。她可以独自在这楼上把这阵熬过去。

她顺着房间走到尽头，跪在壁炉边，一只手搭在炉台上，这样一旦有人进来，她就能悄没声儿地站起来，装作正在点煤气灯。

风琴正奏着《穿戴绿衣》（The Wearin' o' the Green）。

这曲子是上个学期末在学校里流行起来的，在夏天。它让她想起学校花园里酷热中打的圆场球（rounders），那间绿色大房间里的唱歌课，全班喊着“及时采*撷*玫瑰”（Gather ro-ses while ye may），背阴的北屋里炎热的下午，翻动书页的沙沙声，遮阳帘外花园的嗡嗡声，六年级自习室里的聚会。……莉拉（Lilla），一头黑发，棕色的眼睛里嵌着亮琥珀色的斑点，谈着自由意志。

===Original===
She stirred the fire. The windows were quite dark. The flames shot up and shadows darted.

That summer, which still seemed near to her, was going to fade and desert her, leaving nothing behind. Tomorrow it would belong to a world which would go on without her, taking no heed. There would still be blissful days. But she would not be in them.

There would be no more silent sunny mornings with all the day ahead and nothing to do and no end anywhere to anything; no more sitting at the open window in the dining room, reading Lecky and Darwin and bound *Contemporary Reviews* with roses waiting in the garden to be worn in the afternoon, and Eve and Harriett somewhere about, washing blouses or copying waltzes from the library packet﻿﻿ … no more Harriett looking in at the end of the morning, rushing her off to the new grand piano to play the “Mikado” and the “Holy Family” duets. The tennis club would go on, but she would not be there. It would begin in May. Again there would be a white twinkling figure coming quickly along the pathway between the rows of hollyhocks every Saturday afternoon.

Why had he come to tea every Sunday﻿—never missing a single Sunday﻿—all the winter? Why did he say, “Play ‘Abide with Me,’ ” “Play ‘Abide with Me’ ” yesterday, if he didn’t care? What was the good of being so quiet and saying nothing? Why didn’t he say “Don’t go” or “When are you coming back?” Eve said he looked perfectly miserable.

There was nothing to look forward to now but governessing and old age. Perhaps Miss Gilkes was right.﻿﻿ … Get rid of men and muddles and have things just ordinary and be happy. “Make up your mind to be happy. You can be *perfectly* happy without anyone to think about.﻿﻿ …” Wearing that large cameo brooch﻿—long, white, flat-fingered hands and that quiet little laugh.﻿﻿ … The piano-organ had reached its last tune. In the midst of the final flourish of notes the door flew open. Miriam got quickly to her feet and felt for matches.

===Chinese===
她拨了拨火。窗子已经全黑了。火苗蹿起来，影子四处乱窜。

那个夏天，此刻似乎还近在眼前，却要褪色、要抛下她了，什么也不留下。明天它就属于一个没有她也要照样运转的世界，毫不在意。幸福的时光还会有。只是她不在其中了。

再也不会有那样安静晴朗的早晨了——一整天铺在前面，无事可做，任何东西都没有尽头；再也不能坐在饭厅敞开的窗前，读莱基（Lecky）、达尔文（Darwin）和合订的《当代评论》（Contemporary Review），花园里玫瑰等着下午去采来戴上，伊芙和哈丽特在什么地方洗上衣，或是从图书馆借来的乐谱包里抄圆舞曲……再也不会有哈丽特在早晨将尽时探进头来，把她拽到那架新的大钢琴前弹《日本天皇》和《圣家族》二重奏了。网球俱乐部还会照常，只是她不在那里了。五月就会开始。每逢星期六下午，又会有一个白色的、闪闪烁烁的身影，沿着两排蜀葵之间的小径快步走来。

为什么他整个冬天每个星期天都来喝茶——一个星期天都没落下？如果他并不在意，为什么昨天他说“弹《求主同住》吧”，一遍遍地说“弹《求主同住》吧”？这么安静、一言不发又有什么用？他为什么不说“别走”，或者问“你什么时候回来？”伊芙说他看上去惨兮兮的。

如今再没有什么可盼望的了，只有当家庭教师，然后老去。也许吉尔克斯小姐（Miss Gilkes）是对的。……摆脱掉男人和种种纷乱，让一切平平常常，就快活了。“下决心快活起来。没有一个可以惦记的人，你也*照样*能快活。……”她别着那枚大大的浮雕玉石胸针——长长的、白白的、手指扁扁的双手，还有那种轻轻的一笑。……手摇风琴已奏到最后一支曲子。最后的音群正翻涌之际，房门猛地开了。米丽亚姆一跃而起，摸索火柴。

---

===Original===
Harriett came in waggling a thin brown paper parcel.

“Did you hear the Intermezzo? What a dim religious! We got your old collars.”

Miriam took the parcel and subsided on to the hearthrug, looking with a new curiosity at Harriett’s little, round, firelit face, smiling tightly between the rim of her hard felt hat and the bright silk bow beneath her chin.

A footstep sounded on the landing and there was a gentle tap on the open door.

“Oh, come in, Eve﻿—bring some matches. Are the collars piquet, Harry?”

“No, they hadn’t got piquet, but they’re the plain shape you like. You may thank us they didn’t send you things with little rujabiba frills.”

===Chinese===
哈丽特走进来，手里晃着一只薄薄的棕色纸包。

“听见那支间奏曲（Intermezzo）了吗？好一片幽暗的虔诚！我们把你那种老式领子买来了。”

米丽亚姆接过纸包，一屁股坐到炉边地毯上，带着一种新的好奇打量哈丽特那张小小的圆脸——炉火映着，在硬毡帽的帽檐和下巴底下亮闪闪的丝蝴蝶结之间，绷紧地笑着。

楼梯平台上响起脚步声，敞开的门被轻轻叩了一下。

“哦，进来吧，伊芙——带几根火柴来。领子是皮凯（piquet）料的吗，哈里（Harry）？”

“不是，没有皮凯的了，不过是你喜欢的那种素样式。幸亏没给你挑上带小噜噜巴巴（rujabiba）褶边的东西，你得谢我们。”

===Original===
Eve came slenderly down the room and Miriam saw with relief that her outdoor things were off. As the gas flared up she drew comfort from her scarlet serge dress, and the soft crimson cheek and white brow of the profile raised towards the flaring jet.

“What are things like downstairs?” she said, staring into the fire.

“I don’t know,” said Eve. She sighed thoughtfully and sank into a carpet chair under the gas bracket. Miriam glanced at her troubled eyes.

“Pater’s only just come in. I think things are pretty rotten,” declared Harriett from the hearthrug.

“Isn’t it ghastly﻿—for all of us?” Miriam felt treacherously outspoken. It was a relief to be going away. She knew that this sense of relief made her able to speak. “It’s never knowing that’s so awful. Perhaps he’ll get some more money presently and things’ll go on again. Fancy mother having it always, ever since we were babies.”

===Chinese===
伊芙窈窕地穿过房间走来，米丽亚姆欣慰地看到她已经卸了出门的装束。煤气灯腾地亮起，她那身猩红哔叽裙，还有那迎着跳动的灯焰仰起的侧影——柔柔的绯红脸颊、洁白的额头——都让米丽亚姆心里安稳了些。

“楼下情况怎么样？”她盯着炉火说。

“我不知道。”伊芙说。她若有所思地叹了口气，陷进煤气灯支架下面那把铺着地毯面的椅子里。米丽亚姆瞥了瞥她那双忧烦的眼睛。

“爸爸（Pater）刚刚才回来。我看情况糟透了。”哈丽特在炉边地毯上宣布。

“这对我们大家——不都是可怕极了么？”米丽亚姆觉得自己这样直言不讳，近乎背叛。要走了，倒是一种解脱。她知道正是这种解脱感让她能够开口。“最可怕的是永远不知道。也许过些时候他又会弄到些钱，日子又能照旧过下去。想想妈妈，从我们还是婴儿起就一直受着这个。”

===Original===
“Don’t, Mim.”

“All right. I won’t tell you the words he said, how he put it about the difficulty of getting the money for my things.”

“*Don’t*, Mim.”

Miriam’s mind went back to the phrase and her mother’s agonised face. She felt utterly desolate in the warm room.

“I wish *I’d* got brains,” chirped Harriett, poking the fire with the toe of her boot.

“So you have—more than me.”

“Oh﻿—reely.”

===Chinese===
“别说了，米姆（Mim）。”

“好吧。我不跟你说他说的话了，不说他怎么讲给我置办行头凑钱有多难。”

“*别*说了，米姆。”

米丽亚姆的思绪回到那句话和母亲痛苦万分的脸上。在这间暖和的房间里，她只觉得一片荒凉。

“我*要*是有脑子就好了。”哈丽特叽叽喳喳地说，用靴子尖拨了拨炉火。

“你有的——比我强。”

“哦——真的呐。”

===Original===
“You know, I *know* girls, that things are as absolutely ghastly this time as they can possibly be and that something must be done.﻿﻿ … But you know it’s perfectly fearful to face that old school when it comes to the point.”

“Oh, my dear, it’ll be lovely,” said Eve; “all new and jolly, and think how you will enjoy those lectures, you’ll simply love them.”

“It’s all very well to say that. You know you’d feel ill with fright.”

“It’ll be all right﻿—for *you*﻿—once you’re there.”

Miriam stared into the fire and began to murmur shamefacedly.

“No more all day bézique.﻿﻿ … No more days in the West End.﻿﻿ … No more matinées﻿﻿ … no more exhibitions﻿﻿ … no more A.B.C. teas﻿﻿ … no more insane times﻿﻿ … no more anything.”

===Chinese===
“你们知道么，姑娘们，我*知道*这回事情已经糟得不能再糟了，总得做点什么。……可你们知道，真到了节骨眼上，要独自去面对那所老学校，实在太吓人了。”

“哦，亲爱的，会很好的。”伊芙说，“一切都是新鲜的、快活的，想想你会多么喜欢那些讲课，你准会爱上它们的。”

“说说倒容易。你自己知道，你会吓得浑身难受。”

“会没事的——对*你*来说——一到那儿就好了。”

米丽亚姆盯着炉火，开始面带羞惭地喃喃自语。

“再也不能整天打贝西克纸牌（bézique）了。……再也不能去西区逛日子了。……再没有日场戏……再没有画展……再没有 A.B.C. 茶室的下午茶……再没有那些疯疯癫癫的时光……再没有什么了。”

===Original===
“What about holidays? You’ll enjoy them all the more.”

“I shall be staid and governessy.”

“You mustn’t. You must be frivolous.”

Two deeply-burrowing dimples fastened the clean skin tightly over the bulge of Miriam’s smile.

“And marry a German professor,” she intoned blithely.

“Don’t—don’t for *goodney* say that before mother, Miriam.”

===Chinese===
“假期呢？你会加倍享受的。”

“我会变得又古板又一股家庭教师味儿。”

“可不能。你得轻浮点儿。”

两个深深陷落的酒窝把光洁的皮肤绷得紧紧的，兜住米丽亚姆绽开的笑。

“还要嫁个德国教授。”她快活地拖着调子念。

“别说——看在*老天鹅*（goodney）份上，别在妈妈面前说这个，米丽亚姆。”

===Original===
“D’you mean she minds me going?”

“My *dear*!”

Why did Eve use her cross voice?—stupid﻿﻿ … "for goodness’ sake," not "for goodney." Silly of Eve to talk slang.﻿﻿ …

“All right. I won’t.”

“Won’t marry a German professor, or won’t tell mother, do you mean?﻿﻿ … Oo—Crumbs! My old cake in the oven!” Harriett hopped to the door.

“Funny Harriett taking to cookery. It doesn’t seem a bit like her.”

===Chinese===
“你是说她在意我走？”

“我的*天*！”

为什么伊芙用了她生气的嗓音？——真蠢……是“看在老天爷份上”，不是“老天鹅”。伊芙说俚语真傻。……

“好吧。我不说了。”

“不嫁德国教授，还是不告诉妈妈，你说的是哪个？……哎呀——老天！我的蛋糕还在炉子里！”哈丽特一蹦而起，奔向门口。

“真怪，哈丽特居然学起烹饪来了。一点儿也不像她。”

===Original===
“She’ll have to do something﻿—so shall I, I s’pose.”

“It seems awful.”

“We shall simply have to.”

“It’s awful,” said Miriam, shivering.

“Poor old girl. I expect you feel horrid because you’re tired with all the packing and excitement.”

===Chinese===
“她总得做点什么——我想我也一样。”

“想起来真可怕。”

“我们只好如此。”

“真可怕。”米丽亚姆说着，打了个寒噤。

“可怜的姑娘。我看你觉得难受，是因为收拾行装又一阵折腾，你累了。”

===Original===
“Oh well, anyhow, it’s simply ghastly.”

“You’ll feel better tomorrow.”

“D’you think I shall?”

“Yes﻿—you’re so strong,” said Eve, flushing and examining her nails.

“How d’you mean?”

“Oh﻿—all sorts of ways.”

“What way?”

“Oh﻿—well﻿—you arranging all this﻿—I mean answering the advertisement and settling it all.”

===Chinese===
“唉，反正，就是糟透了。”

“你明天会好些的。”

“你觉得我会吗？”

“会的——你这么坚强。”伊芙说，红着脸端详自己的指甲。

“怎么讲？”

“哦——方方面面。”

“哪方面？”

“哦——这个——这一切都是你张罗的——我是说应征那则广告，把事情全都定下来。”

===Original===
“Oh well, you know you backed me up.”

“Oh yes, but other things.﻿﻿ …”

“What?”

“Oh, I was thinking about you having no religion.”

“Oh.”

“You must have such splendid principles to keep you straight,” said Eve, and cleared her throat, “I mean, you must have such a lot in you.”

“Me?”

“Yes, of course.”

“I don’t know where it comes in. What have I done?”

===Chinese===
“唉，你知道是你一直支持我的。”

“哦对，可还有些别的。……”

“什么？”

“哦，我在想你不信教这件事。”

“哦。”

“你一定有很了不起的操守在支撑着你不走歪路。”伊芙说，清了清嗓子，“我是说，你心里一定藏着很多很多东西。”

“我？”

“是啊，当然。”

“我不知道这从何说起。我做过什么？”

===Original===
“Oh, well, it isn’t so much what you’ve done﻿—you have such a good time.﻿﻿ … Everybody admires you and all that﻿﻿ … you know what I mean﻿—you’re so clever.﻿﻿ … You’re always in the right.”

“That’s just what everybody hates!”

“Well, my dear, I wish I had your mind.”

“You needn’t,” said Miriam.

“You’re all right﻿—you’ll come out all right. You’re one of those strong-minded people who have to go through a period of doubt.”

===Chinese===
“哦，这个，倒不在于你做过什么——你日子过得那么好。……人人都佩服你，还有那些……你懂我的意思——你那么聪明。……你永远是对的。”

“这正是人人讨厌的地方！”

“可我亲爱的，我真希望有你那样的头脑。”

“用不着。”米丽亚姆说。

“你没事的——你终究会好好的。你是那种意志坚强的人，免不了要经历一段怀疑的时期。”

===Original===
“But, my *dear*,” said Miriam grateful and proud, “I feel such a humbug. You know when I wrote that letter to the Fräulein I said I was a member of the Church. I know what it will be, I shall have to take the English girls to church.”

“Oh, well, you won’t mind that.”

“It will make me simply ill—I could *never* describe to you,” said Miriam, with her face aglow, “what it is to me to hear some silly man drone away with an undistributed middle term.”

“They’re not all like that.”

“Oh, well, then it will be ignoratio elenchi or *argumentum ad hominem*﻿—”

“Oh, yes, but they’re not the *service*.”

===Chinese===
“可是，我*亲爱的*，”米丽亚姆说，心里又感激又得意，“我觉得自己真是个骗子。你知道我给弗劳莱恩小姐写那封信的时候，说自己是国教会的一员。我知道会怎样的——我得带着那些英国女孩上教堂。”

“哦，这个嘛，你不会介意的。”

“那会让我简直要病倒——我*怎么*也没法向你形容，”米丽亚姆说，脸都发亮了，“听某个蠢男人嗡嗡地念叨一个中词不周延（undistributed middle term），对我来说是什么滋味。”

“他们不都是那样的。”

“哦，好吧，那就会是不相干谬误（ignoratio elenchi），或者*针对人身论证*（argumentum ad hominem）——”

“哦对，可那些并不是*礼拜仪式*呀。”

===Original===
“The service I can’t make head or tail of﻿—think of the Athanasian.”

“Yes.” Eve stirred uneasily and began to execute a gentle scale with her tiny tightly-knit blue and white hand upon her knee.

“It’ll be ghastly,” continued Miriam, “not having anyone to pour out to﻿—I’ve told you such a lot these last few days.”

“Yes, hasn’t it been funny? I seem to know you all at once so much better.”

“Well﻿—don’t you think I’m perfectly hateful?”

“No. I admire you more than ever. I think you’re simply splendid.”

“Then you simply don’t know me.”

“Yes I do. And you’ll be able to write to me.”

===Chinese===
“礼拜仪式我根本摸不着头脑——想想《亚他那修信经》吧。”

“是啊。”伊芙不安地动了动，用她那只小小的、织得紧紧的蓝白相间的手，在膝头轻轻跑了一串音阶。

“那会糟透的，”米丽亚姆接着说，“没有一个可以倾诉的人——这几天我跟你说了多少话啊。”

“是啊，多有意思，是不是？我好像一下子就把你了解得深多了。”

“那——你不觉得我讨厌透了么？”

“不。我比以往更佩服你。我觉得你真是了不起。”

“那你根本就不了解我。”

“我了解。而且你还可以给我写信。”

===Original===
Eve, easily weeping, hugged her and whispered, “You mustn’t. I can’t see you break down﻿—don’t﻿—don’t﻿—don’t. We can’t be blue your last night.﻿﻿ … Think of nice things.﻿﻿ … There *will* be nice things again﻿﻿ … there will, will, will, *will*.”

Miriam pursed her lips to a tight bunch and sat twisting her long thickish fingers. Eve stood up in her tears. Her smile and the curves of her mouth were unchanged by her weeping, and the crimson had spread and deepened a little in the long oval of her face. Miriam watched the changing crimson. Her eyes went to and fro between it and the neatly pinned masses of brown hair.

“I’m going to get some hot water,” said Eve, “and we’ll make ourselves glorious.”

Miriam watched her as she went down the long room﻿—the great oval of dark hair, the narrow neck, the narrow back, tight, plump little hands hanging in profile, white, with a purple pad near the wrist.

===Chinese===
伊芙动辄落泪，这时搂住她，悄声说：“不许这样。我看不得你垮下来——别——别——别。你的最后一晚，我们不能愁云惨雾的。……想点好的。……好日子*还*会有的……会的，会，会，*会的*。”

米丽亚姆把嘴唇抿成紧紧的一小撮，坐在那里绞着自己长长的、略粗的手指。伊芙含着泪站起身。她的微笑和嘴部的曲线丝毫没有因哭泣而改变，绯红在她那张长长的鹅蛋脸上漫开、又深了一层。米丽亚姆望着那变幻的绯红。目光在它和那一头别得整整齐齐的棕色发髻之间来回移动。

“我去弄点热水来，”伊芙说，“咱们把自己打扮得漂漂亮亮的。”

米丽亚姆望着她穿过长长的房间走去——深色头发拢成的大椭圆，纤细的脖颈，窄窄的背，绷紧的、丰润的小手垂在身侧，侧影里白白的，近腕处有一小块紫色的护垫。

---

===Original===
When Miriam woke the next morning she lay still with closed eyes. She had dreamed that she had been standing in a room in the German school and the staff had crowded round her, looking at her. They had dreadful eyes﻿—eyes like the eyes of hostesses she remembered, eyes she had seen in trains and ’buses, eyes from the old school. They came and stood and looked at her, and saw her as she was, without courage, without funds or good clothes or beauty, without charm or interest, without even the skill to play a part. They looked at her with loathing. “Board and lodging﻿—privilege to attend Masters’ lectures and laundry (body linen only).” That was all she had thought of and clutched at﻿—all along, since first she read the Fräulein’s letter. Her keep and the chance of learning﻿﻿ … and Germany﻿—Germany, *das deutsche Vaterland*﻿—Germany, all woods and mountains and tenderness﻿—Hermann and Dorothea in the dusk of a happy village.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨米丽亚姆醒来时，她闭着眼睛一动不动地躺着。她梦见自己站在德国学校的一个房间里，教职员们围拢过来打量她。她们的眼睛可怕极了——像她记得的那些女主人的眼睛，像她在火车上、公共马车里见过的眼睛，像老学校里的眼睛。她们走过来，站定，盯着她看，看穿了她的本来面目：没有勇气，没有钱，没有好衣裳，没有美貌，没有魅力，没有趣味，甚至连扮演一个角色的本事都没有。她们带着厌恶看她。“食宿——有旁听各位教师（Masters）讲课之特权，附洗衣（仅限贴身衣物）。”这就是她一路以来所想、所紧紧抓住的全部——从她第一次读弗劳莱恩小姐的信起。一张嘴的生活，一点学东西的机会……还有德国——德国，*德意志祖国*（das deutsche Vaterland）——德国，满是森林、群山和柔情——幸福村庄暮色里的《赫尔曼与多罗特娅》（Hermann und Dorothea）。

===Original===
And it would really be those women, expecting things of her. They would be so affable at first. She had been through it a million times﻿—all her life﻿—all eternity. They would smile those hateful women’s smiles﻿—smirks﻿—self-satisfied smiles as if everybody were agreed about everything. She loathed women. They always smiled. All the teachers had at school, all the girls, but Lilla. Eve did﻿﻿ … maddeningly sometimes﻿﻿ … Mother﻿﻿ … it was the only funny horrid thing about her. Harriett didn’t.﻿﻿ … Harriett laughed. She was strong and hard somehow.﻿﻿ …

Pater knew how hateful all the world of women were and despised them.

He never included her with them; or only sometimes when she pretended, or he didn’t understand.﻿﻿ …

Someone was saying “Hi!” a gurgling muffled shout, a long way off.

===Chinese===
而那真的会是那些女人，对她怀着种种期待。起初她们会客客气气得不得了。这一套她经历过千百万次了——一辈子——亘古以来。她们会堆出那种可恨的女人的笑——假笑——自满的笑，仿佛天下事人人都已商量妥帖。她厌恶女人。她们总是在笑。学校里所有老师都笑，所有女孩都笑，只有莉拉不。伊芙也笑……有时笑得让人发疯……妈妈……那是她身上唯一又好笑又可怕的地方。哈丽特不笑。……哈丽特是大笑。她不知怎么就是又结实又硬气。……

爸爸知道女人的整个世界有多可恨，并且鄙夷她们。

他从不把她算作她们一类；只偶尔——在她装模作样的时候，或是他没明白过来的时候。……

有人在喊“嘿！”，一声咕噜噜的、闷住的喊叫，隔得很远。

===Original===
She opened her eyes. It was bright morning. She saw the twist of Harriett’s body lying across the edge of the bed. With a gasp she flung herself over her own side. Harry, old Harry, jolly old Harry had remembered the Grand Ceremonial. In a moment her own head hung, her long hair flinging back on to the floor, her eyes gazing across under the bed at the reversed snub of Harriett’s face. It was flushed in the midst of the wiry hair which stuck out all round it but did not reach the floor. “Hi!” they gurgled solemnly, “Hi.﻿﻿ … Hi!” shaking their heads from side to side. Then their four frilled hands came down and they flumped out of the high bed.

They performed an uproarious toilet. It seemed so safe up there in the bright bare room. Miriam’s luggage had been removed. It was away somewhere in the house; far away and unreal and unfelt as her parents somewhere downstairs, and the servants away in the basement getting breakfast and Sarah and Eve always incredible, getting quietly up in the next room. Nothing was real but getting up with old Harriett in this old room.

===Chinese===
她睁开眼睛。是明亮的早晨。她看见哈丽特的身子拧着横在床沿上。她倒抽一口气，翻身扑过自己那一侧。哈里，老哈里，快活的老哈里还记得“大典礼”。转眼间她自己的头也倒垂下去，长发甩到地板上，眼睛从床底下望过去，正对着哈丽特那张倒过来的翘鼻子。那张脸红扑扑的，四周支棱着一圈铁硬的头发，倒也够不着地板。“嘿！”她们咕噜噜地、郑重其事地喊，“嘿。……嘿！”脑袋左摇右晃。然后四只带褶边的袖口的手撑下来，她们扑通一声跳下了那张高床。

她们喧喧闹闹地梳洗起来。在这间明亮空荡的楼上房间里，一切都那么安稳。米丽亚姆的行李已经搬走了。它在宅子里的什么地方；遥远、不真实、无从感知，就像在楼下什么地方的父母，就像在地下室准备早饭的仆人们，还有隔壁房间里总是让人难以置信地悄悄起身、规规矩矩的萨拉（Sarah）和伊芙。只有同老哈丽特在这间老房间里起床，才是唯一真实的事。

===Original===
She revelled in Harriett’s delicate buffoonery (“voluntary incongruity” she quoted to herself as she watched her)﻿﻿—the titles of some of the books on Harriett’s shelf, *Ungava; A Tale of the North*, *Grimm’s Fairy Tales*, *John Halifax*, *Swiss Family Robinson* made her laugh. The curtained recesses of the long room stretched away into space.

She went about dimpling and responding, singing and masquerading as her large hands did their work.

She intoned the titles on her own shelf﻿﻿—as a response to the quiet swearing and jesting accompanying Harriett’s occupations. “The Voyage of the Beeeeeeagle,” she sang “Scott’s *Poetical Works*.” *Villette*﻿—Longfellow﻿—Holy Bible *with* Apocrypha﻿﻿—*Egmont*﻿﻿—

“Binks!” squealed Harriett daintily. “Yink grink binks.”

“Books!” she responded in a low tone, and flushed as if she had given Harriett an affectionate hug. “My rotten books.﻿﻿ …” She would come back, and read all her books more carefully. She had packed some. She could not remember which and why.

===Chinese===
她尽情享受哈丽特精巧的滑稽表演（她一面看着，心里默诵“主动的不协调”（voluntary incongruity））——哈丽特书架上几本书的名字，《翁加瓦：北方的故事》（Ungava; A Tale of the North）、《格林童话》、《约翰·哈利法克斯》（John Halifax）、《瑞士家庭鲁滨逊》，逗得她直笑。长房间那一道道挂帘的凹室伸展开去，没入远方。

她带着酒窝走来走去，有唱有和，边干活边又唱又演，一双大手忙个不停。

她拖腔拿调地念自己书架上的书名——作为对哈丽特边干活边低声咒骂、打趣的回应。“《小——猎犬号航海记》（The Voyage of the Beeeeeeagle），”她唱道，“《司各特诗集》（Scott's Poetical Works）。”《维莱特》（Villette）——朗费罗（Longfellow）——《圣经》（含《次经》）——《艾格蒙特》（Egmont）——

“宾克斯！”哈丽特娇声尖叫。“因克·格林克·宾克斯。”

“布克斯（书）！”她低声应道，脸红了，仿佛给了哈丽特一个爱意的拥抱。“我这些破书。……”她会回来的，要把她所有的书都细细重读一遍。有一些她已经装了箱。她记不得是哪些，也记不得为什么。

===Original===
“Binks,” she said, and it was quite easy for them to crowd together at the little dressing table. Harriett was standing in her little faded red moirette petticoat and a blue flannelette dressing jacket brushing her wiry hair. Miriam reflected that she need no longer hate her for the set of her clothes round her hips. She caught sight of her own faded jersey and stiff, shapeless black petticoat in the mirror. Harriett’s “Hinde’s” lay on the dressing table, her own still lifted the skin of her forehead in suffused puckerings against the shank of each pin.

===Chinese===
“宾克斯。”她说，于是她们俩挤到小梳妆台前，一点儿也不费事。哈丽特穿着她那条小小的褪了色的红莫雷特绸衬裙和蓝色棉法兰绒晨衣，刷着她那头铁硬的头发。米丽亚姆心想，自己再不必为衣裳在哈丽特胯部的样子而恨她了。她在镜子里瞥见自己褪色的针织上衣和硬邦邦、没个形状的黑衬裙。哈丽特那盒“欣德牌”（Hinde's）发夹搁在梳妆台上，她自己那几枚还别着，把额头的皮肤顶起一道道泛红的小褶，紧箍着每根发夹的针梗。

===Original===
Unperceived, she eyed the tiny stiff plait of hair which stuck out almost horizontally from the nape of Harriett’s neck, and watched her combing out the tightly-curled fringe standing stubbly out along her forehead and extending like a thickset hedge midway across the crown of her head, where it stopped abruptly against the sleekly-brushed longer strands which strained over her poll and disappeared into the plait.

“Your old wool’ll be just right in Germany,” remarked Harriett.

“Mm.”

“You ought to do it in basket plaits like Sarah.”

“I wish I could. I can’t think how she does it.”

“Ike spect it’s easy enough.”

“Mm.”

“But you’re all right, anyhow.”

“Anyhow, it’s no good bothering when you’re plain.”

===Chinese===
她趁人不备，端详着从哈丽特后颈几乎水平支出来的那一小截又硬又小的辫子，看她梳理那排紧紧卷曲的刘海——沿着额头短短地立着，又像一道矮壮的树篱伸过头顶一半，在那里猛然止住，让位给刷得油光水滑的长发，那些长发绷过头顶，没进辫子里。

“你那头旧羊毛到了德国正好。”哈丽特评论道。

“嗯。”

“你该学萨拉，编成篮式辫（basket plaits）。”

“我要是会就好了。我怎么也想不出她是怎么编的。”

“我瞅（Ike spect）挺容易的。”

“嗯。”

“不过不管怎么说，你这样挺好。”

“反正，人长得平常，操心也没用。”

===Original===
“You’re *not* plain.”

Miriam looked sharply round.

“Go on, Gooby.”

“You’re not. You don’t know. Granny said you’ll be a bonny woman, and Sarah thinks you’ve got the best shape face and the best complexion of any of us, and cook was simply crying her eyes out last night and said you were the light of the house with your happy, pretty face, and mother said you’re much too attractive to go about alone, and that’s partly why Pater’s going with you to Hanover, silly.﻿﻿ … You’re not plain,” she gasped.

Miriam’s amazement silenced her. She stood back from the mirror. She could not look into it until Harriett had gone. The phrases she had just heard rang in her head without meaning. But she knew she would remember all of them. She went on doing her hair with downcast eyes. She had seen Harriett vividly, and had longed to crush her in her arms and kiss her little round cheeks and the snub of her nose. Then she wanted her to be gone.

===Chinese===
“你*才*不是相貌平平呢。”

米丽亚姆霍地回过头来。

“去你的，古比（Gooby）。”

“你不难看。你不知道罢了。奶奶说你会长成一个标致女人，萨拉觉得咱们几个里数你的脸型最好、气色最好，厨娘昨天晚上简直哭肿了眼，说你那张快快活活、漂漂亮亮的脸是这个家的光，妈妈又说你太招人喜欢了，不能一个人出门，爸爸所以要陪你去汉诺威（Hanover），一部分也是为这个，傻姑娘。……你不难看。”她一口气说完，直喘气。

米丽亚姆惊得说不出话来。她从镜子前退开。哈丽特不走，她没法照镜子。刚才听到的话在她脑子里嗡嗡回响，却不知所云。可她知道，她会一字不落地全都记住。她垂着眼睛继续梳头。她刚刚清清楚楚地看见了哈丽特，恨不得把她搂进怀里狠狠抱住，亲亲她的小圆脸和那个翘鼻子。随即她又盼着她快走。

===Original===
Presently Harriett took up a brooch and skated down the room, “Ta‑ra‑ra‑la‑eee‑tee!” she carolled, “don’t be long,” and disappeared.

“I’m pretty,” murmured Miriam, planting herself in front of the dressing table. “I’m pretty—they like me—they *like* me. Why didn’t I know?” She did not look into the mirror. “They all like me, *me*.”

The sound of the breakfast bell came clanging up through the house. She hurried to her side of the curtained recess. Hanging there were her old red stockinette jersey and her blue skirt﻿﻿ … never again﻿﻿ … just once more﻿﻿ … she could change afterwards. Her brown, heavy best dress with puffed and gauged sleeves and thick gauged and gathered boned bodice was in her hand. She hung it once more on its peg and quickly put on her old things. The jersey was shiny with wear. “You darling old things,” she muttered as her arms slipped down the sleeves.

===Chinese===
过了一会儿，哈丽特拿起一枚胸针，滑冰似的溜过房间，“塔-拉-拉-拉-咿-蒂！”她欢声唱道，“别磨蹭哦”，人就不见了。

“我好看。”米丽亚姆喃喃道，一屁股站定在梳妆台前。“我好看——他们喜欢我——他们*喜欢*我。我以前怎么会不知道？”她没有照镜子。“他们都喜欢我，喜欢*我*。”

早饭铃的声音当当地穿过整座宅子传上来。她急忙跑到挂帘凹室自己那一侧。挂在那儿的是她那件旧的红色平针针织上衣和蓝裙子……再也不能穿了……就再穿一回……回头再换好了。她那件棕色厚重的最佳裙装——泡泡的抽褶袖、厚厚的打褶收拢的骨撑紧身胸衣——正拿在她手里。她又把它挂回挂钩上，飞快地穿上旧衣裳。那件针织上衣都磨得发亮了。“你们这些可爱的老伙计。”她咕哝着，胳膊滑进袖子。

===Original===
The door of the next room opened quietly and she heard Sarah and Eve go decorously downstairs. She waited until their footsteps had died away and then went very slowly down the first flight, fastening her belt. She stopped at the landing window, tucking the frayed end of the petersham under the frame of the buckle﻿﻿ … they were all downstairs, liking her. She could not face them. She was too excited and too shy.﻿﻿ … She had never once thought of their “feeling” her going away﻿﻿ … saying goodbye to each one﻿﻿ … all minding and sorry﻿—even the servants. She glanced fearfully out into the garden, seeing nothing. Someone called up from the breakfast room doorway, “Mim‑my!” How surprised Mr. Bart had been when he discovered that they themselves never knew whose voice it was of all four of them unless you saw the person, “but yours is really richer”﻿﻿ … it was cheek to say that.

“Mim‑my!”

Suddenly she longed to be gone﻿—to have it all over and be gone.

She heard the kak-kak of Harriett’s wooden heeled slippers across the tiled hall. She glanced down the well of the staircase. Harriett was mightily swinging the bell, scattering a little spray of notes at each end of her swing.

With a frightened face Miriam crept back up the stairs. Violently slamming the bedroom door, “I’m a-comin’﻿—I’m a-comin’,” she shouted and ran downstairs.

===Chinese===
隔壁房间的门轻轻开了，她听见萨拉和伊芙规规矩矩地下楼去。她等到脚步声消散，才非常缓慢地走下第一段楼梯，一边系腰带。她在楼梯平台的窗前停下，把磨破的彼得沙姆（petersham）腰带末端塞进带扣的框里……他们都在楼下，都喜欢她。她没法面对他们。她又太激动，又太害臊。……她从来没有想到过他们会为她的离去“有什么感觉”……要同每一个人告别……个个都放在心上、都不舍得——连仆人们也是。她惶惶地朝花园里瞥了一眼，什么也没看见。有人在早餐厅门口朝上喊：“米——米！”巴特先生（Mr. Bart）当初发现她们四姐妹的声音连她们自己都分不清谁是谁，除非亲眼见到人，他是多么惊讶——“不过你的嗓音确实更醇厚”……说这话真不知害臊。

“米——米！”

突然她渴望走了——恨不得一切统统了结，人已经在路上。

她听见哈丽特木跟拖鞋的咔嗒咔嗒声穿过铺砖的门厅。她朝楼梯井里瞥了一眼。哈丽特正起劲地抡着铃，每抡到一头，就洒出一小串铃声。

米丽亚姆带着一张受了惊的脸，蹑手蹑脚地溜回楼上。她砰的一声狠摔上卧室门，“来啦——来啦，”她喊着，跑下楼去。

## II / II

===Original===
The crossing was over. They were arriving. The movement of the little steamer that had collected the passengers from the packet boat drove the raw air against Miriam’s face. In her tired brain the grey river and the flat misty shores slid constantly into a vision of the gaslit dining room at home﻿ ﻿… the large clear glowing fire, the sounds of the family voices. Every effort to obliterate the picture brought back again the moment that had come at the dinner table as they all sat silent for an instant with downcast eyes and she had suddenly longed to go on forever just sitting there with them all.

Now, in the boat she wanted to be free for the strange grey river and the grey shores. But the home scenes recurred relentlessly. Again and again she went through the last moments﻿ ﻿… the goodbyes, the unexpected convulsive force of her mother’s arms, her own dreadful inability to give any answering embrace. She could not remember saying a single word. There had been a feeling that came like a tide carrying her away. Eager and dumb and remorseful she had gone out of the house and into the cab with Sarah, and then had come the long sitting in the loop-line train﻿ ﻿… “talk about something”﻿ ﻿… Sarah sitting opposite and her unchanged voice saying “What shall we talk about?” And then a long waiting, and the brown leather strap swinging against the yellow grained door, the smell of dust and the dirty wooden flooring, with the noise of the wheels underneath going to the swinging tune of one of Heller’s *Sleepless Nights*. The train had made her sway with its movements. How still Sarah seemed to sit, fixed in the old life. Nothing had come but strange cruel emotions.

After the suburban train nothing was distinct until the warm snowflakes were drifting against her face through the cold darkness on Harwich quay. Then, after what seemed like a great loop of time spent going helplessly up a gangway towards “the world” she had stood, face to face with the pale polite stewardess in her cabin. “I had better have a lemon, cut in two,” she had said, feeling suddenly stifled with fear. For hours she had lain despairing, watching the slowly swaying walls of her cabin or sinking with closed eyes through invertebrate dipping spaces. Before each releasing paroxysm she told herself “this is like death; one day I shall die, it will be like this.”

She supposed there would be breakfast soon on shore, a firm room and a teapot and cups and saucers. Cold and exhaustion would come to an end. She would be talking to her father.

===Chinese===
渡海结束了。他们就要到了。从邮船上接走乘客的小汽船一路开动，把湿冷的生风刮在米丽亚姆脸上。在她疲惫的脑海里，灰色的河流和平坦雾蒙蒙的海岸不断滑入家中那间煤气灯餐厅的幻象……又大又亮、烧得通红的炉火，家人说话的声音。每一次想把这画面抹去的努力，都又带回晚餐桌上的那一刻——全家人垂着眼、静默地坐了一瞬，她突然渴望就这样永远和他们大家一起坐在那里。

此刻，在船上，她想把自己放开，交给这陌生灰暗的河流和灰色的海岸。可家中的场景无情地一再重现。她一遍又一遍地重温最后那些时刻……道别，母亲双臂那出人意料的痉挛般的力量，她自己可怖地竟给不出任何回应的拥抱。她不记得自己说过一个字。有一种感觉曾像潮水般涌来，把她整个人卷走。她急切、失语、又满心悔疚地走出家门，和萨拉一起上了马车，然后是环线火车里漫长的枯坐……"说点什么吧"……萨拉坐在对面，用她一成不变的声音说："我们说什么呢？"接着是长长的等待，棕色的皮带吊环在黄色木纹的车门上晃荡，尘土的气味，脏污的木地板，脚下车轮的声响，合着赫勒《不眠之夜》里一支曲子的摇摆调子。火车摇晃着她随之一晃一晃。萨拉坐得多么安稳，仿佛钉在了旧日的生活里。除了陌生而残酷的情绪，什么都没有来。

上了郊区火车之后，一切都模糊不清，直到温暖的雪花飘上她的脸，穿过哈里奇码头上寒冷的黑暗。然后，仿佛绕过了时间的一大圈，无助地登上一条跳板、走向"外面的世界"之后，她站在自己的船舱里，与那位面色苍白、客气有礼的女乘务员面对面。"我最好要一个柠檬，切成两半，"她当时说，突然感到被恐惧噎住。一连好几个小时她绝望地躺着，望着船舱四壁缓缓摇晃，或是闭着眼睛沉入无脊椎般起伏俯冲的空间。每一阵带来解脱的剧呕发作之前，她都告诉自己："这就像死；总有一天我会死的，就是这个样子。"

她想，岸上很快就会有一顿早餐，一间踏实的房间，一把茶壶，杯子和碟子。寒冷和疲惫终会结束。她将和父亲说话。

---

===Original===
He was standing near her with the Dutchman who had helped her off the boat and looked after her luggage. The Dutchman was listening, deferentially. Miriam saw the strong dark blue beam of his eyes.

“Very good, very good,” she heard him say, “fine education in German schools.”

Both men were smoking cigars.

She wanted to draw herself upright and shake out her clothes.

“Select,” she heard, “excellent staff of masters﻿ ﻿… daughters of gentlemen.”

“Pater is trying to make the Dutchman think I am being taken as a pupil to a finishing school in Germany.” She thought of her lonely pilgrimage to the West End agency, of her humiliating interview, of her heart-sinking acceptance of the post, the excitements and misgivings she had had, of her sudden challenge of them all that evening after dinner, and their dismay and remonstrance and reproaches﻿—of her fear and determination in insisting and carrying her point and making them begin to be interested in her plan.

But she shared her father’s satisfaction in impressing the Dutchman. She knew that she was at one with him in that. She glanced at him. There could be no doubt that he was playing the role of the English gentleman. Poor dear. It was what he had always wanted to be. He had sacrificed everything to the idea of being a “person of leisure and cultivation.” Well, after all, it was true in a way. He was﻿—and he had, she knew, always wanted her to be the same and she *was* going to finish her education abroad﻿ ﻿… in Germany.﻿ ﻿… They were nearing a little low quay backed by a tremendous saffron-coloured hoarding announcing in black letters “Sunlight Zeep.”

===Chinese===
他站在她近旁，和那个扶她下船、照看行李的荷兰人在一起。荷兰人恭恭敬敬地听着。米丽亚姆看见他眼睛里强烈深蓝的光芒。

"很好，很好，"她听见他说，"德国学校里一流的教育。"

两个男人都抽着雪茄。

她想挺直身子，抖开身上的衣服。

"上流的，"她听见，"一流的师资……全是绅士人家的女儿。"

"爸爸是想让这个荷兰人以为，我是作为一名学生被送进德国一所女子进修学校的。"她想起自己孤身一人去西区那家介绍所的朝圣之行，想起那场屈辱的面谈，想起她心一沉地接受了这个职位，那些兴奋与忐忑；想起那天晚饭后她突然向全家发难，他们的惊愕、劝阻与责备——想起她的恐惧与决心，硬是坚持到底、逼得让步，让他们终于开始对她的计划有了兴趣。

但她分享了父亲镇住荷兰人的那份得意。她知道在这件事上她和他是一条心。她瞥了他一眼。毫无疑问，他正在扮演英国绅士的角色。可怜的、亲爱的人。那正是他一直想成为的。为了做"一个有闲而有教养的人"，他牺牲了一切。嗯，毕竟，在某种意义上这是真的。他确实是——而且她知道，他也一直想让她成为同样的人，而她*确实*要到国外去完成学业……在德国。……他们正驶近一座低矮的小码头，码头后面立着一大片藏红花色的广告牌，用黑字写着"Sunlight Zeep"（日光肥皂）。

---

===Original===
“Did you see, Pater; did you *see*?”

They were walking rapidly along the quay.

“Did you see? Sunlight *Zeep*!”

She listened to his slightly scuffling stride at her side.

Glancing up she saw his face excited and important. He was not listening. He was being an English gentleman, “emerging” from the Dutch railway station.

“Sunlight *Zeep*,” she shouted. “*Zeep*, Pater!”

He glanced down at her and smiled condescendingly.

“Ah, yes,” he admitted with a laugh.

There were Dutch faces for Miriam﻿—men, women and children coming towards her with sturdy gait.

“They’re talking Dutch! They’re all talking *Dutch*!”

The foreign voices, the echoes in the little narrow street, the flat waterside effect of the sounds, the bright clearness she had read of, brought tears to her eyes.

“The others *must* come here,” she told herself, pitying them all.

They had an English breakfast at the Victoria Hotel and went out and hurried about the little streets. They bought cigars and rode through the town on a little tramway. Presently they were in a train watching the Dutch landscape go by. One level stretch succeeded another. Miriam wanted to go out alone under the grey sky and walk over the flat fields shut in by poplars.

She looked at the dykes and the windmills with indifferent eyes, but her desire for the flat meadows grew.

Late at night, seated wide-awake opposite her sleeping companion, rushing towards the German city, she began to think.

===Chinese===
"你看见了吗，爸爸；你*看见*了吗？"

他们沿着码头快步走着。

"你看见了吗？Sunlight *Zeep*（日光肥皂）！"

她听着身旁他的脚步声，微微拖沓着擦过地面。

抬眼一瞥，她看见他的脸兴奋而郑重。他没有在听。他正在当一位英国绅士，正从荷兰的火车站里"现身"。

"Sunlight *Zeep*，"她喊道，"*Zeep*（肥皂），爸爸！"

他低头瞥了她一眼，带着居高临下的微笑。

"啊，是的，"他笑着承认了。

在米丽亚姆眼前是一张张荷兰面孔——男人、女人和孩子迈着坚实的步伐朝她走来。

"他们在说荷兰语！他们全都在说*荷兰语*！"

异国的语音，狭窄小街里的回声，水边平坦开阔的声效，还有她在书里读到过的那种明亮清澈，让她眼眶发热。

"其他几个*一定*得来这儿，"她对自己说，为他们大家感到惋惜。

他们在维多利亚饭店吃了一顿英式早餐，然后出门，在小小的街巷里匆匆逛了一通。他们买了雪茄，坐小电车穿城而过。不久他们就上了火车，看着荷兰的风景一路掠过。一片平坦的地面接着又一片。米丽亚姆想独自走出去，走到灰色天空下，在白杨树围拢的平坦田野上散步。

她漠然望着沟渠和风车，但对平坦草甸的渴望却越来越强。

深夜，她毫无睡意地坐在熟睡的旅伴对面，朝那座德国城市疾驰而去，她开始思索。

---

===Original===
It was a fool’s errand.﻿ ﻿… To undertake to go to the German school and teach﻿ ﻿… to be going there﻿ ﻿… with nothing to give. The moment would come when there would be a class sitting round a table waiting for her to speak. She imagined one of the rooms at the old school, full of scornful girls.﻿ ﻿… How was English taught? How did you begin? English grammar﻿ ﻿… in German? Her heart beat in her throat. She had never thought of that﻿ ﻿… the rules of English grammar? Parsing and analysis.﻿ ﻿… Anglo-Saxon prefixes and suffixes﻿ ﻿… gerundial infinitive.﻿ ﻿… It was too late to look anything up. Perhaps there would be a class tomorrow.﻿ ﻿… The German lessons at school had been dreadfully good.﻿ ﻿… Fräulein’s grave face﻿ ﻿… her perfect knowledge of every rule﻿ ﻿… her clear explanations in English﻿ ﻿… her examples.﻿ ﻿… All these things were there, in English grammar.﻿ ﻿… And she had undertaken to teach them and could not even speak German.

Monsieur﻿ ﻿… had talked French all the time﻿ ﻿… *dictées*﻿ ﻿… lectures﻿ ﻿… *Le Conscrit*﻿ ﻿… *Waterloo*﻿ ﻿… “La Maison déserte”﻿ ﻿… his careful voice reading on and on﻿ ﻿… until the room disappeared.﻿ ﻿… She must do that for her German girls. Read English to them and make them happy.﻿ ﻿… But first there must be verbs﻿ ﻿… there had been *cahiers* of them﻿ ﻿… first, second, third conjugation.﻿ ﻿… It was impudence, an impudent invasion﻿ ﻿… the dreadful clever, foreign school.﻿ ﻿… They would laugh at her.﻿ ﻿… She began to repeat the English alphabet.﻿ ﻿… She doubted whether, faced with a class, she could reach the end without a mistake.﻿ ﻿… She reached *z* and went on to the parts of speech.

===Chinese===
这是一桩蠢人的差事。……竟答应去德国的学校教书……竟然就这样去了……却拿不出任何可以给人的东西。那一刻总会来的：一班学生围坐在桌旁，等她开口。她想象出旧日学校里的一间屋子，坐满了满脸轻蔑的女孩。……英语是怎么教的？从哪里开始？英语语法……用德语讲？她的心跳到了嗓子眼。这个她从没想过……英语语法的规则？析句与分析。……盎格鲁-撒克逊的前缀和后缀……动名词式不定式。……想查什么都已经太迟了。也许明天就有课。……学校里的德语课好得可怕。……弗劳莱恩小姐端庄的脸……她对每一条规则都烂熟于心……她用英语作的清晰讲解……她的例句。……这一切，英语语法里全都有。……而她竟承接下来要教这些东西，却连德语都不会说。

Monsieur（先生）……从头到尾都说法语……听写……讲读……《应征入伍者》……《滑铁卢》……《荒宅》（La Maison déserte）……他那小心翼翼的嗓音读啊读啊……读到整间屋子都消失了。……她也要为她的德国姑娘们这样做。给她们读英语，让她们快乐。……可首先得有动词……当年有一本一本的练习簿全是动词……第一、第二、第三变位。……这是狂妄，一场狂妄的入侵……那所聪明得可怕的外国学校。……她们会笑话她的。……她开始默背英文字母表。……她怀疑，真面对一个班的时候，自己能不能不出一个错地背到底。……她背到了 z，又接着背词类。

---

===Original===
There would be a moment when she must have an explanation with the Fräulein. Perhaps she could tell her that she found the teaching was beyond her scope and then find a place somewhere as a servant. She remembered things she had heard about German servants﻿—that whenever they even dusted a room they cleaned the windows and on Sundays they waited at lunch in muslin dresses and afterwards went to balls. She feared even the German servants would despise her. They had never been allowed into the kitchen at home except when there was jam-making﻿ ﻿… she had never made a bed in her life.﻿ ﻿… A shop? But that would mean knowing German and being quick at giving change. Impossible. Perhaps she could find some English people in Hanover who would help her. There was an English colony she knew, and an English church. But that would be like going back. That must not happen. She would rather stay abroad on any terms﻿—away from England﻿—English people. She had scented something, a sort of confidence, everywhere, in her hours in Holland, the brisk manner of the German railway officials and the serene assurance of the travelling Germans she had seen, confirmed her impression. Away out here, the sense of imminent catastrophe that had shadowed all her life so far, had disappeared. Even here in this dim carriage, with disgrace ahead she felt that there was freedom somewhere at hand. Whatever happened she would hold to that.

===Chinese===
总会有那么一刻，她必须同弗劳莱恩小姐把话说明白。也许她可以告诉她，自己觉得教书超出了自己的能力，然后到什么地方找个女仆的差事。她想起听人说过的那些关于德国女仆的事——哪怕只是掸一掸屋子的灰，她们也要把窗子擦干净，星期天她们穿着细棉纱裙侍候午餐，之后就去参加舞会。她怕连德国女仆都会看不起她。在家里她们从来不许进厨房，只有熬果酱的时候例外……她这辈子从没铺过一次床。……去店铺做事？可那就意味着要会德语，找零钱还要手快。不可能。也许她能在汉诺威找到几个愿意帮她一把的英国人。她知道那儿有一个英国人聚居区，还有一座英国教堂。可那就等于走回头路。那绝不可以。她宁愿以任何条件留在国外——远离英国——远离英国人。在荷兰的那几个小时里，她在各处都嗅到了一种东西，一种笃定；德国铁路职员干练利落的做派、她所见到的旅行中的德国人那种安详的自信，都印证了她的印象。在这遥远的异乡，那种大难临头的感觉——它至今一直笼罩着她全部的生活——消失了。即使在这昏暗的车厢里，纵然前头等着的是羞辱，她也感到自由就在近旁的什么地方。无论发生什么，她都要守住这一点。

---

===Original===
She glanced up at her small leather handbag lying in the rack and thought of the solid money in her purse. Twenty-five shillings. It was a large sum and she was to have more as she needed.

She glanced across at the pale face with its point of reddish beard, the long white hands laid one upon the other on the crossed knees. He had given her twenty-five shillings and there was her fare and his, and his return fare and her new trunk and all the things she had needed. It must be the end of taking money from him. She was grown up. She was the strong-minded one. She must manage. With a false position ahead and after a short space, disaster, she must get along.

The peaceful Dutch fields came to her mind. They looked so secure. They had passed by too soon. We have always been in a false position, she pondered. Always lying and pretending and keeping up a show﻿—never daring to tell anybody.﻿ ﻿… Did she want to tell anybody? To come out into the open and be helped and have things arranged for her and do things like other people? No.﻿ ﻿… No.﻿ ﻿… “Miriam always likes to be different”—“Society is no boon to those not sociable.” Dreadful things﻿ ﻿… and the girls laughing together about them. What did they really mean?

“Society is no boon to those not sociable”—on her birthday page in Ellen Sharpe’s birthday book. Ellen handed it to her going upstairs and had chanted the words out to the others and smiled her smile﻿ ﻿… she had not asked her to write her name﻿ ﻿… was it unsociable to dislike so many of the girls.﻿ ﻿… Ellen’s people were in the Indian﻿ ﻿… her thoughts hesitated.﻿ ﻿… Sivvle﻿ ﻿… something grand﻿—All the grand girls were horrid﻿ ﻿… somehow mean and sly﻿ ﻿… Sivvle﻿ ﻿… *Sivvle*﻿ ﻿… *Civil!* Of course! Civil *what*?

Miriam groaned. She was a governess now. Someone would ask her that question. She would ask Pater before he went.﻿ ﻿… No, she would not.﻿ ﻿… If only he would answer a question simply, and not with a superior air as if he had invented the thing he was telling about. She felt she had a right to all the knowledge there was, without fuss﻿ ﻿… oh, without fuss﻿—without fuss and﻿—emotion.﻿ ﻿… I *am* unsociable, I suppose﻿—she mused. She could not think of anyone who did not offend her. I don’t like men and I loathe women. I am a misanthrope. So’s Pater. He despises women and can’t get on with men. We are different﻿—it’s us, him and me. He’s failed us because he’s different and if he weren’t we should be like other people. Everything in the railway responded and agreed. Like other people﻿ ﻿… horrible.﻿ ﻿… She thought of the fathers of girls she knew﻿—the Poole girls, for instance, they were to be “independent” trained and certificated﻿—she envied that﻿—but her envy vanished when she remembered how heartily she had agreed when Sarah called them “sharp” and “knowing.”

Mr. Poole was a business man﻿ ﻿… common﻿ ﻿… trade.﻿ ﻿… If Pater had kept to Grandpa’s business they would be trade, too﻿—well-off, now﻿—all married. Perhaps as it was he had thought they would marry.

===Chinese===
她抬眼看了看行李架上她那只小皮手提包，想到钱包里那笔实实在在的钱。二十五先令。这是一大笔钱，而且需要时她还会得到更多。

她朝对面瞥了一眼：那张苍白的脸，一撮红褐色的胡须，瘦长白皙的手一只叠着一只搭在翘起的膝盖上。他给了她二十五先令，还有她的路费、他的路费、他的回程票，她的新衣箱，和她需要的所有东西。再拿他的钱，必须到此为止了。她已经长大了。她是有主见的那个。她必须自己撑起来。前头是站不住脚的处境，再过一小段便是灾祸，她也必须撑下去。

宁静的荷兰田野浮上她的心头。它们看上去那么安稳。它们过去得太快了。我们一直就处在站不住脚的境地，她思量着。总是撒谎、装假、撑场面——从不敢告诉任何人。……她想告诉任何人吗？走到光天化日之下，受人帮助，让人替她把事情安排好，像别人那样做事？不。……不。……"米丽亚姆总爱与众不同"——"社交于不善社交者无益。"可怕的话……姑娘们还一起拿这些话取笑。这些话到底是什么意思？

"社交于不善社交者无益"——写在埃伦·夏普那本生日纪念册里她生日那一页上。上楼的时候埃伦把册子递给她，还把这句话大声念给大家听，然后露出她的那种微笑……她并没有请她题名……不喜欢这么多姑娘，这就算不善社交吗。……埃伦家的人在印度做官……她的思绪迟疑了。……Sivvle……什么体面的词——所有高贵的姑娘都讨厌……总有些刻薄又鬼祟……Sivvle……*Sivvle*……是 *Civil*（民政）！当然！Civil *什么*来着？

米丽亚姆呻吟了一声。她现在是个家庭女教师了。会有人来问她这个问题的。趁爸爸还没走，她要问问他。……不，她不会问的。……要是他回答问题时能简单些就好了，别摆出一副高人一等的神气，仿佛他讲的东西全是他发明出来的。她觉得自己有权得到世上一切知识，不必小题大做……哦，不必小题大做——不必小题大做，也不必——动感情。……我*确实*不善社交吧，大概——她默默思忖。她想不出有哪一个人不触犯她。我不喜欢男人，我厌恶女人。我是个厌世的人。爸爸也是。他鄙视女人，又和男人处不来。我们是不同的——是我们，他和我。他辜负了我们，因为他与众不同；他要不是这样，我们就会和别人一样了。火车上的一切都在回应她、附和她。像别人一样……可怕。……她想起她认识的那些姑娘们的父亲——比如普尔家的姑娘们，她们要做"独立的"人，受训练、拿证书——她羡慕这个——可她一想起萨拉说她们"精明""世故"时自己多么由衷地附和，那羡慕便烟消云散了。

普尔先生是个生意人……平庸……做买卖的。……要是爸爸当初守着外公的生意，他们也会是做买卖的——如今日子殷实——姑娘全都嫁了。也许他正是抱着那样的指望，以为她们都会嫁出去的。

---

===Original===
She thought sleepily of her Wesleyan grandparents, gravely reading the *Wesleyan Methodist Recorder*, the shop at Babington, her father’s discontent, his solitary fishing and reading, his discovery of music﻿ ﻿… science﻿ ﻿… classical music in the first Novello editions﻿ ﻿… Faraday﻿ ﻿… speaking to Faraday after lectures. Marriage﻿ ﻿… the new house﻿ ﻿… the red brick wall at the end of the garden where young peach trees were planted﻿ ﻿… running up and downstairs and singing﻿ ﻿… both of them singing in the rooms and the garden﻿ ﻿… she sometimes with her hair down and then when visitors were expected pinned in coils under a little cap and wearing a small hoop﻿ ﻿… the garden and lawns and shrubbery and the long kitchen garden and the summerhouse under the oaks beyond and the pretty old gabled “town” on the river and the woods all along the river valley and the hills shining up out of the mist. The snow man they both made in the winter﻿—the birth of Sarah and then Eve﻿ ﻿… his studies and book-buying﻿—and after five years her own disappointing birth as the third girl, and the coming of Harriett just over a year later﻿ ﻿… her mother’s illness, money troubles﻿—their two years at the sea to retrieve﻿ ﻿… the disappearance of the sunlit red-walled garden always in full summer sunshine with the sound of bees in it or dark from windows﻿ ﻿… the narrowing of the house life down to the Marine Villa﻿—with the sea creeping in﻿—wading out through the green shallows, out and out till you were more than waist-deep﻿—shrimping and prawning hour after hour for weeks together﻿ ﻿… poking in the rock pools, watching the sun and the colours in the strange afternoons﻿ ﻿… then the sudden large house at Barnes with the “drive” winding to the door.﻿ ﻿… He used to come home from the City and the Constitutional Club and sometimes instead of reading *The Times* or the *Globe* or the *Proceedings of the British Association* or Herbert Spencer, play Pope Joan or Jacoby with them all, or Table Billiards and laugh and be “silly” and take his turn at being “bumped” by Timmy going the round of the long dining room table, tail in the air; he had taken Sarah and Eve to see *Don Giovanni* and *Winter’s Tale* and the new piece, *Lohengrin*. No one at the tennis club had seen that. He had good taste. No one else had been to Madame Schumann’s Farewell﻿ ﻿… sitting at the piano with her curtains of hair and her dreamy smile﻿ ﻿… and the Philharmonic Concerts. No one else knew about the lectures at the Royal Institution, beginning at nine on Fridays.﻿ ﻿… No one else’s father went with a party of scientific men “for the advancement of science” to Norway or America, seeing the Falls and the Yosemite Valley. No one else took his children as far as Dawlish for the holidays, travelling all day, from eight until seven﻿ ﻿… no esplanade, the old stone jetty and coves and cowrie shells.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
她睡意朦胧地想起她的卫斯理宗祖父母，一本正经地读着《卫斯理宗循道会记录报》，想起巴宾顿的店铺，父亲的不满，他独自一人的垂钓与阅读，他对音乐的发现……科学……诺韦洛初版乐谱里的古典音乐……法拉第……讲座过后与法拉第交谈。结婚……新房子……花园尽头那道红砖墙，墙边新栽了桃树……跑上跑下、唱着歌……他们两个在房间里、在花园里唱歌……她有时披散着头发，等到有客人要来，就把头发盘成发圈用小帽罩住，穿一条小裙撑……花园、草坪、灌木丛、长长的菜园，再过去是橡树下的凉亭，河畔那座漂亮的老式人字顶"宅子"，沿河谷绵延的树林，还有从雾气里亮闪闪升起来的山峦。冬天他们俩一起堆的雪人——萨拉出生，然后是伊芙……他的研读和买书——五年后，她自己令人失望地出世，是第三个女孩，一年多之后哈丽特又来了……母亲的病，钱的困窘——为了挽回，他们去海边住了两年……那座阳光下的红墙花园消失了，它总是浸在盛夏的阳光里，满是蜜蜂的嗡嗡声，或从窗口望去一片幽暗……家居的日子收缩进海滨别墅——海水悄悄漫进来——蹚过绿色的浅滩往外走，越走越远，直到水深过了腰——一连几个星期，一小时又一小时地捉虾捕虾……在岩石水潭里翻翻戳戳，在那些奇异的下午里看太阳和种种色彩……然后是巴恩斯那座突然出现的大房子，"车道"蜿蜒通到门前。……他常从伦敦城里、从宪政俱乐部回家，有时不读《泰晤士报》、不读《环球报》、不读《英国科学促进会会刊》、也不读赫伯特·斯宾塞，而是和他们大家一起玩"教皇琼"纸牌或是雅各比，或是桌上弹子戏，大笑，装"傻"，轮流让蒂米——翘着尾巴绕着长长的餐桌跑一圈——来"撞"一下；他带萨拉和伊芙去看过《唐·乔万尼》和《冬天的故事》，还有新戏《罗恩格林》。网球俱乐部里没有人看过这些。他品味很好。没有别人去过舒曼夫人的告别音乐会……她坐在钢琴前，垂着帘幕般的头发，带着梦幻般的微笑……也没有别人去听爱乐音乐会。没有别人知道皇家研究院的讲座，星期五九点开始。……没有别人的父亲随一队科学人士"为促进科学"远赴挪威或美国，去看大瀑布和约塞米蒂谷。没有别人的父亲带孩子远到道利什去度假，整整走一天，从八点到七点……没有海滨大道，只有古老的石堤、小海湾和宝贝螺壳。……

## III / III

===Original===
Miriam was practising on the piano in the larger of the two English bedrooms. Two other pianos were sounding in the house, one across the landing and the other in the *Saal* where Herr Kapellmeister Bossenberger was giving a music lesson. The rest of the girls were gathered in the large schoolroom under the care of Mademoiselle for Saturday’s *raccommodage*. It was the last hour of the week’s work. Presently there would be a great gonging, the pianos would cease, Fräulein’s voice would sound up through the house “Anziehen zum Aus‑geh‑hen!”

There would be the walk, dinner, the Saturday afternoon home-letters to be written and then, until Monday, holiday, freedom to read and to talk English and idle. And there was a new arrival in the house. Ulrica Hesse had come. Miriam had seen her. There had been three large leather trunks in the hall and a girl with a smooth pure oval of pale face standing wrapped in dark furs, gazing about her with eyes for which Miriam had no word, liquid—limpid—great-saucers, no—pools … great round deeps. … She had felt about for something to express them as she went upstairs with her roll of music. Fräulein Pfaff who had seemed to hover and smile about the girl as if half afraid to speak to her, had put out a hand for Miriam and said almost deprecatingly, “Ach, mm, dies’ ist unser Ulrica.”

The girl’s thin fingers had come out of her furs and fastened convulsively—like cold, throbbing claws on to the breadth of Miriam’s hand.

“Unsere englische Lehrerin—our teacher from England,” smiled Fräulein.

“Lehrerin!” breathed the girl. Something flinched behind her great eyes. The fingers relaxed, and Miriam feeling within her a beginning of response, had gone upstairs.

As she reached the upper landing she began to distinguish against the clangour of chromatic passages assailing the house from the echoing *Saal*, the gentle tones of the nearer piano, the one in the larger German bedroom opposite the front room for which she was bound. She paused for a moment at the top of the stairs and listened. A little swaying melody came out to her, muted by the closed door. Her grasp on the roll of music slackened. A radiance came for a moment behind the gravity of her face. Then the careful unstumbling repetition of a difficult passage drew her attention to the performer, her arms dropped to her sides and she passed on. It was little Bergmann, the youngest girl in the school. Her playing, on the bad old piano in the dark dressing room in the basement, had prepared Miriam for the difference between the performance of these German girls and nearly all the piano-playing she had heard. It was the morning after her arrival. She had been unpacking and had taken, on the advice of Mademoiselle, her heavy boots and outdoor things down to the basement room. She had opened the door on Emma sitting at the piano in her blue and buff check ribbon-knotted stuff dress. Miriam had expected her to turn her head and stop playing. But as, arms full, she closed the door with her shoulders, the child’s profile remained unconcerned. She noticed the firmly-poised head, the thick creamy neck that seemed bare with its absence of collar-band and the soft frill of tucker stitched right on to the dress, the thick cable of string-coloured hair reaching just beyond the rim of the leather-covered music stool, the steel-beaded points of the little slippers gleaming as they worked the pedals, the serene eyes steadily following the music. She played on and Miriam recognised a quality she had only heard occasionally at concerts, and in the playing of one of the music teachers at school.

She had stood amazed, pretending to be fumbling for empty pegs as this round-faced child of fourteen went her way to the end of her page. Then Miriam had ventured to interrupt and to ask her about the hanging arrangements, and the child had risen and speaking soft South German had suggested and poked tiptoeing about amongst the thickly-hung garments and shown a motherly solicitude over the disposal of Miriam’s things. Miriam noted the easy range of the child’s voice, how smoothly it slid from birdlike queries and chirpings to the consoling tones of the lower register. It seemed to leave undisturbed the softly-rounded, faintly-mottled chin and cheeks and the full unpouting lips that lay quietly one upon the other before she spoke, and opened flexibly but somehow hardly moved to her speech and afterwards closed again gradually until they lay softly blossoming as before.

Emma had gathered up her music when the clothes were arranged, sighing and lamenting gently, “Wäre ich nur zu Hause”—how happy one was at home—her little voice filled with tears and her cheeks flushed, “haypie, haypie to home,” she complained as she slid her music into its case, “where all so good, so nice, so beautiful,” and they had gone, side by side, up the dark uncarpeted stone stairs leading from the basement to the hall. Halfway up, Emma had given Miriam a shy firm hug and then gone decorously up the remainder of the flight.

The sense of that sudden little embrace recurred often to Miriam during the course of the first day.

It was unlike any contact she had known—more motherly than her mother’s. Neither of her sisters could have embraced her like that. She did not know that a human form could bring such a sense of warm nearness, that human contours could be eloquent—or anyone so sweetly daring.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆正在两间英国卧室里较大的那一间中练琴。屋子里还有两架钢琴在响，一架在平台对面，另一架在*大厅*（*Saal*）里，卡佩尔迈斯特·波森贝格先生（Herr Kapellmeister Bossenberger）正在那里上音乐课。其余的姑娘们聚在大教室里，由小姐（Mademoiselle）照看着，做星期六的*缝补*（*raccommodage*）。这是本周功课的最后一个钟头。一会儿就会响起好大一阵锣声，钢琴都会停下来，弗劳莱恩小姐的声音会一路传上楼来：“Anziehen zum Aus-geh-hen！（穿好衣裳准备出门！）”

接下来会是散步、正餐，写星期六下午的家信，然后一直到星期一，都是假日，可以自由自在地读书、说英语、闲待着。而且屋里还来了一个新到的人。乌尔丽卡·黑塞（Ulrica Hesse）到了。米丽亚姆见过她了。门厅里立着三只大皮箱，一个女孩裹在深色的毛皮里站着，苍白的一张鹅蛋脸光洁无瑕，一双眼睛四下打量——那双眼睛，米丽亚姆找不出词来形容，水汪汪的——清澈的——像大茶碟，不——是水潭……又大又圆的深潭。……她抱着乐谱卷上楼时，一路在心里摸索着形容它们的词。普法夫小姐在那女孩身边转来转去、满面堆笑，仿佛有点不敢同她说话似的，这时朝米丽亚姆伸出一只手，几乎带着歉意地说：“Ach，嗯，dies’ ist unser Ulrica（这就是我们的乌尔丽卡）。”

女孩细细的手指从毛皮里伸出来，痉挛般地——像冰冷而突突跳动的爪子——紧紧扣住了米丽亚姆宽宽的手掌。

“Unsere englische Lehrerin——我们从英国来的老师。”弗劳莱恩小姐微笑着说。

“Lehrerin（女教师）！”女孩轻轻吁出一口气。她那双大眼睛后面有什么东西瑟缩了一下。手指松开了，米丽亚姆感到心里有一丝回应正在萌动，便上楼去了。

走到上面一层平台时，在余音回荡的*大厅*里传来的半音阶乐句的铿锵声中，她开始分辨出较近那架琴轻柔的琴声——那架琴在德国姑娘们的大卧室里，就在她要去的临街房间对面。她在楼梯顶上停了片刻，听了听。一支小小的、摇曳的旋律传到她耳边，被关着的门闷住了。她握着乐谱卷的手松了松。她那张庄重的脸后面，有一瞬亮起了光彩。随后，一段艰难乐句被小心地、不错一个音地反复弹奏，把她的注意力引向了弹琴的人，她双臂垂落身侧，继续往前走。那是小贝格曼，学校里年纪最小的姑娘。她先前在地下室那间昏暗的穿衣室里、在那架破旧钢琴上的弹奏，已经让米丽亚姆对这儿德国姑娘的演奏同她平生听过的一切钢琴演奏之间的差别有了准备。那是她到后的第一个早晨。她当时正开箱整理，听从小姐的建议，把笨重的靴子和出门的衣物送到地下室去。她推开门，正看见爱玛坐在钢琴前，穿着蓝黄格子的呢布裙，系着打成蝴蝶结的缎带。米丽亚姆本以为她会回过头来、停下琴。可是米丽亚姆抱满了东西、用肩膀顶上房门的时候，孩子的侧影依然不为所动。她注意到那稳稳端着的头，那仿佛光着似的丰润脖颈——因为没有领座，裙衣上只直接缝了一圈软褶边——那缆绳般粗的麻灰色发辫，才刚过蒙皮琴凳的凳沿，小小的便鞋上钢珠缀出的鞋尖随着踩踏板的动作一闪一闪，还有那双安详的眼睛，静静地跟着谱子走。她只管往下弹，米丽亚姆听出了一种她只在音乐会上偶尔听到过、还有学校里一位音乐老师的弹奏中听到过的品质。

她惊讶地站在那里，装作在摸索空衣钩，任这个十四岁的圆脸姑娘自管自弹完她那一页。后来米丽亚姆才壮起胆子打断她，向她请教挂衣裳的安排，孩子便站起来，说着柔软的南德语，一路出主意、踮着脚在挂得厚厚的衣物间拨弄穿行，对米丽亚姆东西的归置流露出一种母亲般的操心。米丽亚姆留意到这孩子嗓音音域的宽绰，它多么圆润地从鸟儿似的问话和啁啾，滑到低声区的慰藉语调。这一切似乎丝毫惊动不了那柔和圆胖、微微斑驳的下巴和脸颊，也惊动不了那两片饱满而不嘟起的嘴唇——她开口之前，双唇安安静静地一瓣叠着一瓣；说话时灵巧地张开，却几乎不怎么动；说完又渐渐合拢，直到重新像先前那样软软地含苞静卧。

衣裳挂好后，爱玛收起她的乐谱，轻轻地叹息哀伤着：“Wäre ich nur zu Hause（要是在家里就好了）”——在家里多么快活——她那小小的嗓音里噙满了泪水，脸颊通红，“haypie，haypie to home（快活，快活在家里——她把happy念成了haypie），”她一边把乐谱插进套子一边抱怨着，“那里的一切都那么好，那么美，那么漂亮。”随后她们并排走上了从地下室通往门厅的、没铺地毯的黑黢黢的石楼梯。走到半途，爱玛羞怯而紧紧地抱了米丽亚姆一下，然后端端正正地走完了剩下的一段。

头一天里，那个突如其来的小小拥抱的感觉，一再回到米丽亚姆心头。

那与她所知道的任何接触都不一样——比她母亲的拥抱更多了几分母性。她的两个姐妹谁也不可能那样抱她。她从不知道一个人的身体能带来这样一种温暖贴近的感觉，从不知道人的轮廓也能这样倾诉衷肠——也从不知道有人能这样甜美地大胆。

---

===Original===
That first evening at Waldstraße there had been a performance that had completed the transformation of Miriam’s English ideas of “music.” She had caught the word *Vorspielen* being bandied about the long tea table, and had gathered that there was to be an informal playing of “pieces” before Fräulein Pfaff. She welcomed the event. It relieved her from the burden of being in high focus—the relief had come as soon as she took her place at the gaslit table. No eye seemed to notice her. The English girls having sat out two mealtimes with her, had ceased the hard-eyed observation which had made the long silence of the earlier repasts only less embarrassing than Fräulein’s questions about England. The four Germans who had neither stared nor even appeared aware of her existence, talked cheerfully across the table in a general exchange that included tall Fräulein Pfaff smiling her horse smile—Miriam provisionally called it—behind the tea urn, as chairman. The six English-speaking girls, grouped as it were towards their chief, a dark-skinned, athletic looking Australian with hot, brown, slightly bloodshot eyes sitting as vice president opposite Fräulein, joined occasionally, in solo and chorus, and Miriam noted with relief a unanimous atrocity of accent in their enviable fluency. Rapid sotto voce commentary and half-suppressed wordless byplay located still more clearly the English quarter. Animation flowed and flowed. Miriam safely ignored, scarcely heeding, but warmed and almost happy, basked. She munched her black bread and butter, liberally smeared with the rich savoury paste of liver sausage, and drank her sweet weak tea and knew that she was very tired, sleepy and tired. She glanced, from her place next to Emma Bergmann and on Fräulein’s left hand, down the table to where Mademoiselle sat next the Martins in similar relation to the vice-president. Mademoiselle, preceding her up through the quiet house carrying the jugs of hot water, had been her first impression on her arrival the previous night. She had turned when they reached the candle-lit attic with its high uncurtained windows and red-covered box beds, and standing on the one strip of matting in her full-skirted grey wincey dress with its neat triple row of black ribbon velvet near the hem, had shown Miriam steel-blue eyes smiling from a little triangular spritelike face under a high-standing pouf of soft dark hair, and said, “Voilà!” Miriam had never imagined anything in the least like her. She had said, “Oh, thank you,” and taken the jug and had hurriedly and silently got to bed, weighed down by wonders. They had begun to talk in the dark. Miriam had reaped sweet comfort in learning that this seemingly unreal creature who was, she soon perceived, not educated—as she understood education—was the resident French governess, was seventeen years old and a Protestant. Such close quarters with a French girl was bewildering enough—had she been a Roman Catholic, Miriam felt she could not have endured her proximity. She was evidently a special kind of French girl—a Protestant from East France—Besançon—Besançon—Miriam had tried the pretty word over until unexpectedly she had fallen asleep.

They had risen hurriedly in the cold March gloom and Miriam had not spoken to her since. There she sat, dainty and quiet and fresh. White frillings shone now at the neck and sleeves of her little grey dress. She looked a clean and clear miniature against the general dauby effect of the English girls—poor though, Miriam was sure; perhaps as poor as she. She felt glad as she watched her gentle spritelike wistfulness that she would be upstairs in that great bare attic again tonight. In repose her face looked pinched. There was something about the nose and mouth—Miriam mused … *frugal*—John Gilpin’s wife—how sleepy she was.

===Chinese===
在瓦尔德街的头一个晚上，有一场演奏，把米丽亚姆英国式的“音乐”观念彻底改造完成了。她听见*试奏会*（*Vorspielen*）这个词在长长的茶桌上传来传去，明白了将有一场不拘形式的“曲子”演奏，就在普法夫小姐面前。她欢迎这件事。它把她从众目睽睽的重负之下解脱出来——这解脱，从她在煤气灯照亮的长桌旁落座的那一刻起就来了。似乎没有一只眼睛注意到她。英国姑娘们同她一起坐过了两顿饭的工夫，已经不再用生硬的眼光打量她——正是那种打量，使最初几餐漫长的沉默，只比弗劳莱恩关于英国的种种提问少些难堪。四位德国姑娘既不盯着她看、甚至显不出察觉她的存在，隔着桌子兴高采烈地交谈，普谈之中还有高大的普法夫小姐——她在茶炊后面露出马一般的笑，米丽亚姆暂且这样称呼它——像个主席。六位说英语的姑娘，仿佛簇拥着她们的首领坐成一伙——首领是个深色皮肤、运动员模样的澳大利亚姑娘，一双热辣辣的棕色眼睛微微带血丝，作为副主席坐在弗劳莱恩对面——偶尔以独唱和合唱的方式加入进来，米丽亚姆欣慰地注意到，她们那令人艳羡的流利里带着清一色的可怕口音。急促的耳语和半压着的、不出声的小动作，把英国人那一角标示得更加清楚。谈兴流了又流。米丽亚姆安然地被忽略着，几乎不去留意，只觉得温暖、几乎快活，晒着太阳似的。她大嚼她的黑面包抹黄油——厚厚涂满了肝肠浓香的酱——喝她那甜甜的淡茶，知道自己累极了，又困又乏。她从自己坐的地方——挨着爱玛·贝格曼、在弗劳莱恩左手边——朝桌子另一头望去，小姐正坐在马丁家姐妹旁边，与副主席处在同样的位置。小姐是她前夜抵达时的第一个印象——提着大壶热水，领她走上这幢安静的屋子。她们走到那间点着蜡烛的阁楼，高高的窗上没有窗帘，摆着红罩子的箱柜床，小姐转过身来，站在仅有的一条草席上，她那件灰色温茜呢宽裙的下摆附近齐齐缝着三道黑丝绒缎带，向米丽亚姆露出一张小小的三角形精灵脸，一双钢蓝色的笑眼在高高蓬起的柔软深色头发底下。“Voilà（到啦）！”米丽亚姆从未想象过世上有半点像她这样的人。她说过一声“哦，谢谢”，接过水壶，便默默匆忙地上了床，被种种奇观压得沉沉的。黑暗中她们说起话来。米丽亚姆甜蜜地得了安慰，得知这个看似不真实的小人儿——她很快看出来，她没有受过教育，按她对教育的理解——就是住校的法国女教师，十七岁，新教徒。同一个法国姑娘住得这样近，本来就够让人发懵——倘若她是罗马天主教徒，米丽亚姆觉得自己准受不了跟她同处一室。她显然是法国姑娘里特殊的一种——东法国——贝桑松（Besançon）来的新教徒——贝桑松——贝桑松——米丽亚姆把这个好听的词翻来覆去地念，念着念着，出其不意地就睡着了。

她们在三月份寒冷的昏暗中匆匆起身，此后米丽亚姆还没同她说过话。她此刻坐在那里，秀气、安静、鲜亮。她那件小小的灰裙子的领口和袖口如今闪着白色的褶边。在英国姑娘们普遍涂抹般的效果衬托下，她像一幅干净明澈的袖珍画——不过穷，米丽亚姆敢肯定；也许同她自己一样穷。望着她那温柔精灵般的怅惘，米丽亚姆庆幸今晚又要同她一起上那间空荡荡的大阁楼去。安静下来时，她的脸显得清瘦。鼻子和嘴那儿有点什么——米丽亚姆寻思……节俭——约翰·吉尔平的妻子——她多困啊。

---

===Original===
The conversation was growing boisterous. She took courage to raise her head towards the range of girls opposite to her. Those quite near to her she could not scrutinise. Some influence coming to her from these German girls prevented her risking with them any meeting of the eyes that was not brought about by direct speech. But she felt them. She felt Emma Bergmann’s warm plump presence close at her side and liked to take food handed by her. She was conscious of the pink bulb of Minna Blum’s nose shining just opposite to her, and of the way the light caught the blond sheen of her exquisitely coiled hair as she turned her always smiling face and responded to the louder remarks with, “Oh, thou *dear* God!” or “Is it possible!” “How charming, *charming*,” or “What in life dost thou say, rascal!”

Next to her was the faint glare of Elsa Speier’s silent sallowness. Her clear-threaded nimbus of pallid hair was the lowest point in the range of figures across the table. She darted quick glances at one and another without moving her head, and Miriam felt that her pale eyes fully met would be cunning and malicious.

After Elsa the “English” began with Judy. Miriam guessed when she heard her ask for *Brötchen* that she was Scotch. She sat slightly askew and ate eagerly, stooping over her plate with smiling mouth and downcast heavily-freckled face. Unless spoken to she did not speak, but she laughed often, a harsh involuntary laugh immediately followed by a drowning flush. When she was not flushed her eyelashes shone bright black against the unstained white above her cheekbones. She had coarse fuzzy red-brown hair.

Miriam decided that she was negligible.

Next to Judy were the Martins. They were as English as they could be. She felt she must have noticed them a good deal at breakfast and dinnertime without knowing it. Her eyes after one glance at the claret-coloured merino dresses with hard white collars and cuffs, came back to her plate as from a familiar picture. She still saw them sitting very upright, side by side, with the front strands of their hair strained smoothly back, tied just on the crest of the head with brown ribbon and going down in “rats’-tails” to join the rest of their hair which hung straight and flat halfway down their backs. The elder was dark with thick shoulders and heavy features. Her large expressionless rich brown eyes flashed slowly and reflected the light. They gave Miriam a slight feeling of nausea. She felt she knew what her hands were like without looking at them. The younger was thin and pale and slightly hollow-cheeked. She had pale eyes, cold, like a fish, thought Miriam. They both had deep hollow voices.

When she glanced again they were watching the Australian with their four strange eyes and laughing German phrases at her, “Go on, Gertrude!” “Are you *sure*, Gertrude?” “How do you *know*, Gertrude!”

Miriam had not yet dared to glance in the direction of the Australian. Her eyes at dinnertime had cut like sharp steel. Turning, however, towards the danger zone, without risking the coming of its presiding genius within the focus of her glasses she caught a glimpse of “Jimmie” sitting back in her chair tall and plump and neat, and shaking with wide-mouthed giggles. Miriam wondered at the high neat peak of hair on the top of her head and stared at her pearly little teeth. There was something funny about her mouth. Even when she strained it wide it was narrow and tiny—rabbity. She raised a short arm and began patting her peak of hair with a tiny hand which showed a small onyx seal ring on the little finger. “Ask Judy!” she giggled, in a fruity squeak.

“Ask Judy!” they all chorused, laughing.

Judy cast an appealing flash of her eyes sideways at nothing, flushed furiously and mumbled, “Ik weiss nik—I don’t know.”

In the outcries and laughter which followed, Miriam noticed only the hoarse hacking laugh of the Australian. Her eyes flew up the table and fixed her as she sat laughing, her chair drawn back, her knees crossed—tea was drawing to an end. The detail of her terrifyingly stylish ruddy-brown frieze dress with its Norfolk jacket bodice and its shiny black leather belt was hardly distinguishable from the dark background made by the folding doors. But the dreadful outline of her shoulders was visible, the squarish oval of her face shone out—the wide forehead from which the wiry black hair was combed to a high puff, the red eyes, black now, the long straight nose, the wide laughing mouth with the enormous teeth.

Her voice conquered easily.

“Nein,” she tromboned, through the din.

Mademoiselle’s little finger stuck up sharply like a steeple, her mouth said, “Oh—Oh—”

Fräulein’s smile was at its widest, waiting the issue.

“Nein,” triumphed the Australian, causing a lull.

“Leise, Kinder, leise, doucement, gentlay,” chided Fräulein, still smiling.

“Hermann, *yes*,” proceeded the Australian, “aber Hugo—*né*!”

Miriam heard it agreed in the end that someone named Hugo did not wear a moustache, though someone named Hermann did. She was vaguely shocked and interested.

===Chinese===
谈话渐渐热闹起来。她鼓起勇气，朝对面那排姑娘抬眼望去。离她很近的那几个，她细看不得。从这些德国姑娘身上传来的一种什么影响，使她不敢冒险同她们发生任何不由直接说话引出的对视。可她感觉得到她们。她感觉得到爱玛·贝格曼温软丰盈的存在就在自己身边，喜欢从她手里接过递来的食物。她意识到明娜·布卢姆（Minna Blum）那粉红的蒜头鼻正在对面发亮，意识到每当她转动那张总在微笑的脸，光怎样点亮她那盘得精巧的头发上的金色光泽，而她又怎样用“哦，亲爱的*上*帝！”“可能吗！”“多迷人啊，*迷人*，”或是“你到底在说什么呀，小坏蛋！”来应答那些更响亮的话。

再过去，是埃尔莎·施派尔（Elsa Speier）沉默的焦黄带来的一团淡淡的亮。她那一圈发丝历历的苍白光晕，是桌子对面一排身影中的最低点。她头也不动，飞快地朝这个那个瞥视，米丽亚姆觉得，她那双浅色的眼睛若被正眼相对，准是又狡又毒的。

埃尔莎之后，从朱迪（Judy）起就是“英国人”了。米丽亚姆听她要*小圆面包*（*Brötchen*）时，就猜到她是苏格兰人。她微微歪着坐，急切地吃着，俯在盘子上，嘴上带笑，密密雀斑的脸低垂着。别人不跟她说话她就不说话，可她常常笑，一种刺耳的、不由自主的笑，紧接着是一阵淹没一切的涨红。不涨红的时候，她的睫毛在颧骨上方未染尘污的洁白上闪着亮黑。她长着粗糙蓬乱的棕红色头发。

米丽亚姆断定，她可以忽略不计。

朱迪旁边是马丁家的姐妹。她们英国得不能再英国了。米丽亚姆觉得，自己在早餐和正餐时一定不知不觉地留意过她们不少。她的眼睛朝那两身酒红色美利奴呢裙——配着硬挺的白领白袖——瞥过一眼，便像从一幅熟悉的画上那样收回到自己盘子里。她仍旧看得见她们笔直并排坐着的样子，前额的头发一绺绺紧绷绷地平掠向后，用棕色缎带恰好在头顶正中扎住，垂下几条“老鼠尾巴”汇入其余的头发，笔直平贴地垂到背心一半的地方。姐姐深色皮肤，肩膀厚实，五官凝重。她那双大而无神、色泽浓郁的棕眼睛慢慢地闪着，映着灯光。它们让米丽亚姆微微有点作呕。她觉得不用看也知道她们那双手是什么样子。妹妹瘦削苍白，两颊微微凹陷。她的眼睛颜色浅，冷冷的，像鱼，米丽亚姆想。她们俩都有深深的、中空嗓音。

等她再看过去，她们正用四只古怪的眼睛盯着那位澳大利亚姑娘，笑着朝她抛出一串德国腔：“接着说呀，格特鲁德（Gertrude）！”“你*敢*肯定吗，格特鲁德？”“你怎么*知道*的，格特鲁德！”

米丽亚姆还不敢朝澳大利亚姑娘的方向望一眼。晚餐时她的目光曾像锋利的钢。不过，朝那危险地带转过身去、却又不让那位主宰的精灵进入自己镜片的焦点，她瞥见了“吉米”（Jimmie）——高高的、丰润的、齐齐整整靠在椅背上，咧着大嘴咯咯笑得直颤。米丽亚姆惊叹她头顶上那绺端端正正的高发峰，盯着看她的珍珠般的小牙。她的嘴有点好笑。哪怕使劲咧到最宽，它也还是又窄又小——兔子似的。她抬起一条短短的胳膊，用一只小手拍打她的发峰，小指上露出一枚小小的缟玛瑙图章戒指。“问朱迪去！”她咯咯笑着，用一种果子似的又尖又脆的声音说。

“问朱迪去！”她们全体齐声喊，笑着。

朱迪把恳求的眼光朝旁边什么也没有的地方一闪，涨红了脸，咕哝道：“Ik weiss nik（我不道）——我不知道。”

随后的一片叫嚷大笑里，米丽亚姆只注意到那澳大利亚姑娘嘶哑断续的笑声。她的目光顺着桌子飞过去，钉在她身上——她正仰着笑，椅子往后撤着，两腿交叠——茶快要到尾声了。她那身吓人地时髦的红棕色粗呢裙——诺福克夹克式的上衣，亮闪闪的黑皮带——在折门构成的暗背景前，细节几乎分辨不出。但她那副可怕的肩膀的轮廓还看得见，那张略方的鹅蛋脸亮了出来——宽阔的前额，铁硬的黑发从那儿高高梳起、蓬作一团；红红的眼睛，此刻发黑了；又长又直的鼻子；咧开笑着的宽嘴和那一口大牙。

她的嗓音轻易就压倒了一切。

“Nein（不），”她穿过喧闹，长号似的哼了一声。

小姐的小手指尖尖地翘起，像一座小尖塔，她的嘴说着：“哦——哦——”

弗劳莱恩的笑扩到了最宽处，等着结果。

“Nein，”那澳大利亚姑娘得胜了，引得一阵安静。

“Leise，Kinder，leise（轻些，孩子们，轻些），doucement，gentlay（法语‘轻点儿’混着英语‘斯文点儿’），”弗劳莱恩责备道，仍旧笑着。

“Hermann，*是的*，”澳大利亚姑娘接着说，“aber Hugo（可是雨果）——*née*（不——德语口语的‘不’）！”

米丽亚姆听到最后大家一致同意：某个名叫雨果的人不蓄上唇髭，而某个名叫赫尔曼的蓄。她隐隐觉得又震惊又有趣。

---

===Original===
After tea the great doors were thrown open and the girls filed into the *Saal*. It was a large high room furnished like a drawing-room—enough settees and easy chairs to accommodate more than all the girls. The polished floor was uncarpeted save for an archipelago of mats and rugs in the wide circle of light thrown by the four-armed chandelier. A grand piano was pushed against the wall in the far corner of the room, between the farthest of the three high French windows and the shining pillar of porcelain stove.

===Chinese===
茶后，两扇大门敞开，姑娘们鱼贯走进*大厅*。那是一间高大的房间，布置得像一间客厅——长椅和安乐椅多得住得下所有姑娘还绰绰有余。打过蜡的地板上没铺地毯，只有四臂枝形吊灯投下的一大圈光里，群岛似的散着一方方小垫和地毯。一架大钢琴推靠在房间远端的墙边，在三扇高大的法式窗中最远的那扇和瓷炉闪亮的立柱之间。

---

===Original===
The high room, the bright light, the plentiful mirrors, the long sweep of lace curtains, the many faces—the girls seemed so much more numerous scattered here than they had when collected in the schoolroom—brought Miriam the sense of the misery of social occasions. She wondered whether the girls were nervous. She was glad that music lessons were no part of her remuneration. She thought of dreadful experiences of playing before people. The very first time, at home, when she had played a duet with Eve—Eve playing a little running melody in the treble—her own part a page of minims. The minims had swollen until she could not see whether they were lines or spaces, and her fingers had been so weak after the first unexpectedly loud note that she could hardly make any sound. Eve had said “louder” and her fingers had suddenly stiffened and she had worked them from her elbows like sticks at the end of her trembling wrists and hands. Eve had noticed her dreadful movements and resented being elbowed. She had heard nothing then but her hard loud minims till the end, and then as she stood dizzily up someone had said she had a nice firm touch, and she had pushed her angry way from the piano across the hearthrug. She should always remember the clear red-hot mass of the fire and the bottle of green Chartreuse warming on the blue and cream tiles. There were probably only two or three guests, but the room had seemed full of people, stupid people who had made her play. How angry she had been with Eve for noticing her discomfiture and with the forgotten guest for her silly remark. She knew she had simply poked the piano. Then there had been the annual school concert, all the girls almost unrecognisable with fear. She had learnt her pieces by heart for those occasions and played them through with trembling limbs and burning eyes—alternately thumping with stiff fingers and feeling her whole hand faint from the wrist on to the notes which fumbled and slurred into each other almost soundlessly until the thumping began again. At the musical evenings, organised by Eve as a winter set-off to the tennis club, she had both played and sung, hoping each time afresh to be able to reproduce the effects which came so easily when she was alone or only with Eve. But she could not discover the secret of getting rid of her nervousness. Only twice had she succeeded—at the last school concert when she had been too miserable to be nervous and Mr. Strood had told her she did him credit and, once she had sung “Chanson de Florian” in a way that had astonished her own listening ear—the notes had laughed and thrilled out into the air and come back to her from the wall behind the piano. … The day before the tennis tournament.

===Chinese===
这高大的房间、明亮的灯光、众多的镜子、蕾丝窗帘长长的垂幅、许多张脸——姑娘们这样散在各处，似乎比聚在教室里时多出了好些——给米丽亚姆带来了社交场合的那种愁苦。她不知道姑娘们紧不紧张。她庆幸音乐课不算在她的酬劳之内。她想起在人前演奏过的可怕经历。最最开始的那一次，在家里，她和伊芙弹二重奏——伊芙在高音部弹一支轻快流动的旋律——她自己的声部是一整页的二分音符。那些二分音符胀得越来越大，直到她看不清是线还是间；第一声出其不意的响音过后，她的手指软得几乎发不出任何声音。伊芙说“响一点”，她的手指猛然僵住，她只能从肘部把它们当作棍子来使唤，戳在颤抖的手腕和手掌的尽头。伊芙注意到了她那可怕的姿势，恼恨被她的胳膊肘顶到。她后来什么也没听见，只听见自己硬邦邦、响亮的二分音符，直到曲终；她晕乎乎站起来的时候，有人说她的触键又稳又好听，她便怒气冲冲地从钢琴前挤出来，穿过炉边地毯走开了。她会永远记得那团通红透亮的炉火，记得在蓝白花砖上温着的绿色查特酒（Chartreuse）。客人大概只有两三位，可那房间仿佛满满的全是人，尽是些蠢人，逼她弹琴的人。她多气伊芙觉察了她的狼狈，多气那位忘了姓名的客人说的那句蠢话。她知道自己简直就是戳钢琴。再有就是一年一度的校际音乐会，所有的姑娘怕得几乎变了模样。那些场合她都把曲子背得烂熟，四肢发抖、两眼烧灼地从头弹到尾——一会儿用僵硬的手指重捶，一会儿觉得整只手从手腕起在琴键上发晕，音符跌跌撞撞、黏作一团，几乎无声，直到捶击重新开始。在伊芙组织的那些音乐晚会上——给网球俱乐部添的冬季节目——她又弹又唱，每一次都重新指望能再现她独自一人、或只同伊芙在一起时来得那么容易的效果。可她找不到祛除紧张的秘诀。只有两次她成功了——最后一次校际音乐会上，她已悲惨得顾不上紧张，斯特鲁德先生（Mr. Strood）告诉她，她没有辜负他的教导；还有一次，她唱《弗洛里安之歌》（“Chanson de Florian”），唱得连她自己那聆听着耳朵都惊讶——音符笑着、颤着飞进空气里，又从钢琴后面的墙上回到她耳边。……网球锦标赛的前一天。

---

===Original===
The girls were all settling down to fancy work, the white-cuffed hands of the Martins were already jerking crochet needles, faces were bending over fine embroideries and Minna Blum had trundled a mounted lace pillow into the brighter light.

Miriam went to the schoolroom and fetched from her workbasket the piece of canvas partly covered with red and black wool in diamond pattern that was her utmost experience of fancy work.

As she returned she half saw Fräulein Pfaff, sitting as if enthroned on a high-backed chair in front of the centremost of the mirrors filling the wall spaces between the long French windows, signal to her, to come to that side of the room.

Timorously ignoring the signal she got herself into a little low chair in the shadow of the half-closed swing door and was spreading out her woolwork on her knee when the *Vorspielen* began.

Emma Bergmann was playing. The single notes of the opening motif of Chopin’s Fifteenth Nocturne fell pensively into the waiting room. Miriam, her fatigue forgotten, slid to a featureless freedom. It seemed to her that the light with which the room was filled grew brighter and clearer. She felt that she was looking at nothing and yet was aware of the whole room like a picture in a dream. Fear left her. The human forms all round her lost their power. They grew suffused and dim. … The pensive swing of the music changed to urgency and emphasis. … It came nearer and nearer. It did not come from the candle-lit corner where the piano was. … It came from everywhere. It carried her out of the house, out of the world.

It hastened with her, on and on towards great brightness. … Everything was growing brighter and brighter. …

Gertrude Goldring, the Australian, was making noises with her hands like inflated paper bags being popped. Miriam clutched her wool needle and threaded it. She drew the wool through her canvas, one, three, five, three, one and longed for the piano to begin again.

===Chinese===
姑娘们纷纷坐定做针线活，马丁姐妹戴着白袖口的手已经在急急地抽动钩针，一张张脸俯向精细的绣活，明娜·布卢姆把一只装在架子上的花边枕滚到更亮的光线下。

米丽亚姆到教室去，从针线篮里取出那块画布——上面用红黑两色毛线绣了一部分菱形花纹——这便是她女红经历的全部了。

回来的时候，她隐约看见普法夫小姐高坐在一把高背椅上、仿佛登着宝座，正对着三扇长法式窗之间填满墙面的大镜子中正中央的那面，向她示意，叫她到房间的这一边来。

她怯生生地装作没看见那示意，把自己安置在半掩的弹簧门阴影里的一把小矮椅上，正要把毛线活摊到膝上，*试奏会*开始了。

爱玛·贝格曼在弹。肖邦第十五号夜曲开头主题的一个个单音，沉思地落进静静等候的房间里。米丽亚姆忘了疲劳，滑入一种无形无相的自由。她只觉得，充满房间的光变得更亮、更清澈了。她觉得自己什么也没看，却像梦里的一幅画那样意识到整个房间。恐惧离开了她。她四周的人形都失去了威力。他们变得蒙蒙眬眬。……音乐沉思的摆荡转为急切和强调。……它越来越近。它不是从点着蜡烛的、钢琴所在的那个角落传来。……它从四面八方传来。它把她带出了这幢房子，带出了这个世界。

它带着她疾疾向前，一直向着巨大的光明。……一切变得越来越亮、越来越亮。……

澳大利亚姑娘格特鲁德·戈德林（Gertrude Goldring）正用双手弄出一阵阵响，像胀鼓鼓的纸袋被拍破。米丽亚姆攥住毛线针，穿上毛线。她把毛线穿过画布，一、三、五、三、一，一心盼着钢琴重新响起。

---

===Original===
Clara Bergmann followed. Miriam watched her as she took her place at the piano—how square and stout she looked and old, careworn, like a woman of forty. She had high square shoulders and high square hips—her brow was low and her face thin broad and flat. Her eyes were like the eyes of a dog and her thin-lipped mouth long and straight until it went steadily down at the corners. She wore a large fringe like Harriett’s—and a thin coil of hair filled the nape of her neck. She played, without music, her face lifted boldly. The notes rang out in a prelude of unfinished phrases—the kind, Miriam noted, that had so annoyed her father in what he called newfangled music—she felt it was going to be a brilliant piece—fireworks—execution—style—and sat up self-consciously and fixed her eyes on Clara’s hands. “Can you see the hands?” she remembered having heard someone say at a concert. How easily they moved. Clara still sat back, her face raised to the light. The notes rang out like trumpet calls as her hands dropped with an easy fling and sprang back and dropped again. What loose wrists she must have, thought Miriam. The clarion notes ceased. There was a pause. Clara threw back her head, a faint smile flickered over her face, her hands fell gently and the music came again, pianissimo, swinging in an even rhythm. It flowed from those clever hands, a half-indicated theme with a gentle, steady, throbbing undertow. Miriam dropped her eyes—she seemed to have been listening long—that wonderful light was coming again—she had forgotten her sewing—when presently she saw, slowly circling, fading and clearing, first its edge, and then, for a moment the whole thing, dripping, dripping as it circled, a weed-grown mill wheel. … She recognised it instantly. She had seen it somewhere as a child—in Devonshire—and never thought of it since—and there it was. She heard the soft swish and drip of the water and the low humming of the wheel. How beautiful … it was fading. … She held it—it returned—clearer this time and she could feel the cool breeze it made, and sniff the fresh earthy scent of it, the scent of the moss and the weeds shining and dripping on its huge rim. Her heart filled. She felt a little tremor in her throat. All at once she knew that if she went on listening to that humming wheel and feeling the freshness of the air, she would cry. She pulled herself together, and for a while saw only a vague radiance in the room and the dim forms grouped about. She could not remember which was which. All seemed good and dear to her. The trumpet notes had come back, and in a few moments the music ceased. … Someone was closing the great doors from inside the schoolroom. As the side behind which she was sitting swung slowly to, she caught a glimpse, through the crack, of four boys with close-cropped heads, sitting at the long table. The gas was out and the room was dim, but a reading lamp in the centre of the table cast its light on their bowed heads.

===Chinese===
接下来是克拉拉·贝格曼（Clara Bergmann）。米丽亚姆望着她在钢琴前落座——她看上去多么方正、多么壮实，多么苍老而饱经忧患，像个四十岁的妇人。她有着又高又方的肩膀和又高又方的胯部——额角低低的，脸瘦长扁平。她的眼睛像狗的眼睛，薄唇的嘴长而直直的，直到嘴角才坚定地垂下去。她留着哈丽特那样的浓密刘海——颈窝里填着一圈细细的发盘。她不看谱子弹奏，脸大胆地扬着。音符在一串未完成乐句的前奏中鸣响——米丽亚姆注意到，正是那种她父亲在他所谓的时髦新音乐里深恶痛绝的乐句——她感到这将是一支辉煌的曲子——烟火——技巧——风格——便不自然地坐直，眼睛盯住克拉拉的双手。“看得清那双手吗？”她记得曾在某场音乐会上听人这样说过。它们动得多么从容。克拉拉仍旧靠后坐着，脸迎着光。她的双手轻松地一甩落下、又弹回、再落下，音符如号角般鸣响。她的手腕该多么松弛啊，米丽亚姆想。嘹亮的音符停了。有一个停顿。克拉拉把头向后一仰，一丝浅笑掠过她的脸，她的手轻轻落下，音乐重新到来，极轻（pianissimo），摇在一个均匀的节奏里。它从那双灵巧的手里流出来，一个半露半藏的主题，底下是一股温柔、沉稳、搏动的潜流。米丽亚姆垂下眼睛——她似乎已经听了很久——那种奇妙的光又来了——她忘了针线——忽然她看见，慢慢地旋转着、暗下去又亮起来，先是一道边缘，然后，有一瞬是那整个东西，滴着、滴着水旋转着，一只长满杂草的磨坊水轮。……她立刻认出了它。她小时候在什么地方见过它——在德文郡（Devonshire）——此后再没想起过——可它就在这儿。她听见水轻柔的哗哗与滴沥，还有水轮低低的嗡鸣。多么美啊……它在暗下去。……她抓住它——它回来了——这一次更清楚，她能觉出它扇起的凉风，嗅到它那新鲜泥土的气息，苔藓和杂草的气味，那些在它巨大的轮缘上闪光滴水的苔藓与杂草。她的心涨满了。喉咙里有一阵轻微的颤动。她忽然明白，要是再听那嗡鸣的水轮、再感受那空气的清凉，她就要哭了。她让自己收拢回来，有一阵子只看见房间里一片朦胧的光辉和四周模糊的身影。她记不清哪个是哪个了。一切在她眼里都显得又好又亲。号角般的音符回来了，不多时，音乐结束了。……有人正从教室那一头关上大门。她背后那扇门缓缓转拢时，她从门缝里瞥见四个剃着平头的男孩，坐在长桌旁。煤气灯熄了，房间昏暗，只有桌子中央的一盏读书灯把光投在他们低伏的头上。

---
===Original===
The playing of the two Martins brought back the familiar feeling of English self-consciousness. Solomon, the elder one, sat at her Beethoven sonata, an adagio movement, with a patch of dull crimson on the pallor of the cheek she presented to the room, but she played with a heavy fervour, preserving throughout the characteristic marching staccato of the bass, and gave unstinted value to the shading of each phrase. She made Miriam feel nervous at first and then—as she went triumphantly forward and let herself go so tremendously—traction engine, thought Miriam—in the heavy fortissimos—a little ashamed of such expression coming from English hands. The feeling of shame lingered as the younger sister followed with a spirited vivace. Her hollow-cheeked pallor remained unstained, but her thin lips were set and her hard eyes were harder. She played with determined nonchalance and an extraordinarily facile rapidity, and Miriam’s uneasiness changed insensibly to the conviction that these girls were learning in Germany not to be ashamed of “playing with expression.” All the things she had heard Mr. Strood—who had, as the school prospectus declared, been “educated in Leipzig”—preach and implore, “style,” “expression,” “phrasing,” “light and shade,” these girls were learning, picking up from these wonderful Germans. They did not do it quite like them though. They did not think only about the music, they thought about themselves too. Miriam believed she could do it as the Germans did. She wanted to get her own music and play it as she had always dimly known it ought to be played and hardly ever dared. Perhaps that was how it was with the English. They knew, but they did not dare. No. The two she had just heard playing were, she felt sure, imitating something—but hers would be no imitation. She would play as she wanted to one day in this German atmosphere.
