===Original===
For a week the whole of the housework and cooking was done by the girls under the superintendence of Gertrude, who seemed to be all over the house acting as forewoman to little gangs of workers. Miriam took but a small part in the work—Minna was paying long visits to the aurist every day—but she shared the depleted table and knew that the whole school was taking part in weathering the storm of Fräulein’s ill-humour that had broken first upon Anna. She once caught a glimpse of Gertrude flushed and downcast, confronting Fräulein’s reproachful voice upon the stairs; and one day in the basement she heard Ulrica tearfully refuse to clean her own boots and saw Fräulein stand before her bowing and smiling, and with the girls gathered round, herself brush and polish the slender boots.

She was glad to get away with Minna.

Her blouses came at the beginning of the week. She carried them upstairs. Her hands took them incredulously from their wrappages. The “squashed strawberry” lay at the top, soft warm clear madder-rose, covered with a black arabesque of tiny leaves and tendrils. It was compactly folded, showing only its turned-down collar, shoulders and breast. She laid it on her bed side by side with its buff companion and shook out the underlying skirt.﻿﻿ … How sweet of them to send her the things﻿﻿ … she felt tears in her eyes as she stood at her small looking glass with the skirt against her body and the blouses held in turn above it﻿﻿ … they both went perfectly with the light skirt.﻿﻿ … She unfolded them and shook them out and held them up at arms’ length by the shoulder seams. Her heart sank. They were not in the least like anything she had ever worn. They had no shape. They were square and the sleeves were like bags. She turned them about and remembered the shapeliness of the stockinette jerseys smocked and small and clinging that she had worn at school. If these were blouses then she would never be able to wear blouses.﻿﻿ … “They’re so flountery!” she said, frowning at them. She tried on the rose-coloured one. It startled her with its brightness.﻿﻿ … “It’s no good, it’s no good,” she said, as her hands fumbled for the fastenings. There was a hook at the neck; that was all. Frightful﻿﻿ … she fastened it, and the collar set in a soft roll but came down in front to the base of her neck. The rest of the blouse stuck out all round her﻿﻿ … “it’s got no cut﻿﻿ … they couldn’t have looked at it.”﻿﻿ … She turned helplessly about, using her hand glass, frowning and despairing. Presently she saw Harriett’s quizzical eyes and laughed woefully, tweaking at the outstanding margin of the material. “It’s all very well,” she murmured angrily, “but it’s all I’ve *got*.” … She wished Sarah were there. Sarah would do something, alter it or something. She heard her encouraging voice saying, “You haven’t half got it on yet. It’ll be all right.” She unfastened her black skirt, crammed the flapping margin within its band and put on the beaded black stuff belt.

The blouse bulged back and front shapelessly and seemed to be one with the shapeless sleeves which ended in hard loose bands riding untrimmed about her wrists with the movements of her hands.﻿﻿ … “It’s like a nightdress,” she said wrathfully and dragged the fulnesses down all round under her skirt. It looked better so in front; but as she turned with raised hand glass it came riding up at the side and back with the movement of her arm.

===Chinese===
整整一个星期，全部的家务和烹调都由姑娘们在格特鲁德的督导下操办——她仿佛遍布全屋，给一小伙一小伙的工人当女工头。米丽亚姆在这场劳动里只占了很小的份——明娜每天去耳科医生那儿作漫长的复诊——但减色的饭桌有她一份，她也知道，全校都在一同承受弗劳莱恩这场坏脾气的风暴，风暴最先劈在安娜身上。她有一次瞥见格特鲁德红着脸、垂着头，在楼梯上直面弗劳莱恩责备的声音；有一天在地下室，她听见乌尔丽卡噙着泪拒绝擦自己的靴子，又看见弗劳莱恩站在她面前，躬着身、微笑着，当着围拢来的姑娘们，亲手把那双纤小的靴子刷净擦亮。

她庆幸能同明娜一起出门。

她的上衣是那个星期开头寄到的。她把它们抱上楼。她的双手难以置信地把它们从包裹纸里取出来。那件“压碎的草莓”放在最上面，柔和、温暖、纯净的茜红玫瑰色，覆着黑色的小叶和卷须织成的阿拉伯花纹。它紧凑地折着，只露出翻下来的领子、肩和胸。她把它同浅黄褐色的那件并排放在床上，又抖开底下那条裙子。……她们把东西寄来，多贴心啊……她站在小小的穿衣镜前，裙子贴着身子，两件上衣轮流举在上面……眼中涌起了泪……两件都同这条浅色裙子配极了。……她把它们展开、抖开，捏着肩缝伸直了臂举起来。她的心沉了下去。它们同她平生穿过的任何东西没有一丝相像。它们没有形状。方方正正，袖子像口袋。她把它们翻来翻去，想起学校里穿过的那些针织紧身衣——打褶抽褶的、小小的、贴身合体的。倘若这些就是上衣，那她一辈子也穿不来上衣。……“真是花哨飘飘的（flountery）！”她皱着眉说。她试了那件玫瑰色的。它的鲜亮吓了她一跳。……“不行，不行的。”她的手摸索着纽钩时她说。领口有一只钩扣；仅此而已。吓人……她扣上它，领子翻成柔软的一道卷，前面却一直开到颈根。上衣的其余部分四面支棱在她身上……“它没有剪裁……她们根本没看过它一眼。”……她无助地转来转去，用手镜照前照后，皱着眉，绝望了。不一会儿她看见哈丽特那双揶揄的眼睛，凄然笑了，揪着支棱出来的那圈料子。“说得倒好听，”她忿忿地喃喃说，“可我*就*有这个。”……她多希望萨拉在。萨拉会有办法，改一改或者怎么样。她听见她鼓劲的声音说：“你还没穿上去一半呢。会好的。”她解开黑裙子，把扑腾腾的衣摆一把塞进裙腰，系上缀珠的黑料腰带。

上衣前后没形地鼓着，同没形的袖子连成了一体，袖口收成松松的硬圈，毛边也不锁，随着两手的动作在腕上滑来滑去。……“像件睡衣。”她怒冲冲地说，把四面的松垮使劲往下拽，塞进裙腰里。前面这样看着好些了；可她举着手镜一转身，衣摆又随着胳膊的动作从侧面和后面拱上来。

---
===Original===
Minna was calling to her from the stairs. She went on to the landing to answer her and found her on the top flight dressed to go out.

“Ach!” she whispered as Miriam drew back. “Jetzt mag’ ich Sie leiden. *Now* I like you.”

She ran back to her room. There was no time to change. She fixed a brooch in the collar to make it come a little higher at the join.

Going downstairs she saw Pastor Lahmann hanging up his hat in the hall. His childish eyes came up as her step sounded on the lower flight.

Miriam was amazed to see him standing there as though nothing had happened. She did not know that she was smiling at him until his face lit up with an answering smile.

“Bonjour, mademoiselle.”

Miriam did not answer and he disappeared into the *Saal*.

She went on downstairs listening to his voice, repeating his words over and over in her mind.

Jimmie was sweeping the basement floor with a duster tied round her hair.

“Hullo, Mother Bunch,” she laughed.

“It *is* weird, isn’t it? Not a bit the kind I meant to have.”

“The blouse is all right, my dear, but it’s all round your ears and you’ve got all the fullness in the wrong place. There.﻿﻿ … Bless the woman, you’ve got no drawstring! And you must pin it at the back! And haven’t you got a proper leather belt?”

===Chinese===
明娜在楼梯上唤她。她走到平台上应声，看见她已经穿戴整齐，站在顶层楼梯上。

“Ach（啊）！”米丽亚姆退后一步时她悄声说。“Jetzt mag’ ich Sie leiden（现在我中意您了）。*现在*我喜欢您了。”

她跑回自己的房间。来不及换了。她在领口别上一枚胸针，好让接缝处高起一点。

下楼时她看见拉曼牧师正在门厅里挂帽子。她的脚步在下层楼梯上一响，他那双孩子气的眼睛便抬了起来。

米丽亚姆惊异地看见他站在那里，仿佛什么都没有发生过。直到他的脸亮起一个相应的微笑，她才知道自己正对着他微笑。

“Bonjour, mademoiselle（日安，小姐）。”

米丽亚姆没有回答，他走进*大厅*去了。

她继续往下走，听着他的嗓音，在心里一遍又一遍地重复他的话。

吉米正用一块抹布扎着头发，扫地下室的地面。

“喂，邦奇妈妈（Mother Bunch）。”她笑道。

“是*怪吧*，不是吗？一点也不是我想要的那一种。”

“上衣挺好的，亲爱的，可是它全堆到耳朵边了，松垮劲儿也全放错了地方。瞧。……老天爷，你居然没装抽带！后背一定要用别针别住！还有，你没有一条像样的皮带吗？”

---

===Original===
Minna and Miriam ambled gently along together. Miriam had discarded her little fur pelerine and her double-breasted jacket bulged loosely over the thin fabric of her blouse. She breathed in the leaf-scented air and felt it playing over her breast and neck. She drew deep breaths as they went slowly along under the Waldstraße lime trees and looked up again and again at the leaves brilliant opaque green against white plaster with sharp black shadows behind them, or brilliant transparent green on the hard blue sky. She felt that the scent of them must be visible. Every breath she drew was like a long yawning sigh. She felt the easy expansion of her body under her heavy jacket.﻿﻿ … “Perhaps I won’t have any more fitted bodices,” she mused and was back for a moment in the stale little sitting room of the Barnes dressmaker. She remembered deeply breathing in the odour of fabrics and dust and dankness and cracking her newly fitted lining at the pinholes and saying, “It is too tight there”—crack-crack. “I can’t go like that.”﻿﻿ …

“But you never want to go like that, my dear child,” old Miss Ottridge had laughed, readjusting the pins; “just breathe in your ordinary way—there, see? That’s right.”

Perhaps Lilla’s mother was right about blouses﻿﻿ … perhaps they were “slommucky.” She remembered phrases she had heard about people’s figures﻿﻿ … “falling abroad”﻿﻿ … “the middle-aged sprawl”﻿﻿ … that would come early to her as she was so old and worried﻿﻿ … perhaps that was why one had to wear boned bodices﻿﻿ … and never breathe in gulps of air like this?﻿﻿ … It was as if all the worry were being taken out of her temples. She felt her eyes grow strong and clear; a coolness flowed through her—obstructed only where she felt the heavy pad of hair pinned to the back of her head, the line of her hat, the hot line of compression round her waist and the confinement of her inflexible boots.

They were approaching the Georgstraße with its long-vistaed width and its shops and cafés and pedestrians. An officer in pale blue Prussian uniform passed by flashing a single hard preoccupied glance at each of them in turn. His eyes seemed to Miriam like opaque blue glass. She could not remember such eyes in England. They began to walk more quickly. Miriam listened abstractedly to Minna’s anticipations of three days at a friend’s house when she would visit her parents at the end of the week. Minna’s parents, her faraway home on the outskirts of a little town, its garden, their little carriage, the spring, the beautiful country seemed unreal and her efforts to respond and be interested felt like a sort of treachery to her present bliss.﻿﻿ … Everybody, even docile Minna, always seemed to want to talk about something else.﻿﻿ …

Suddenly she was aware that Minna was asking her whether, if it was decided that she should leave school at the end of the term, she, Miriam, would come and live with her.

Miriam beamed incredulously. Minna, crimson-faced, with her eyes on the pavement and hurrying along explained that she was alone at home, that she had never made friends—her mother always wanted her to make friends—but she could not—that her parents would be so delighted—that she, she wanted Miriam, “You, you are so different, so reasonable—I could live with you.”

Minna’s garden, her secure country house, her rich parents, no worries, nothing particular to do, seemed for a moment to Miriam the solution and continuation of all the gay day. There would be the rest of the term—increasing spring and summer—Fräulein divested of all mystery and fear and then freedom—with Minna.

She glanced at Minna—the cheerful pink face and the pink bulb of nose came round to her and in an excited undertone she murmured something about the *Apotheker*.

“I should love to come—simply love it,” said Miriam enthusiastically, feeling that she would not entirely give up the idea yet. She would not shut off the offered refuge. It would be a plan to have in reserve. She had been daunted as Minna murmured by a picture of Minna and herself in that remote garden—she receiving confidences about the *Apotheker*—no one else there—the Waldstraße household blotted out—herself and Minna finding pretexts day after day to visit the chemist’s in the little town.

===Chinese===
明娜和米丽亚姆缓缓地并肩闲步。米丽亚姆撂下了她的小毛皮披肩，双排扣的外衣松松地鼓在薄薄的上衣外面。她呼吸着带树叶香的空气，感到它在胸颈上抚过。沿着瓦尔德街的椴树慢慢走着，她一口一口深吸着气，一遍一遍仰头看那些叶子——衬着白灰墙、背后拖着尖锐黑影的，是不透明的亮绿；映在硬蓝天空上的，是透明的亮绿。她觉得它们的香气一定是看得见的。她的每一口气都像一声长长的、打哈欠似的叹息。她感到身体在沉重的外衣底下自在地舒张。……“也许我再也不穿紧腰身的上衣了。”她寻思着，一时又回到巴恩斯裁缝铺那间憋闷的小客厅里。她想起自己深深吸进布料、灰尘和潮气的味道，把新试好的衬里在针孔处绷得咔咔作响，说“这儿太紧了”——咔、咔。“我不能这样出去。”……

“可您从来也不想这样出去呀，亲爱的孩子。”奥特里奇老小姐笑道，重新别了别针；“照您平常的样子呼吸——喏，看见没？这就对了。”

也许莉拉的妈妈对上衣的看法是对的……也许它们就是“邋遢”。她想起听人说过的人们身材的那些话……“散架子”……“中年的摊塌”……她年纪这么轻就这么操劳，这些怕是要早早找上门来……也许正因如此才得穿缀骨的紧身上衣……再不能像这样大口大口地吸气？……仿佛所有的烦忧正从她的太阳穴里被取出去。她感到眼睛变得清明有力；一股清凉流遍全身——只在她感到后脑别着的那一垫沉沉的头发、帽檐的一圈、腰上箍着的热热的一道、和不肯屈伸的靴子的紧束时，才受了阻碍。

她们渐渐走近格奥尔格大街，长长的街景宽宽地铺开去，商店、咖啡馆、行人。一位穿浅蓝普鲁士军装的军官走过，对她们两个挨个闪过一记冷硬的、心不在焉的注视。在米丽亚姆看来，他的眼睛像不透明的蓝玻璃。她不记得英国有这样的眼睛。她们开始走得快了些。米丽亚姆心不在焉地听着明娜的憧憬：周末回家探望父母之后，要在一位朋友家里住上三天。明娜的父母、她在小镇边缘的遥远的家、家里的花园、他们的小马车、春天、美丽的乡野，都显得不真实；她努力应答、努力表示兴趣，感觉起来倒像是对眼下这份幸福的一种背叛。……每个人，连温顺的明娜，好像总想谈点别的什么。……

突然她察觉明娜正在问她：倘若学期末定下来让她离开学校，她，米丽亚姆，愿不愿意来同她一起住。

米丽亚姆难以置信地绽开了笑。明娜满脸绯红，眼睛盯着路面，匆匆走着，解释说她在家孤零零的，她从没交过朋友——妈妈总希望她交朋友——可她做不到——她父母会喜出望外——她，她想要米丽亚姆，“你，你不一样，这么通情达理——我可以同你一起生活。”

明娜的花园、她安稳的乡间宅子、她阔绰的父母，没有忧患，没有什么非做不可的事，一时之间在米丽亚姆看来，正是这一天欢快的解决和延续。这个学期剩下的日子会过去——渐浓的春和夏——弗劳莱恩卸尽一切神秘与可怕——然后就是自由——同明娜在一起。

她瞥了明娜一眼——那张快活的粉红脸和粉红的蒜头鼻转过来对着她，压低嗓门兴奋地咕哝了句关于*药剂师*（Apotheker）的什么。

“我愿意来——简直太愿意了。”米丽亚姆热热诚诚地说，心里并不想就此把念头全然放弃。她不想关上这扇递过来的避难所。留作一个备用的计划也好。明娜低语时，她被一幅图景慑住了：明娜同她在那座偏远的花园里——她听着关于*药剂师*的知心话——别无他人——瓦尔德街的一家人被抹去了——她自己同明娜，一天又一天地找着借口，去小镇上的药房。

---

===Original===
Miriam almost ran home from seeing Minna into the three o’clock train﻿﻿ … dear beautiful, beautiful Hanover﻿﻿ … the sunlight blazed from the rain-sprinkled streets. Everything shone. Bright confident shops, happy German cafés moved quickly by as she fled along. Sympathetic eyes answered hers. She almost laughed once or twice when she met an eye and thought how funny she must look “tearing along” with her long, thick, black jacket bumping against her.﻿﻿ … She would leave it off tomorrow and go out in a blouse and her long black lace scarf.﻿﻿ … She imagined Harriett at her side—Harriett’s long scarf and longed to do the “crab walk” for a moment or the halfpenny dip, hippety-hop. She did them in her mind.

She heard the sound of her boot soles tapping the shining pavement as she hurried along﻿﻿ … she would write a short note to her mother “a girl about my own age with very wealthy parents who wants a companion” and enclose a note for Eve or Harriett﻿﻿ … Eve, “Imagine me in Pomerania, my dear”﻿﻿ … and tell her about the coffee parties and the skating and the sleighing and Minna’s German Christmasses.﻿﻿ …

She saw Minna’s departing face leaning from the carriage window, its new gay boldness: “I shall no more when we are at home call you Miss Henderson.”

When she got back to Waldstraße she found Anna’s successor newly arrived cleaning the neglected front doorstep. Her lean yellow face looked a vacant response to Miriam’s enquiry for Fräulein Pfaff.

“Ist Fräulein zu Hause,” she repeated. The girl shook her head vaguely.

How quiet the house seemed. The girls, after a morning spent in turning out the kitchen for the reception of the new *Magd* were out for a long ramble, including *Schokolade mit Schlagsahne* until teatime.

The empty house spread round her and towered above her as she took off her things in the basement and the schoolroom yawned bright and empty as she reached the upper hall. She hesitated by the door. There was no sound anywhere.﻿﻿ … She would play﻿﻿ … on the *Saal* piano.

“I’m not a Lehrerin—I’m not—I’m—not,” she hummed as she collected her music﻿﻿ … she would bring her songs too.﻿﻿ … “I’m going to Pom-pom-pom-Pom‑erain‑eeya.”

===Chinese===
送明娜上了三点钟的火车，米丽亚姆几乎是一路跑回家的……亲爱的、美丽的、美丽的汉诺威……阳光从洒过雨的街面上腾腾地烧起来。样样东西都在发亮。明亮而自信的商店、快活的德国咖啡馆，随着她一路飞奔迅速掠过。投来的目光同她的目光相答。有一两次她迎上一道目光，想到自己“一路狂奔”、又长又厚的黑外衣扑腾腾拍打着，模样该多滑稽，几乎笑出来。……明天她不穿它了，就穿一件上衣，围上她那条长长的黑蕾丝披巾。……她想象哈丽特在身边——哈丽特的长披巾——一时真想走一回“螃蟹步”，或者来个半便士颠步、单脚蹦跳步。她在心里把它们走了一遍。

她听见自己的鞋底叩在发亮的人行道上，嗒、嗒、嗒，一路匆匆……她要给妈妈写封短信，“一个同我差不多大的姑娘，父母极有钱，想请一位女伴”，再附上一封给伊芙或哈丽特的信……给伊芙的，“想想我在波美拉尼亚的样子，亲爱的”……再讲给她听咖啡会、滑冰、雪橇，还有明娜家的德国圣诞节。……

她看见明娜从车窗里探出来的、正在远去的脸，那副新添的快活的泼辣：“到了家，我就再不会叫你亨德森小姐了。”

回到瓦尔德街，她发现安娜的继任者刚到，正在擦那被怠慢的前门台阶。她探问普法夫小姐，那张瘦黄的脸上是一片茫然的回应。

“Ist Fräulein zu Hause（弗劳莱恩在家吗）。”她又问了一遍。姑娘含糊地摇了摇头。

这宅子显得多静啊。姑娘们花了一个上午翻厨房、迎候新来的女仆（*Magd*），此刻都出门远足去了，一路玩到茶点时分，外加巧克力加掼奶油（*Schokolade mit Schlagsahne*）。

她在地下室卸衣装时，空宅四面围着她、高高耸在她头上；走上楼上的门厅时，教室明亮地打着哈欠，空空的。她在门边犹豫。四处没有一点声音。……她要弹琴……用*大厅*的那架钢琴。

“我不是个女教师（Lehrerin）——我不是——我不是——”她一面收乐谱一面哼着……她要把歌也带去。……“我要去波——波——波美拉——尼亚。”

---

===Original===
“Pom‑erain‑eeya,” she hummed, swinging herself round the great door into the *Saal*. Pastor Lahmann was standing near one of the windows. The rush of her entry carried her to the middle of the room and he met her there smiling quietly. She stared easily and comfortably up into his great mild eyes, went into them as they remained quietly and gently there, receiving her. Presently he said in a soft low tone, “You are vairy happy, mademoiselle.”

Miriam moved her eyes from his face and gazed out of the window into the little sunlit summerhouse. The sense of the outline of his shoulders and his comforting black mannishness so near to her brought her almost to tears. Fiercely she fixed the sunlit summerhouse, “Oh, I’m *not*,” she said.

“Not? Is it possible?”

“I think life is perfectly appalling.”

She moved awkwardly to a little chiffonier and put down her music on its marble top.

He came safely following her and stood near again.

“You do not like the life of the school?”

“Oh, I don’t know.”

“You are from the country, mademoiselle.”

Miriam fumbled with her music.﻿﻿ … Was she?

“One sees that at once. You come from the land.”

Miriam glanced at his solid white profile as he stood with hands clasped, near her music, on the chiffonier. She noticed again that strange flatness of the lower part of the face.

“I, too, am from the land. I grew up on a farm. I love the land and think to return to it—to have my little strip when I am free—when my boys have done their schooling. I shall go back.”

He turned towards her and Miriam smiled into the soft brown eyes and tried to think of something to say.

“My grandfather was a gentleman farmer.”

“Ah—that does not surprise me—but what a very English expression!”

“Is it?”

“Well, it sounds so to us. We Swiss are very democratic.”

“I think I’m a radical.”

Pastor Lahmann lifted his chin and laughed softly.

“You are a vairy ambitious young lady.”

“Yes.”

Pastor Lahmann laughed again.

“I, too, am ambitious. I have a good Swiss ambition.”

Miriam smiled into the mild face.

“You have a beautiful English provairb which expresses my ambition.”

Miriam looked, eagerly listening, into the brown eyes that came round to meet hers, smiling:

“A little land, well-tilled,

A little wife, well-willed,

Are great riches.”

Miriam seemed to gaze long at a pallid, rounded man with smiling eyes. She saw a garden and fields, a firelit interior, a little woman smiling and busy and agreeable moving quickly about﻿﻿ … and Pastor Lahmann—presiding. It filled her with fury to be regarded as one of a world of little tame things to be summoned by little men to be well-willed wives. She must make him see that she did not even recognise such a thing as “a well-willed wife.” She felt her gaze growing fixed and moved to withdraw it and herself.

“Why do you wear glasses, mademoiselle?”

The voice was full of sympathetic wistfulness.

“I have a severe myopic astigmatism,” she announced, gathering up her music and feeling the words as little hammers on the newly seen, pallid, rounded face.

“Dear me﻿﻿ … I wonder whether the glasses are really necessary.﻿﻿ … May I look at them?﻿﻿ … I know something of eye work.”

Miriam detached her tightly fitting pince-nez and having given them up stood with her music in hand anxiously watching. Half her vision gone with her glasses, she saw only a dim black-coated knowledge, near at hand, going perhaps to help her.

“You wear them always—for how long?

“Poor child, poor child, and you must have passed through all your schooling with those lame, lame eyes﻿﻿ … let me see the eyes﻿﻿ … turn a little to the light﻿﻿ … so.”

Standing near and large he scrutinised her vague gaze.

“And sensitive to light, too. You were vairy, vairy blonde, even more blonde than you are now, as a child, mademoiselle?”

“Na guten Tag, Herr Pastor.”

Fräulein Pfaff’s smiling voice sounded from the little door.

Pastor Lahmann stepped back.

Miriam was pleased at the thought of being grouped with him in the eyes of Fräulein Pfaff. As she took her glasses from his outstretched hand she felt that Fräulein would recognise that they had established a kind of friendliness. She halted for a moment at the door, adjusting her glasses, amiably uncertain, feeling for something to say.

Pastor Lahmann was standing in the middle of the room examining his nails. Fräulein, at the window, was twitching a curtain into place. She turned and drove Miriam from the room with speechless waiting eyes.

The sunlight was streaming across the hall. It seemed gay and homelike. Pastor Lahmann had made her forget she was a governess. He had treated her as a girl. Fräulein’s eyes had spoiled it. Fräulein was angry about it for some extraordinary reason.

===Chinese===
“波美拉——尼亚。”她哼着，一个旋身荡进*大厅*的大门。拉曼牧师正站在一扇窗子近旁。她进门的那股冲势把她带到房间中央，他含着安静的微笑在那里迎住了她。她安安逸逸地仰头望进他那双大大的、温和的眼睛，往里面走去，那双眼睛安安静静、温温和和地待在那里，接纳着她。过了一会儿，他柔声低语：“您很快活呀（vairy happy），小姐。”

米丽亚姆把目光从他脸上挪开，望出窗外，望着那座洒满阳光的小凉亭。他的肩膀的轮廓、他那令人安心的黑色的男子气概，近在身旁，几乎让她落泪。她狠狠地盯住阳光里的凉亭。“哦，我*不*快活。”她说。

“不？这可能吗？”

“我觉得生活可怕极了。”

她笨拙地挪到一张小柜前，把乐谱放在它的大理石面上。

他稳妥地跟了过来，又站在近旁。

“您不喜欢学校的生活？”

“哦，我不知道。”

“您是乡下来的，小姐。”

米丽亚姆摆弄着乐谱。……她是吗？

“一眼就看得出来。您从土地上来。”

他双手交叠、站在她的乐谱旁的小柜边，米丽亚姆瞥了瞥他那副坚实的白色侧影。她再次注意到他脸的下部那种奇异的扁平。

“我也是从土地上下来的。我在农场上长大。我爱土地，想着回去——等我一自由——等我的男孩子们念完书——就回去要上我小小的一条。我会回去的。”

他朝她转过身来，米丽亚姆对着那双温柔的棕色眼睛微笑，努力想找一句话说。

“我的祖父是一位乡绅农场主（gentleman farmer）。”

“啊——这不令我吃惊——不过，好一个地道的英国说法！”

“是吗？”

“嗯，在我们听来是这样。我们瑞士人非常民主。”

“我想我是个激进派。”

拉曼牧师抬起下巴，轻轻笑了。

“您是位雄心勃勃的年轻小姐（young lady）。”

“是的。”

拉曼牧师又笑了。

“我也雄心勃勃。我有一副很好的瑞士式的雄心。”

米丽亚姆对着那张温和的脸微笑。

“你们有一句漂亮的英国谚语（provairb），正说出我的雄心。”

米丽亚姆望着那双转过来迎她的棕色眼睛，热切地听着，它们笑着：

“一小片地，耕得细细，

一个小妻子，柔柔顺顺，

便是大财富。”

米丽亚姆仿佛久久凝视着一个苍白的、圆滚滚的男人，长着一双含笑的眼睛。她看见一座花园和田野，一间炉火照亮的屋子，一个小巧的女人，微笑着、忙碌着、和和悦悦地快步走动……而拉曼牧师——高高在上地主持着一切。被当作一个世界里的成员——一个由小男人们随时召唤、去做柔顺妻子的驯顺小东西的世界——这让她满腔怒火。她必须让他明白，她甚至不承认有“柔顺的妻子”这种东西。她感到自己的目光渐渐定住，便挪动身子，把目光同自己一起撤了回来。

“您为什么戴眼镜，小姐？”

那声音里满是同情的怅惘。

“我有严重的近视性散光。”她一本正经地宣布，一面收拢乐谱，一面感到这些字像小锤子，一下下敲在那张新看见的、苍白的圆脸上。

“哎呀……我不知道这眼镜是否真的必要。……能让我看看吗？……眼睛的事我略知一二。”

米丽亚姆摘下夹得紧紧的夹鼻眼镜，交出去之后，手里握着乐谱，不安地站在一旁看着。眼镜一去，她一半的视力也没了，只看见近在手边的一团模糊的黑衣的知识，也许要去帮助她。

“您一直戴着——戴了多久？

“可怜的孩子，可怜的孩子，您整个求学期间，想必就是靠这双跛了的、跛了的眼睛熬过来的……让我看看眼睛……朝光转一转……对，这样。”

他近近地、大大的站着，端详她茫然的目光。

“还怕光。您小时候一定很浅、很浅的金发（vairy, vairy blonde），比现在还要浅，小姐？”

“Na guten Tag, Herr Pastor（日安，牧师先生）。”

普法夫小姐含笑的声音从那扇小门传来。

拉曼牧师退后一步。

想到在普法夫小姐眼里自己同他归成了一伙，米丽亚姆心里高兴。她从他伸出的手里接回眼镜时，觉得弗劳莱恩会看出，他们之间已经建立起了一种友好的情谊。她在门口踌躇了一瞬，扶正眼镜，和和气气地犹豫着，想说句什么。

拉曼牧师站在房间中央，端详自己的指甲。弗劳莱恩在窗边，正把一幅窗帘抖齐归位。她转过身来，用一双不出声的、等待着的眼睛，把米丽亚姆从房间里赶了出去。

阳光正斜斜地淌过门厅。它显得快活而像家。拉曼牧师让她忘了自己是家庭教师。他把她当一个姑娘看待。弗劳莱恩的眼睛毁了它。弗劳莱恩不知为了什么非同小可的原因，在为此生气。

## VII / VII

===Original===
“Don’t let her *do* it, Miss Henderson.”

Fräulein Pfaff’s words broke the silence accompanying the servant’s progress from Gertrude whose soup plate she had first seized, to Miriam more than halfway down the table.

===Chinese===
“别让她*这么*干，亨德森小姐（Miss Henderson）。”

普法夫小姐（Fräulein Pfaff）的话打破了寂静——那寂静一路伴着女仆从格特鲁德（Gertrude）跟前走过来：她先端走的是格特鲁德的汤盘，此刻已走到长桌过了半程、米丽亚姆的身边。

===Original===
Startled into observation Miriam saw the soupspoon of her neighbour whisked, dripping, from its plate to the uppermost of Marie’s pile and Emma shrinking back with a horrified face against Jimmie who was leaning forward entranced with watching.… The whole table was watching. Marie, having secured Emma’s plate to the base of her pile clutched Miriam’s spoon. Miriam moved sideways as the spoon swept up, saw the desperate hard, lean face bend towards her for a moment as her plate was seized, heard an exclamation of annoyance from Fräulein and little sounds from all round the table. Marie had passed on to Clara. Clara received her with plate and spoon held firmly together and motioned her before she would relinquish them, to place her load upon the shelf of the lift.

Miriam felt she was in disgrace with the whole table.… She sat, flaring, rapidly framing phrase after phrase for the lips of her judges… “slow and awkward”… “never has her wits about her.…”

“Don’t let her do it, Miss Henderson.…” Why should Fräulein fix upon *her* to teach her common servants? Struggling through her resentment was pride in the fact that she did not know how to handle soup plates. Presently she sat refusing absolutely to accept the judgment silently assailing her on all hands.

===Chinese===
一惊之下米丽亚姆才留意去看：只见邻座爱玛（Emma）的汤匙被猛地拎起，滴着汤，从盘里飞到玛丽（Marie）那摞盘子的最顶上；爱玛满脸惊恐地缩回去，靠在吉米（Jimmie）身上，吉米正探着身子，看得入了迷。……全桌都在看。玛丽把爱玛的盘子扣牢在自己那摞的底下之后，又一把抓住米丽亚姆的汤匙。汤匙扫上来的当儿，米丽亚姆往旁边挪了挪，看见那张孤注一掷的、又硬又瘦的脸朝她俯下来一瞬，她的盘子就被收走了；又听见弗劳莱恩一声恼怒的斥责，还有满桌四面八方响起的小声响。玛丽已经过去了，到了克拉拉（Clara）跟前。克拉拉把盘子和汤匙紧紧扣在一起接待她，先比划着让她把那一摞放到升降机的搁板上，才肯交出来。

米丽亚姆觉得自己在全桌人跟前丢了脸。……她坐着，心头火起，飞快地替她的裁判们一句接一句地编着话……“又慢又笨”……“脑子从来不知道拐弯。……”

“别让她这么干，亨德森小姐。……”为什么弗劳莱恩偏偏挑中*她*来管教自己那些粗使女仆？在怨愤里挣扎着的，还有一丝自豪——她确实不知道怎么摆弄汤盘。不多时，她坐在那里，断然不肯接受那从四面八方无声袭来的判决。

===Original===
“You are not very domesticated, Miss Henderson.”

“No,” responded Miriam quietly, in joy and fear.

Fräulein gave a short laugh.

Goaded, Miriam plunged forward.

“We were never even allowed in the kitchen at home.”

“I see. You and your sisters were brought up like Countesses, wie Gräfinnen,” observed Fräulein Pfaff drily.

===Chinese===
“您可不太会操持家务啊，亨德森小姐。”

“是的。”米丽亚姆轻声应道，心里又欢喜又害怕。

弗劳莱恩短短地笑了一声。

被这一笑一激，米丽亚姆贸然往前冲了。

“在家里，人家连厨房都不许我们进。”

“我明白了。你和你那几个姐妹，是被当作伯爵夫人抚养大的，wie Gräfinnen（像伯爵夫人）。”普法夫小姐干巴巴地说。

===Original===
Miriam’s whole body was on fire… “and your sisters and your sisters,” echoed through and through her. Holding back her tears she looked full at Fräulein and met the brown eyes. She met them until they turned away and Fräulein broke into smiling generalities. Conversation was released all round the table. Emphatic undertones reached her from the English side. “Fool”… “simply idiotic.”

“I’ve done it now,” mused Miriam calmly, on the declining tide of her wrath.

Pretending to be occupied with those about her she sat examining the look Fräulein had given her… she hates me.… Perhaps she did from the first.… She did from the first.… I shall have to go… and suddenly, lately, she has grown worse.…

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆浑身像着了火……“还有你姐妹、还有你姐妹”，在她里里外外回响不息。她忍住眼泪，直直地望住弗劳莱恩，迎上那双棕色的眼睛。她就那样迎着，直到那双眼睛转了开去，弗劳莱恩绽出笑来，说起泛泛的闲话。全桌的谈话顿时解了冻。英国那一侧有加重语气的低语传到她耳中——“傻瓜”……“简直是白痴。”

“这下我可是闯了祸。”米丽亚姆平静地想道，怒潮正在退落。

她装作忙着应付身边的人们，坐在那里细细检视弗劳莱恩方才给她的那一眼……她恨我。……也许从一开始就恨。……从一开始就恨。……我恐怕只得走了……还有，突然间——近来，她变本加厉了。……

## VIII / VIII

===Original===
Walking along a narrow muddy causeway by a little river overhung with willows, girls ahead of her in single file and girls in single file behind, Miriam drearily recognised that it was June. The month of roses, she thought, and looked out across the flat green fields. It was not easy to walk along the slippery pathway. On one side was the little grey river, on the other long wet grass repelling and depressing. Not far ahead was the roadway which led, she supposed to the farm where they were to drink new milk. She would have to walk with someone when they came to the road, and talk. She wondered whether this early morning walk would come, now, every day. Her heart sank at the thought. It had been too hot during the last few days for any going out at midday, and she had hoped that the strolling in the garden, sitting about under the chestnut tree and in the little wooden garden room off the *Saal* had taken the place of walks for the summer.

She had got up reluctantly, at the surprise of the very early gonging. Mademoiselle had guessed it would be a “milk walk.” Pausing in the bright light of the top landing as Mademoiselle ran downstairs she had seen through the landing window the deep peak of a distant gable casting an unfamiliar shadow﻿—a shadow sloping the wrong way, a morning shadow. She remembered the first time, the only time, she had noticed such a shadow﻿—getting up very early one morning while Harriett and all the household were still asleep﻿—and how she had stopped dressing and gazed at it as it stood there cool and quiet and alone across the mellow face of a neighbouring stone porch﻿—had suddenly been glad that she was alone and had wondered why that shadowed porch peak was more beautiful than all the summer things she knew and felt at that moment that nothing could touch or trouble her again.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆沿着一条狭窄泥泞的堤道走着，堤道傍着一条垂柳低覆的小河，她前面是排成单行的女孩们，后面也是排成单行的女孩们。她凄惶地意识到：已经是六月了。玫瑰的月份啊，她想，一面放眼望过平坦的绿野。这条滑溜溜的小径走起来并不容易。一边是灰色的小河，另一边是长长的湿草，拒人千里，叫人丧气。前面不远就是大路，她猜想那路通往她们要去喝鲜牛奶的农庄。到了大路上，她就得跟什么人并肩走了，还得说话。她寻思，这样一大早的散步，往后会不会天天都有。一想到这个，她的心就沉了下去。这几天中午热得根本出不了门，她本还指望，在花园里遛遛、在栗子树下坐坐、在*大厅*（*Saal*）旁边那间小木造花园房里坐坐，就把这个夏天的散步全给顶替了呢。

她是被那阵出人意料的、极早的锣声催起来的，老大不情愿。Mademoiselle猜到了，这会是一次“牛奶散步”。Mademoiselle跑下楼去时，她在顶层平台明亮的天光里停留了片刻，透过平台的窗子望见远处一道山墙的尖顶投下陌生的影子——影子斜向不对的方向，是一个早晨的影子。她记起自己第一次、也是唯一一次注意到这样的影子——一天清晨她起得极早，哈丽特和全家还都睡着——她怎样停下不再穿衣，凝望着它，看它凉凉的、静静的、孤零零地立在邻居一座石头门廊那温润的脸上——她怎样突然为独自一人而欢喜，纳闷那罩着影子的门廊尖顶，为什么比她所知的一切夏天的东西都美，而且在那一刻感到，再没有什么能触碰她、搅扰她了。

===Original===
She could not find anything of that feeling in the early day outside Hanover. She was hemmed in, and the fields were so sad she could not bear to look at them. The sun had disappeared since they came out. The sky was grey and low and it seemed warmer already than it had been in the midday sun during the last few days. One of the girls on ahead hummed the refrain of a student song:﻿—

“In der Ecke steht er

Seinen Schnurrbart dreht er

Siehst du wohl, da steht er schon

Der versoff’ne Schwiegersohn.”

Miriam felt very near the end of endurance.

===Chinese===
在汉诺威城外的这个清晨里，那感觉她一丝一毫也找不回来了。她被围堵着，田野又那么哀愁，她不忍去看。她们出来以后，太阳就不见了。天是灰的，低低的，似乎已经比这几天正午的日头底下还要暖。前面一个女孩哼起一支学生歌的副歌：——

*In der Ecke steht er*
*Seinen Schnurrbart dreht er*
*Siehst du wohl, da steht er schon*
*Der versoff’ne Schwiegersohn.*
（他站在角落里，
捻着他的小胡子，
瞧见了吧，他已经站在那儿了——
那个灌得烂醉的女婿。）

米丽亚姆觉得自己快要忍到头了。

===Original===
Elsa Speier who was just behind her, became her inevitable companion when they reached the roadway. A farmhouse appeared about a quarter of a mile away.

Miriam’s sense of her duties closed in on her. Trying not to see Elsa’s elaborate clothes and the profile in which she could find no meaning, no hope, no rest, she spoke to her.

“Do you like milk, Elsa?” she said cheerfully.

Elsa began swinging her lace-covered parasol.

“If I like milk?” she repeated presently, and flashed mocking eyes in Miriam’s direction.

Despair touched Miriam’s heart.

“Some people don’t,” she said.

Elsa hummed and swung her parasol.

“Why should I like milk?” she stated.

The muddy farmyard, lying back from the roadway and below it, was steamy and choking with odours. Miriam who had imagined a cool dairy and cold milk frothing in pans, felt a loathing as warmth came to her fingers from the glass she held. Most of the girls were busily sipping. She raised her glass once towards her lips, snuffed a warm reek, and turned away towards the edge of the group, to pour out the contents of her glass, unseen, upon the filth-sodden earth.

===Chinese===
到了大路上，走在她身后的埃尔莎·施派尔（Elsa Speier）便成了她躲不开的同伴。四分之一英里外现出一座农舍。

米丽亚姆的职责感把她攥住了。她竭力不去看埃尔莎那身考究的衣裳，不去看那张她从中找不到任何意义、任何希望、任何安宁的侧脸，开口同她说话。

“你喜欢牛奶吗，埃尔莎？”她快活地说。

埃尔莎摇晃起她那把缀着花边的小阳伞。

“我喜欢不喜欢牛奶？”过了片刻她重复道，朝米丽亚姆这边闪过来一双讥诮的眼睛。

绝望触到了米丽亚姆的心。

“有些人不喜欢的。”她说。

埃尔莎哼着什么，摇着她的阳伞。

“我凭什么要喜欢牛奶？”她陈述道。

那片农家庭院缩在大路后面，比路面低洼，蒸腾着水汽，种种气味呛得人透不过气。米丽亚姆本想象的是凉爽的奶坊、平底盆里翻涌着冷奶的泡沫，此刻玻璃杯的暖意一传到指头上，她便觉出一阵憎恶。大多数女孩都在起劲地啜饮。她把杯子朝嘴唇举了一回，闻见一股温热的腥气，便转身朝人群边上挪去，趁人不注意，把杯里的东西倒在浸透了污秽的泥地上。

---

===Original===
Passing languidly up through the house after breakfast, unable to decide to spend her Saturday morning as usual at a piano in one of the bedrooms, Miriam went, wondering in response to a quiet call from Fräulein Pfaff into the large room shared by the Bergmanns and Ulrica Hesse. Explaining that Clara was now to take possession of the half of Elsa Speier’s room that had been left empty by Minna﻿—“poor Minna now with her good parents seeking health in the Swiss mountains, schooldays at an end, at an end, at an end,” she repeated mournfully. Fräulein indicated that Clara’s third of the large room would now be Miriam’s.

Miriam stood incredulous at her side as she indicated a large empty chest of drawers, a white covered bed in a deep corner away from the window, a small drawer in the dressing table and five pegs in a large French wardrobe. Emma was going very gravely about the room collecting her workbasket and things for *raccommodage*. She flung one ecstatic glance at Miriam as she went away with these.

“I shall hold you responsible here amongst these dear children, Miss Henderson,” fluted Fräulein, quietly gathering up a few last things of Minna’s collected on the bed, “our dear Ulrica and our little Emma,” she smiled, passing out, leaving Miriam standing in the wonderful room.

“My goodney,” she breathed, gathering gently clenched fists close to her person. She stood for a few moments; she felt like a visitor﻿﻿ … embroidered toilet covers, polished furniture, gold and cream crockery, lace curtains, white beds, the large screen cutting off her third of the room﻿﻿ … then she rushed headlong upstairs, a member of the downstairs landing, to collect her belongings.

On the landing just outside the door of the garret bedroom stood a huge wicker travelling basket; a clumsy umbrella with a large knobby handle, like a man’s umbrella, lay on the top of it partly covering a large pair of goloshes.

She was tired and very warm by the time everything was arranged in her new quarters.

Taking a last look round she caught the eye of Eve’s photograph gazing steadily at her from the chest of drawers.﻿﻿ … It would be quite easy now that this had happened to write and tell them that the Pomerania plan had come to nothing.

Evidently Fräulein approved of her, after all.

===Chinese===
早饭后她懒懒地穿过宅子上楼，还没拿定主意要不要照常到某间卧室的钢琴前去消磨这个星期六的上午，这时普法夫小姐（Fräulein Pfaff）轻轻唤了她一声，她便应声走进贝格曼家姊妹和乌尔丽卡·黑塞（Ulrica Hesse）合住的那间大房间。普法夫小姐解释说，克拉拉现在要搬进埃尔莎·施派尔房间里、米娜搬走后空出来的那一半——“可怜的米娜，如今跟着她善良的双亲在瑞士的群山里养身体，学生时代到头了，到头了，到头了。”她哀伤地一遍遍说。普法夫小姐表示，克拉拉在这间大房间里的三分之一，如今归米丽亚姆了。

米丽亚姆站在她身边，难以置信，随着她的指点看过去：一只空的大五斗橱，一个罩着白床罩的床位，在远离窗子的一个深凹的角落里，梳妆台上的一个小抽屉，还有一座法式大衣柜里的五枚挂钩。爱玛正极其郑重地在房间里转来转去，收拾她的针线筐和*缝补*（*raccommodage*）用的东西。她抱着这些东西往外走时，朝米丽亚姆抛来一个欣喜若狂的眼神。

“这群亲爱的孩子，我就托付给您了，亨德森小姐（Miss Henderson）。”普法夫小姐用长笛般细柔的嗓音说，一边静静收拢床上摊着的米娜的最后几样东西。“我们亲爱的乌尔丽卡，和我们的小爱玛。”她微笑着，走了出去，留下米丽亚姆站在那间奇妙的房间里。

“我的老天鹅（goodney）。”她低低吐出一口气，把轻轻攥紧的双拳收拢在身前。她站了片刻；觉得自己像个客人……绣花的坐便罩，擦得发亮的家具，金白两色的瓷器，花边窗帘，一张张白床，那架大屏风隔出她的三分之一……然后她一阵风似的冲上楼去——她如今是楼下平台的人了——去收拾自己的东西。

顶楼卧室门外面的平台上，立着一只硕大的柳条旅行篮；一把粗笨的雨伞，伞柄又大又疙瘩，像男人的伞，横在篮顶上，半掩着一双大大的套鞋。

等一切都在新居安顿妥当，她又累又热。

她最后环顾一圈，正撞上伊芙的照片的视线，它正从五斗橱上定定地望着她。……如今既然有了这一出，写信回家告诉她们波美拉尼亚（Pomerania）计划落了空，就容易了。

看来弗劳莱恩小姐终究是器重她的。

---

===Original===
In the schoolroom she found the *raccommodage* party gathered round the table. At its head sat Mademoiselle, her arms flung out upon the table and her face buried against them.

“Cheer up, Mademoiselle,” said Jimmie as Miriam took an empty chair between Gertrude and the Martins.

Timidly meeting Gertrude’s eye Miriam received her half smile, watched her eyebrows flicker faintly up and the little despairing shrug she gave as she went on with her mending.

“Ah, mammaz*ell*chen c’est pas mal, ne soyez triste, mein Gott mammazellchen es ist aber nichts!” chided Emma consolingly from her place near the window.

“Oh! je ne veux pas, je ne veux pas,” sobbed Mademoiselle.

No one spoke; Mademoiselle lay snuffling and shuddering. Solomon’s scissors fell on to the floor. “Mais pour*quoi* pas, Mademoiselle?” she interrogated as she recovered them.

“Pourquoi, pourquoi!” choked Mademoiselle. Her suffused little face came up for a moment towards Solomon. She met Miriam’s gaze as if she did not see her. “Vous me demandez pourquoi je ne veux pas partager ma chambre avec une femme mariée?” Her head sank again and her little grey form jerked sharply as she sobbed.

“Probably a widder, Mademoiselle,” ventured Bertha Martin, “oon voove.”

“*Verve*, Bertha,” came Millie’s correcting voice and Miriam’s interest changed to excited thoughts of Fräulein﻿—not hating her, and choosing Mademoiselle to sleep with the servant, a new servant﻿—the things on the landing﻿—Mademoiselle refusing to share a room with a married woman﻿﻿ … she felt about round this idea as Millie’s prim, clear voice went on﻿﻿ … her eyes clutched at Mademoiselle, begging to understand﻿﻿ … she gazed at the little down-flung head, fine little tendrils frilling along the edge of her hair, her little hard grey shape, all miserable and ashamed. It was dreadful. Miriam felt she could not bear it. She turned away. It was a strange new thought that anyone should object to being with a married woman﻿﻿ … would she object? or Harriett? Not unless it were suggested to them.﻿﻿ … Was there some special refinement in this French girl that none of them understood? Why should it be refined to object to share a room with a married woman? A cold shadow closed in on Miriam’s mind.

===Chinese===
教室里，她看见*缝补*聚会的人都围坐在桌边。Mademoiselle坐在上首，两条胳膊摊在桌上，脸埋在胳膊里。

“振作些，Mademoiselle。”吉米（Jimmie）说。米丽亚姆在格特鲁德和马丁家姐妹之间挑了把空椅子坐下。

怯怯地迎上格特鲁德的目光，米丽亚姆接到了她半个微笑，看着她眉毛轻轻向上一挑，耸了耸肩——那小小的、绝望的耸肩——随即继续缝补。

“啊，小*姐*小姐（mammazellchen），没什么大不了的，别难过，我的天，小姐小姐，真的没什么呀！”爱玛在窗边的座位上宽慰地嗔道。

“哦！我不要，我不要（je ne veux pas）。”Mademoiselle抽抽噎噎地哭。

没有人说话；Mademoiselle伏在那里，抽着鼻子，浑身发抖。所罗门（Solomon）的剪刀掉在地上。“可是为*什么*不要呢，Mademoiselle（Mais pour*quoi* pas, Mademoiselle?）？”她捡起剪刀，发问道。

“为什么，为什么（Pourquoi, pourquoi）！”Mademoiselle哽咽着。她那涨红的小脸朝所罗门仰起来片刻。她迎上米丽亚姆的目光，仿佛没有看见她。“您问我，为什么我不愿意和一个结了婚的女人同睡一间房（Vous me demandez pourquoi je ne veux pas partager ma chambre avec une femme mariée?）？”她的头又沉下去，小小的灰色身形随着抽泣猛地一抽一抽。

“说不定是个寡妇（widder）呢，Mademoiselle，”贝莎·马丁（Bertha Martin）壮着胆子说，“温——乌夫（oon voove）。”那已是她能念出的法语“寡妇（une veuve）”。

“是*韦尔夫*（Verve），贝莎。”传来米莉纠正的嗓音。米丽亚姆的心思转成了对弗劳莱恩小姐兴奋的种种念头——她并不恨她，却挑中Mademoiselle去和女仆同睡，一个新来的女仆——平台上那些东西——Mademoiselle拒绝同有夫之妇合住一间房……米莉那清脆古板的嗓音继续说着，她的思绪便围着这个念头四处摸索……她的眼睛紧紧抓住Mademoiselle，恳求着要明白……她凝望那颗低垂的小脑袋，细细的鬈发沿着发缘起出一圈小褶，她那小小的、硬邦邦的灰色身形，浑身凄惨又羞惭。太可怕了。米丽亚姆觉得自己受不了了。她转过脸去。居然有人反感同有夫之妇住在一起——这是个奇怪的新念头……她自己会反感吗？哈丽特呢？除非有人向她们提起，否则不会。……这个法国姑娘身上，是不是有什么她们谁都不懂的特殊的高雅？反感同有夫之妇同住一间房，为什么就是高雅呢？一道冰冷的暗影合拢在米丽亚姆的心上。

===Original===
“I don’t care,” said Millie almost quickly, with a crimson face. “It’s a special occasion. I think Mademoiselle ought to complain. If I were in her place I should write home. It’s not right. Fräulein has no right to make her sleep with a servant.”

“Why can’t the servant sleep in one of the back attics?” asked Solomon.

“Not furnished, my sweetheart,” said Gertrude, “and you know Kinder you’re all running on very fast about servants﻿—the good Frau is our housekeeper.”

“Will she have meals with us?”

“Gewiss Jimmie, meals.”

“Mon Dieu, vous êtes terribles, toutes!” came Mademoiselle’s voice. It seemed to bite into the table. “Oh, c’est grossière!” She gathered herself up and escaped into the little schoolroom.

“Armes, armes, Momzell,” wailed Ulrica gently gazing out of the window.

“Som one should go, go you, Henchen,” urged Emma.

“Don’t, for goodness’ sake, Hendy,” begged Jimmie, “not you, she’s wild about you going downstairs,” she whispered.

Miriam struggled with her gratification. “Oh go, som one; go you, Clara!”

“Better leave her alone,” ruled Gertrude.

“We miss old Minna, don’t we?” concluded Bertha.

===Chinese===
“我不在乎，”米莉（Millie）几乎是激动地说，脸涨得通红。“这是特殊情形。我认为Mademoiselle就该抱怨。我要是处在她的地位，我就写信回家。这不对。弗劳莱恩没有权利叫她跟女仆睡。”

“为什么不能让女仆睡后面顶楼的哪一间呢？”所罗门问。

“没有家具，我的小心肝，”格特鲁德说，“还有，Kinder（孩子们），你们知道吗，你们这样议论仆人，跑得太远了——好心的Frau（太太）可是我们的女管家。”

“她要跟我们一起吃饭吗？”

“Gewiss（当然），吉米，一起吃饭。”

“我的天，你们一个个都太可怕了（Mon Dieu, vous êtes terribles, toutes!）！”Mademoiselle的声音传来，像要咬进桌子似的。“哦，太粗俗了（c'est grossière）！”她撑起身子，逃进了小教室。

“可怜呀，可怜呀，Momzell（小姐）。”乌尔丽卡轻轻哀叹，眼睛望着窗外。

“得有个人去（Som one should go），你去吧，小亨迪（Henchen）。”爱玛怂恿道。

“千万别，看在老天爷份上，亨迪（Hendy），”吉米恳求道，“别你去——你一下去她就要发狂的。”她悄声说。

米丽亚姆同心里那点得意搏斗着。“哦，去吧，随便哪个；你去吧，克拉拉（Clara）！”

“最好让她一个人待着。”格特鲁德裁断道。

“咱们想老米娜了，是不是？”贝莎作结道。

---

===Original===
The heat grew intense.

The air was more and more oppressive as the day went on.

Clara fainted suddenly just after dinner, and Fräulein, holding a little discourse on clothing and an enquiry into wardrobes, gave a general permission for the reduction of garments to the minimum and sent everyone to rest uncorseted until teatime, promising a walk to the woods in the cool of the evening. There was a sense of adventure in the house. It was as if it were being besieged. It gave Miriam confidence to approach Fräulein for permission to rearrange her trunk in the basement. She let Fräulein understand that her removal was not complete, that there were things to do before she could be properly settled in her new room.

“Certainly, Miss Henderson, you are quite free,” said Fräulein instantly as the girls trooped upstairs.

Miriam knew she wanted to avoid an afternoon shut up with Emma and Ulrica and she did not in the least want to lie down. It seemed to her a very extraordinary thing to do. It surprised and disturbed her. It suggested illness and weakness. She could not remember having lain down in the daytime. There had been that fortnight in the old room at home with Harriett﻿﻿ … chickenpox and new books coming and games, and Sarah reading the *Song of Hiawatha* and their being allowed to choose their pudding. She could not remember feeling ill. Had she ever felt ill?﻿﻿ … Colds and bilious attacks.﻿﻿ …

She remembered with triumph a group of days of pain two years ago. She had forgotten.﻿﻿ … Bewilderment and pain﻿﻿ … her mother’s constant presence﻿﻿ … everything, the light everywhere, the leaves standing out along the tops of hedgerows as she drove with her mother, telling her of pain and she alone in the midst of it﻿﻿ … for always﻿﻿ … pride, long moments of deep pride.﻿﻿ … Eve and Sarah congratulating her, Eve stupid and laughing﻿﻿ … the new bearing of the servants﻿﻿ … Lilla Belton’s horrible talks fading away to nothing.

===Chinese===
热浪灼人，越来越烈。

随着白昼推移，空气越来越沉闷。

克拉拉刚吃过午饭就突然晕倒了。弗劳莱恩小姐就衣着发表了一小篇训诫，又查问了一遍各人的衣橱，随即格外开恩，准许大家把衣服减到最少，解开束胸去歇息到茶时分，并许诺傍晚凉快时到树林里散步。宅子里有一种历险的气氛，仿佛正被围困着。这给了米丽亚姆底气，去求弗劳莱恩小姐允许她到地下室重新归置她的大衣箱。她让弗劳莱恩小姐明白，她的搬迁还没有完成，要在新房间里安顿妥当，还有些事情要做。

“当然可以，亨德森小姐，您完全自由。”姑娘们成群上楼时，弗劳莱恩小姐当即说。

米丽亚姆知道，自己是不想被关在楼上同爱玛和乌尔丽卡一起过一下午，她也丝毫不想躺下。白天躺下这件事，在她看来非常离奇。它让她又惊讶又不安。它暗示着疾病和软弱。她不记得自己白天躺下过。只有在家里那间老房间里、同哈丽特一起的那两个星期……水痘，新到的书和游戏，萨拉念《海华沙之歌》（*Song of Hiawatha*），还准许她们自己挑布丁。她不记得觉得难受过。她可曾觉得难受过？……伤风，还有胆汁发作。……

她得意地记起两年前的一段疼痛的日子。她竟忘了。……惶惑与疼痛……母亲寸步不离……一切的一切，到处都是光，她同母亲坐车出行时，树篱顶上一排排凸现的叶子，母亲对她讲说着疼痛，而她独自在这一切中间……永永远远……骄傲，一段又一段深深的骄傲。……伊芙和萨拉向她道贺，伊芙傻乎乎地笑着……仆人们换了新的态度……莉拉·贝尔顿（Lilla Belton）那些可怕的谈话淡出成了无。

===Original===
Fräulein had left her and gone to her room. Every door and window on the ground floor stood wide excepting that leading to Fräulein’s little double rooms. She wondered what the rooms were like and felt sorry for Fräulein, tall and gaunt, moving about in them alone, alone with her own dark eyes, curtains hanging motionless at the windows﻿﻿ … was it really bad to tight-lace? The English girls, except Millie and Solomon all had small waists. She wished she knew. She placed her large hands round her waist. Drawing in her breath she could almost make them meet. It was easier to play tennis with stays﻿﻿ … how dusty the garden looked, baked. She wanted to go out with two heavy watering cans, to feel them pulling her arms from their sockets, dragging her shoulders down, throwing out her chest, to spray canful after canful through a great wide rose, sprinkling her ankles sometimes, and to grow so warm that she would not feel the heat. Bella Lyndon had never worn stays; playing rounders so splendidly, lying on the grass between the games with her arms under her head﻿﻿ … simply disgusting, someone had said﻿﻿ … who﻿﻿ … a disgusted face﻿﻿ … nearly all the girls detested Bella.

Going through the hall on her way down to the basement she heard the English voices sounding quietly out into the afternoon from the rooms above. Flat and tranquil they sounded, Bertha and Jimmie she heard, Gertrude’s undertones, quiet words from Millie. She felt she would like a corner in the English room for the afternoon, a book and an occasional remark﻿—“Mr. Barnes of New York”﻿—she would not be able to read her three yellowbacks in the German bedroom. She felt at the moment glad to be robbed of them. It would be much better, of course. There was no sound from the German rooms. She pictured sleeping faces. It was cooler in the basement﻿—but even there the air seemed stiff and dusty with the heat.

Why did the hanging garments remind her of All Saints’ Church and Mr. Brough?﻿﻿ … she must tell Harriett that in her letter﻿﻿ … that day they suddenly decided to help in the church decorations﻿﻿ … she remembered the smell of the soot on the holly as they had cut and hacked at it in the cold garden, and Harriett overturning the heavy wheelbarrow on the way to church, and how they had not laughed because they both felt solemn, and then there had just been the three Anwyl girls and Mrs. Anwyl and Mrs. Scarr and Mr. Brough in the church room all being silly about Birdy Anwyl roasting chestnuts, and how silly and affected they were when a piece of holly stuck in her skirt.

===Chinese===
弗劳莱恩小姐已经离开她，回自己房间去了。底层的每一扇门、每一扇窗都敞得大开，只有通往弗劳莱恩小姐那两小间套房的那扇例外。她不知道那几间房是什么样子，并为弗劳莱恩小姐难过起来——又高又瘦削，独自在里面走动，独自守着她那双黑眼睛，窗帘一动不动垂在窗前……把腰束得紧紧的，真的就那么坏吗？英国姑娘们，除了米莉和所罗门，个个都有纤细的腰身。她但愿自己知道。她把两只大手围住腰。吸足一口气，两只手差不多能合拢。打网球穿着紧身褡（stays）反倒容易些……花园看上去多么尘土飞扬，都烤干了。她想提着两只沉重的洒水桶出去，去感觉它们把她的胳膊从肩窝里往下坠，把她的肩膀往下拽，把她的胸膛挺出来，用一朵大大的宽喷头一桶接一桶地喷洒，不时溅湿脚踝，一直干到浑身发热、感觉不到暑气为止。贝拉·林登（Bella Lyndon）从来没穿过紧身褡；圆场球打得那么漂亮，局间躺在草地上，双臂枕在头下……“简直恶心”，有人说过……是谁……一张厌恶的脸……几乎所有的女孩都恨贝拉。

穿过门厅下地下室去的时候，她听见英语的嗓音从楼上的房间里静静淌进午后的空气。听上去平平的、安安静静的，她听出有贝莎和吉米，有格特鲁德的低音，有米莉的静静的话语。她觉得这个下午自己愿意在英语房间里占一个角落，一本书，偶尔一句话——《纽约的巴恩斯先生》（Mr. Barnes of New York）——她那三本黄皮小说（yellowback）在德国人的卧室里是读不成了。此刻她倒觉得被剥夺了它们是件快活事。那当然好得多。德国人的房间里没有一点声音。她想象着一张张睡熟的脸。地下室里凉快些——可就连那里的空气，似乎也被热气泡得又僵又尘。

为什么那些悬挂着的衣裳让她想起诸圣堂（All Saints' Church）和布拉夫先生（Mr. Brough）？……写信时一定要告诉哈丽特这件事……那一天，她们临时起意去帮忙装点教堂……她记得在冷冰冰的花园里，她们又剪又砍冬青时冬青上煤灰的气味，哈丽特在去教堂的路上掀翻了沉重的独轮车，而她们都没有笑，因为两人都觉得庄重，还有教堂活动室里，当时只有安威尔家三姐妹、安威尔太太（Mrs. Anwyl）、斯卡尔太太（Mrs. Scarr）和布拉夫先生，围着烤栗子的伯蒂·安威尔（Birdy Anwyl）傻闹个不停，还有当一小枝冬青粘在她裙子上时，他们一个个多么傻气、多么做作。

---

===Original===
Coming up the basement stairs in response to the tea gong, Miriam thought there were visitors in the hall and hesitated; then there was Pastor Lahmann’s profile disappearing towards the door and Fräulein patting and dismissing two of his boys. His face looked white and clear and firm and undisturbed, Miriam wanted to arrest him and ask him something﻿—what he thought of the weather﻿—he looked so different from her memory of him in the *Saal* two Saturdays ago﻿—two weeks﻿—four classes she must have missed. Why? Why was she missing Pastor Lahmann’s classes? How had it happened? Perhaps she would see him in class again. Perhaps next week.﻿﻿ …

The other visitors proved to be the Bergmanns in new dresses. Miriam gazed at Clara as she went down the schoolroom to her corner of the table. She looked like﻿﻿ … a hostess. It seemed absurd to see her sit down to tea as a schoolgirl. The dress was a fine black muslin stamped all over with tiny fish-shaped patches of mauve. It was cut to the base of the neck and came to a point in front where the soft white ruching was fastened with a large cameo brooch. Clara’s pallid worried face had grown more placid during the hot inactive days, and today her hard mouth looked patient and determined and responsible. She seemed quite independent of her surroundings. Miriam found herself again and again consulting her calm face. Her presence haunted Miriam throughout teatime. Emma was sweet, pink and bright after her rest in a bright light brown muslin dress dotted with white spots.﻿﻿ …

Funny German dresses, thought Miriam, funny﻿﻿ … and old. Her mind hovered and wondered over these German dresses﻿—did she like them or not﻿—something about them﻿—she glanced at Elsa, sitting opposite in the dull faint electric blue with black lace sleeves she had worn since the warm weather set in. Even Ulrica, thin and straight now﻿﻿ … like a pole﻿﻿ … in a tight flat dress of saffron muslin sprigged with brown leaves, seemed to be included in something that made all these German dresses utterly different from anything the English girls could have worn. What was it? It was crowned by the Bergmanns’ dresses. It had begun in a summer dress of Minna’s, black with a tiny sky-blue spot and a heavy ruche round the hem. She thought she liked it. It seemed to set the full tide of summer round the table more than the things of the English girls﻿—and yet the dresses were ugly﻿—and the English girls’ dresses were not that﻿﻿ … they were nothing﻿﻿ … plain cottons and zephyrs with lace tuckers﻿—no ruches. It was something﻿﻿ … somehow in the ruches﻿—the ruches and the little peaks of neck.

A faint scent of camphor came from the Martins across the way, sitting in their cool creased black-and-white check cotton dresses. They still kept to their hard white collars and cuffs. As tea went on Miriam found her eyes drawn back and back again to these newly unpacked camphor-scented dresses﻿﻿ … and when conversation broke after moments of stillness﻿﻿ … shadowy foliage﻿﻿ … the still hot garden﻿﻿ … the sunbaked wooden room beyond the sunny *Saal*, the light pouring through three rooms and bright along the table﻿﻿ … it was to the Martins’ check dresses that she glanced.

===Chinese===
听到吃茶的锣声上地下室楼梯来，米丽亚姆以为门厅里有客人，迟疑了一下；随即看见拉曼牧师（Pastor Lahmann）的侧影正朝门口隐去，弗劳莱恩小姐正拍拍他的两个男孩，打发他们走。他的脸看上去白净、清晰、坚定、安详，米丽亚姆想拦住他，问他点什么——问他觉得这天气如何——他同她记忆里两个星期六以前的他——在*大厅*里的他——判若两人。两个星期——她准是误了四堂课了。为什么？她为什么一直在误拉曼牧师的课？这是怎么发生的？也许她还会在课堂上见到他。也许下个星期。……

原来另一位客人是贝格曼家姊妹，穿着新衣裳。米丽亚姆朝教室、朝自己那一角桌边走去时，直盯着克拉拉看。她看上去像……一位女主人。看她以女学生的身份坐下吃茶，简直荒唐。那件衣裳是精致的黑色细麻纱，满身印着一个个鱼形的淡紫小斑。领口开到颈根，前面收成一个尖，柔软的白色褶边在那里用一枚大大的浮雕玉石胸针别住。克拉拉那张苍白忧烦的脸，在这些火热的、无所事事的日子里变得平静了些，今天，她那线条硬朗的嘴透出耐心、决断和担当。她看上去全然不为其周遭所动。米丽亚姆发现自己一次又一次去求教她那张沉静的脸。整个吃茶时间，她的存在都萦绕着米丽亚姆。爱玛甜美，歇过之后粉扑扑、亮莹莹的，穿一条明亮的浅棕色细麻纱裙，缀着白色的点子。……

有趣的德国裙子，米丽亚姆想，有趣……而且老气。她的心思在这些德国裙子上盘旋、琢磨——她喜欢还是不喜欢——它们身上有某种东西——她瞥了一眼埃尔莎，她坐在对面，穿着暖天一来就一直穿在身的那件暗淡的电光蓝，配黑花边袖子。就连乌尔丽卡，如今又瘦又直……像根杆子……穿一身平贴的紧身藏红花色细麻纱裙，缀着棕色的叶片碎花，似乎也被囊括进某种东西里，正是它使这些德国裙子同英国姑娘们可能穿的任何衣服截然不同。那是什么呢？它在贝格曼家姊妹的裙子上达到了极致。它最初显现在米娜的一条夏裙上，黑色，细小的天蓝点子，下摆一圈厚厚的褶饰边（ruche）。她觉得自己喜欢它。它似乎比英国姑娘们的衣着，更把夏天的满潮围坐到了桌边——然而这些裙子是难看的——而英国姑娘们的裙子倒不是难看……它们什么也算不上……素棉布和细斜纹薄布（zephyr），配蕾丝胸褶——没有褶饰边。那是某种东西……不知怎么就在那些褶饰边里——褶饰边，还有领口的一个个小尖。

马丁家姊妹坐在对面，一身凉爽的、带着折痕的黑白格棉布裙，散出一股淡淡的樟脑味。她们仍坚持戴着硬挺的白领子和白袖口。吃茶期间，米丽亚姆发现自己的目光一次又一次被拉回这些新近启封的、带着樟脑味的裙子……而当静默片刻后谈话中断时……荫翳的叶丛……静止的、灼热的花园……向阳的*大厅*那头那间被晒透的木房，光漫过三个房间涌进来，亮亮地铺在桌上……她瞥的，总是马丁家姊妹的格子裙。

===Original===
It was intensely hot, but the strain had gone out of the day; the feeling of just bearing up against the heat and getting through the day had gone; they all sat round﻿﻿ … which was which?﻿﻿ … Miriam met eye after eye﻿—how beautiful they all were looking out from faces and meeting hers﻿—and her eyes came back unembarrassed to her cup, her solid *Butterbrot* and the sunlit angle of the garden wall and the bit of tree just over Fräulein Pfaff’s shoulder. She tried to meet Mademoiselle’s eyes, she felt sure their eyes could meet. She wondered intensely what was in Elsa’s mind behind her faint hard blue dress. She wanted to hear Mademoiselle’s voice; Mademoiselle was almost invisible in her corner near the door, the new housekeeper was sitting at her side very upright and close to the table. Once or twice she felt Fräulein’s look; she sustained it, and glowed happily under it without meeting it; she referred back contentedly to it after hearing herself laugh out once﻿—just as she would do at home; once or twice she forgot for a moment where she was. The way the light shone on the housekeeper’s hair, bright brown and plastered flatly down on either side of her bright white-and-crimson face, and the curves of her chocolate and white striped cotton bodice, reminded her sharply of something she had seen once, something that had charmed her﻿﻿ … it was in the hair against the hard white of the forehead and the flat broad cheeks with the hard, clear crimson colouring nearly covering them﻿﻿ … something in the way she sat, standing out against the others.﻿﻿ … Judy on her left hand with almost the same colouring looked small and gentle and refined.

===Chinese===
天气灼热，可这一天的紧绷已经散了；那种顶着暑气硬撑、只求把一天熬过去的劲儿没有了；大家围坐着……谁是谁呢？……米丽亚姆一双双眼睛迎过去——从一张张面孔里望出来、迎上她的目光，多么美啊——而她的眼睛坦然地回到她的茶杯、她那厚实的黄油面包（*Butterbrot*）上，回到花园墙被阳光照亮的一角，回到普法夫小姐肩头露出的一角树影。她想迎上Mademoiselle的眼睛，她确信她们的目光能够相遇。她无比好奇，埃尔莎那件暗淡硬挺的蓝裙后面，脑子里装着什么。她想听见Mademoiselle的声音；Mademoiselle在门边她的角落里几乎看不见了，新来的女管家坐在她身旁，腰板笔直，紧挨着桌沿。有一两回她觉出弗劳莱恩小姐的目光；她承受住了，并在那目光下幸福地发热，并不迎上去；有一回她听见自己放声笑了出来，过后又心满意足地回想起那道目光——就像在家里那样；有一两回她一时忘了自己身在何处。光照在女管家头发上的样子——明亮的棕色，平平地贴伏在她那白里透红、亮堂堂的脸庞两侧——还有她那件巧克力色白条纹棉布紧身胸衣的曲线，猛地让米丽亚姆想起某一次见过的什么东西，某件曾让她着迷的东西……是在那贴着坚硬白额头、平而宽的面颊上的头发里，那种硬净的绯红色几乎盖满了双颊……还有她坐着的姿态，从旁人中间凸显出来。……她左手边的朱迪（Judy），几乎是同样的配色，看上去小巧、温雅、精致。

---

===Original===
Tea was over. Fräulein decided against a walk and they all trooped into the *Saal*. No programme was suggested; they all sat about unoccupied. There was no centre; Fräulein Pfaff was one of them. The little group near her in the shady half of the sunlit summerhouse was as quietly easy as those who sat far back in the *Saal*. Miriam had got into a low chair near the *Saal* doors whence she could see across the room through the summerhouse window through the gap between the houses across the way to the far-off afternoon country. Its colours gleamed, a soft confusion of tones, under the heat haze. For a while she sat with her eyes on Fräulein’s thin profile, clean and cool and dry in the intense heat﻿﻿ … “she must be looking out towards the lime trees.”﻿﻿ … Ulrica sat drooped on a low chair near her knees﻿﻿ … “sweet beautiful head”﻿﻿ … the weight of her soft curved mouth seemed too much for the delicate angles of her face and it drooped faintly, breaking their sharp lines. Miriam wished all the world could see her.﻿﻿ … Presently Ulrica raised her head, as Elsa and Clara broke into words and laughter near her, and her drooping lips flattened gently back into their place in the curve of her face. She gazed out through the doorway of the summerhouse with her great despairing eyes﻿﻿ … the housekeeper was rather like a Dutch doll﻿﻿ … but that was not it.

===Chinese===
茶吃完了。弗劳莱恩小姐决定不散步了，大家便都涌进*大厅*。没有提议任何节目；大家无所事事地各处坐着。没有中心；普法夫小姐只是她们中的一个。在她近旁、那向阳花园棚屋背阴半边的一小簇人，同坐在*大厅*深远处的那些人一样安然自在。米丽亚姆坐进了*大厅*门边的一把矮椅，从这里她能越过房间，透过花园棚屋的窗子，透过对面房屋之间的豁口，望到遥远的午后的乡野。在暑气的薄霭下面，那些色彩闪烁着，一片柔柔的、音调纷然的迷离。有一阵子，她坐在那里望着弗劳莱恩小姐清瘦的侧影，在这灼热里洁净、凉爽、干燥……“她一定是在朝椴树那边望。”……乌尔丽卡萎坐在她膝边的一把矮椅上……“甜美的、美丽的头颅”……她那柔软弯曲的嘴唇的分量，似乎是她脸上那些纤秀棱角承受不动的，微微垂着，折断了它们锐利的线条。米丽亚姆巴不得全世界都能看看她。……不一会儿，埃尔莎和克拉拉在她近旁爆出了话语和笑声，乌尔丽卡抬起头来，低垂的双唇轻轻抿平，回到脸上原有的弧线里。她睁着大大的、绝望的眼睛，凝望花园棚屋门外的远处……女管家倒有几分像一只荷兰玩偶……可那又不全是。

---

===Original===
The sun had set. Miriam had found a little thin volume of German poetry in her pocket. She sat fumbling the leaves. She felt the touch of her limp straightening hair upon her forehead. It did not matter. Twilight would soon come, and bedtime. But it must have been beginning to get like that at teatime. Perhaps the weather would get even hotter. She must do something about her hair﻿﻿ … if only she could wear it turned straight back.

There was a stirring in the room; beautiful forms rose and stood and spoke and moved about. Someone went to the door. It opened gently with a peaceful sound on to the quiet hall and footsteps ran upstairs. Two figures going out from the *Saal* passed in front of the two still sitting quietly grouped in the light of the summerhouse. They were challenged as they passed and turned soft profiles and stood talking. Behind the voices﻿—flutings, single notes, broken phrases, long undisturbed warblings came from the garden.

Clara was at the piano. Tall behind her stood Millie’s gracious shapeless baby form.

===Chinese===
太阳落了。米丽亚姆在口袋里摸到一小册薄薄的德语诗集。她坐在那里捻着书页。她感觉到自己那疲软着慢慢直起来的头发拂在额头上。不要紧。暮色很快就会来，还有就寝的时辰。可吃茶的时候，一定就已经开始变成这样了。也许天气还会更热。她得把头发想点办法……要是能把头发全部向后拢直了梳，该多好。

房间里起了一阵窸窣；美丽的身影站起来，立定，说话，走动。有人走到门边。门带着安谧的声响轻轻开向静静的门厅，脚步声奔上楼去。两个身影从*大厅*里往外走，经过那还静静围坐在花园棚屋光线里的两个人面前。她们经过时被喝问了一句，便转过柔和的侧影，站住说话。人语背后——花园里传来笛鸣似的啁啾、单音、断句，还有长长的、不受惊扰的啼啭。

克拉拉坐在钢琴前。她身后高高立着米莉那雍容的、没个形状的小娃娃般的身形。

===Original===
As Millie’s voice climbing carefully up and down the even stages of Solveig’s song reached the second verse, Miriam tried to separate the music from the words. The words were wrong. She half saw a fair woman with a great crown of plaited hair and very broad shoulders singing the song in the Hanover concert room in Norwegian. She remembered the moment of taking her eyes away from the singer and the platform, and feeling the crowded room and the airlessness, and then the song going steadily on from note to note as she listened﻿﻿ … no trills and no tune﻿﻿ … saying something. It stood in the air. All the audience were saying it. And then the fair-haired woman had sung the second verse as though it was something about herself﻿—tragically﻿﻿ … tragic muse.﻿﻿ … It was not *her* song, standing there in the velvet dress.﻿﻿ … She stopped it from going on. There was nothing but the movement of the lace round her shoulders and chest, her expanded neck, quivering, and the pressure in her voice.﻿﻿ … And then there had been Herr Bossenberger, hammering and shouting it out in the *Saal* with Millie, and everything in the schoolroom, even the dust on the paper rack, standing out clearer and clearer as he bellowed slowly along. And then she had got to know that everybody knew about it; it was a famous song. There were people singing it everywhere in German and French and English﻿—a girl singing about her lover.﻿﻿ … It was not that; even if people sang it like that, if a real girl had ever sung something like that, that was not what she meant﻿﻿ … “the winter may pass”﻿﻿ … yes, that was all right﻿—and mountains with green slopes and narrow torrents﻿—and a voice going strongly out and ceasing, and all the sky filled with the sound﻿—and the song going on, walking along, thinking to itself.﻿﻿ … She looked about as Millie’s voice ceased trembling on the last high note. She hoped no one would hum the refrain. There was no one there who knew anything about it.﻿﻿ … Judy? Judy knew, perhaps. Judy would never hum or sing anything. If she did, it would be terrible. She knew so much. Perhaps Judy knew everything. She was sitting on the low sill of the window behind the piano sewing steel beads on to a shot silk waistband held very close to her eyes. Minna could. Minna might be sitting in her plaid dress on the window seat with her embroidery, her smooth hair polished with bay rum humming Solveig’s song.

The housekeeper brought in the milk and rolls and went away downstairs again. The cold milk was very refreshing but the room grew stifling as they all sat round near the little centre table with the French window nearly closed, shutting off the summerhouse and garden. Everybody in turn seemed to be saying “Ik kenne meine Tasse sie ist svatz.” Bertha had begun it, holding up her white glass of milk as she took it from the tray and exactly imitating the housekeeper’s voice.

“Platt Deutsch spricht sie, ja?” Clara had said. It seemed as if there were no more to be said about the housekeeper. At prayers when they were all saying “Vater unser,” she heard Jimmie murmur, “Ik kenne meine Tasse.”

===Chinese===
米莉的嗓音沿着索尔维格之歌（Solveig's song）均匀的音级小心翼翼地爬上爬下，唱到第二段时，米丽亚姆试着把音乐从歌词里剥出来。歌词是错的。她仿佛看见一个金发女人，顶着一大冠辫成的发髻，肩膀很宽很宽，在汉诺威的音乐厅里用挪威语唱着这支歌。她记得自己把目光从歌者和台上移开的那一刻，感觉到拥挤的厅堂和沉闷的空气，然后那支歌仍然一音一音稳稳地唱下去，她听着……没有颤音，也没有旋律……在诉说着什么。它立在空气里。满座的听众都在诉说它。然后那金发女人唱起了第二段，仿佛唱的是她自己的什么事——悲怆地……悲怆的缪斯。……这不是*她*的歌——那个穿着天鹅绒裙子站在那里的人。……她拦住了它，不让它继续。剩下的只有她肩头和胸前花边的动，她绷起的颈项，颤抖着，还有她声音里的压迫。……再后来就是波森贝格先生（Herr Bossenberger），在*大厅*里同米莉一起把它敲打出来、吼出来，教室里的一切，连纸架上的灰尘，都随着他缓缓的吼唱越来越清晰、越来越清晰地凸现出来。再后来她知道了，原来人人都知道这支歌；它是一支名曲。处处有人用德语、法语、英语唱它——一个姑娘唱着她的爱人。……不是那样的；即便人们那样唱，倘若真有一个姑娘曾那样唱过什么，那也不是她的意思……“冬天也许会过去”……对，这没问题——还有绿坡的群山、狭窄的急流——一个嗓音有力地传出去，然后止息，满天空都盛满了那声音——而那支歌继续着，一路走，一路想它自己的心事。……米莉的声音在最高一个音符上颤着止息时，她四下里看了看。她希望没有人会哼那副歌。这里没有人真正懂得它。……朱迪？朱迪也许懂。朱迪从不哼也不唱任何东西。她要是哼了唱了，那可不得了。她懂得那么多。也许朱迪什么都懂。她正坐在钢琴后面那扇窗的矮窗台上，往一条闪色绸（shot silk）腰带上缝钢珠，那带子凑得离她的眼睛近极了。米娜会的。米娜这会儿说不定穿着她的格呢裙坐在窗座上做刺绣，用月桂朗姆发水（bay rum）抿得油光水滑的头发，哼着索尔维格之歌。

女管家端进来牛奶和小面包，又下楼去了。冷牛奶很解乏，可当大家围坐在小圆桌旁、法式窗几乎关严、把花园棚屋和花园都隔在外面时，房间里越来越闷。人人都轮流说起了“Ik kenne meine Tasse sie ist svatz（我认得我的杯子，它是黑的）。”这句是贝莎起的头，她从托盘上接过那杯盛在白玻璃杯里的牛奶，举起来，把女管家的嗓音模仿得一丝不差。

“她讲的是低地德语（Platt Deutsch），对吧（ja）？”克拉拉当时说。关于女管家，似乎再没有什么可说的了。晚祷时大家正念着“Vater unser（我们在天上的父）”，她听见吉米咕哝了一句“Ik kenne meine Tasse（我认得我的杯子）。”

---

===Original===
Fräulein Pfaff came upstairs behind the girls and ordered silence as they went to their rooms. “Hear, all, children,” she said in German in the quiet clear even tone with which she had just read prayers, “no one to speak to her neighbour, no one to whisper or bustle, nor tonight to brush her hair, but each to compose her mind and go quietly to her rest. Thus acting the so great heat shall injure none of us and peaceful sleep will come. Do you hear, children?”

Answering voices came from the bedrooms. She entered each room, shifting screens, opening each window for a few moments, leaving each door wide.

“Each her little corner,” she said in Miriam’s room, “fresh water set for the morning. The heavens are all round us, my little ones; have no fear.”

Gently sighing and moaning Ulrica moved about in her corner. Emma dropped a slipper and muttered consolingly. Thankfully Miriam listened to Fräulein’s short, deprecating footsteps pacing up and down the landing. She was safe from the dreadful challenge of conversation with her pupils. She felt hemmed in in the stifling room with the landing full of girls all round her. She wanted to push away her screen, push up the hot white ceiling. She wished she could be safely upstairs with Mademoiselle and the height of the candle-lit garret above her head. It could not possibly be hotter up there than in this stifling room with its draperies and furniture and gas.

Fräulein came in very soon and turned out the light with a formal good night greeting. For a while after all the lights were out, she continued pacing up and down.

Across the landing someone began to sneeze rapidly sneeze after sneeze. “Ach, die Millie!” muttered Emma sleepily. For several minutes the sneezing went on. Sighs and impatient movements sounded here and there. “Ruhig, Kinder, ruhig. Millie shall soon sleep peacefully as all.”

===Chinese===
弗劳莱恩小姐跟在姑娘们身后上楼来，在大家走向各自房间时命令肃静。“都听着，孩子们，”她用刚才诵读祷文的那种安静、清亮、平匀的语调用德语说，“谁也不要同邻铺说话，谁也不要窃窃私语、窸窸窣窣，今晚谁也不要梳头，各人只管收敛心神，安安静静地去休息。这样，这般大热便伤不着我们任何人，安睡自会来临。听见了么，孩子们？”

一间间卧室里传出应答的声音。她走进每个房间，挪动屏风，把每扇窗敞开片刻，让每扇门大敞着。

“各人有各人的小角落，”她在米丽亚姆的房间里说，“清水已经为早晨备好。诸天四围环绕着我们，我的小家伙们；不要害怕。”

乌尔丽卡在她那角落里轻轻地叹息、呜咽着走动。爱玛掉了一只拖鞋，含含糊糊地说着宽慰的话。米丽亚姆心怀感激地听着弗劳莱恩小姐那短短的、谦抑的脚步声在平台上踱来踱去。她算是躲开了同学生们交谈的那可怕的应酬。可她在这闷窒的房间里，被四周围满平台的姑娘们围困着。她想推开她的屏风，想把滚烫的白色天花板顶上去。她但愿自己能安稳地在楼上，同Mademoiselle在一起，头顶上是烛光照亮的顶楼。那里不可能比这间塞满帷幕、家具和煤气的闷房更热。

弗劳莱恩小姐很快就进来，带着一声例行的晚安问候熄了灯。所有的灯都熄了之后，她还踱了一会儿。

平台那头有人开始打喷嚏，一个接一个，喷嚏连着喷嚏。“Ach（啊），这个米莉！”爱玛睡意朦胧地咕哝。喷嚏接连打了好几分钟。四处响起叹息和不耐烦的翻动。“Ruhig（安静），Kinder（孩子们），ruhig。米莉很快就会像大家一样安然入睡。”

---

===Original===
Miriam could not remember hearing Fräulein Pfaff go away when she woke in the darkness feeling unendurably oppressed. She flung her sheet aside and turned her pillow over and pushed her frilled sleeves to her elbows. How energetic I am, she thought and lay tranquil. There was not a sound. “I shall never be able to sleep down here, it’s too awful,” she murmured, and puffed and shifted her head on the pillow.

The win‑ter may﻿—pass.﻿﻿ … The win‑ter﻿﻿ … may pass. The winter may﻿﻿ … pass. The Academy﻿﻿ … a picture in very bright colours﻿﻿ … a woman sitting by the roadside with a shawl round her shoulders and a red skirt and red cheeks and bright green country behind her﻿﻿ … people moving about on the shiny floor, someone just behind saying, “that is plein-air, these are the plein-airistes”﻿—the woman in the picture was like the housekeeper.﻿﻿ …

A brilliant light flashed into the room﻿﻿ … lightning﻿—how strange the room looked﻿—the screens had been moved﻿—the walls and corners and little beds had looked like daylight. Someone was talking across the landing. Emma was awake. Another flash came and movements and cries. Emma screamed aloud, sitting up in bed. “Ach Gott! Clara! *Clara!*” she screamed. Cries came from the next room. A match was struck across the landing and voices sounded. Gertrude was in the room lighting the gas and Clara tugging down the blind. Emma was sitting with her hands pressed to her eyes, quickly gasping, “Ach Clara! Mein Gott! Ach Gott!” On Ulrica’s bed nothing was visible but a mound of bedclothes. The whole landing was astir. Fräulein’s voice called up urgently from below.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆不记得听到过普法夫小姐离开，当她在这黑暗里醒来时，只觉得闷得无法忍受。她掀开被单，把枕头翻了个面，把带褶边的袖子推到肘弯。我好有劲啊，她想，随即静静躺下。一点声音也没有。“在这儿楼下我永远也睡不着，太难受了。”她喃喃着，喘着气，在枕头上挪动着头。

冬——天也许会——过去。……冬——天……也许会过去。冬天也许……会过去。皇家艺术院（the Academy）……一幅色彩极鲜亮的画……一个妇人坐在路边，肩上披着披巾，红裙子，红脸颊，身后是鲜绿的乡野……人们在亮闪闪的地板上走动，身后有人说，“这就是外光派（plein-air），这些是外光派画家（plein-airistes）”——画里那个妇人像极了女管家。……

一道亮得刺眼的光闪进房间……闪电——这房间看上去多么陌生——屏风挪动过了——墙壁、角落和一张张小床看上去如同白昼。有人在平台那头说话。爱玛醒了。又是一道闪，随之而来的是骚动和喊叫。爱玛在床上坐起来放声尖叫。“Ach Gott（我的天）！克拉拉！*克拉拉*！”她尖叫着。隔壁房间传来哭喊。平台那头划着一根火柴，人声响起。格特鲁德进屋来点上煤气灯，克拉拉在拽下百叶窗。爱玛坐着，双手捂住眼睛，急促地喘着：“Ach Clara！Mein Gott! Ach Gott!（啊克拉拉！我的天！我的天！）”乌尔丽卡床上只看得见一堆隆起的被褥。整个平台都骚动起来。弗劳莱恩小姐的声音从楼下急迫地传上来。

---

===Original===
Miriam was the last to reach the schoolroom. The girls were drawn up on either side of the gaslit room﻿—leaving the shuttered windows clear. She moved to take a chair at the end of the table in front of the *Saal* doors. “Na!” said Fräulein sharply from the sofa corner. “Not there! In full current!” Her voice shook. Miriam drew the chair to the end of the row of figures and sat down next to Solomon Martin. The wind rushed through the garden, the thunder rattled across the sky. “Oh, Clara! Fräulein! Nein!” gasped Emma. She was sitting opposite, between Clara and Jimmie with flushed face and eyes strained wide, twisting her linked hands against her knees. Jimmie patted her wrist, “It’s all right, Emmchen,” she muttered cheerfully. “Nein, Christina!” jerked Fräulein sharply. “I will not have that! To touch the flesh! You understand, all! That you know. All! Such immodesty!”

Miriam leaned forward and glanced. Fräulein was sitting very upright on the sofa in a shapeless black cloak with her hands clasped on her breast. Near her was Ulrica in her trailing white dressing gown, her face pressed against the back of the sofa. In the far corner, the other side of Fräulein sat Gertrude in her grey ulster, her knees comfortably crossed, a quilted scarlet silk bedroom slipper sticking out under the hem of her ulster.

The thunder crashed and pounded just above them. Everyone started and exclaimed. Emma flung her arms up across her face and sat back in her chair with a hooting cry. From the sofa came a hidden sobbing and gasping. “Ach Himmel! Ach Herr *Jé*-sus! Ach du *lie*-ber, *lie*-ber Gott!”

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆最后一个到达教室。姑娘们在煤气灯照亮的房间两侧排开——把关着百叶窗的窗户让出来。她挪过去，想在*大厅*门前桌端的一把椅子上坐下。“Na（呐）！”弗劳莱恩小姐从沙发角上厉声说。“不许坐那儿！正对着穿堂风！”她的嗓音发着颤。米丽亚姆把椅子拉到那排人影的末端，挨着所罗门·马丁坐下。风卷过花园，雷声在天空里辘辘滚过。“哦，克拉拉！弗劳莱恩！Nein（不）！”爱玛喘着气说。她坐在对面，在克拉拉和吉米之间，满脸通红，眼睛瞪得老大，绞着的双手抵着膝盖拧来拧去。吉米拍拍她的手腕。“没事的，小爱玛（Emmchen）。”她轻快地低声说。“Nein，克里斯蒂娜（Christina）！”弗劳莱恩小姐厉声打断。“我不容许这样！碰触肉体！你们都明白！都要记着。所有人！这般不知羞耻！”

米丽亚姆探身瞥了一眼。弗劳莱恩小姐笔直地坐在沙发上，裹着一件没个形状的黑斗篷，双手交叠在胸前。她近旁是乌尔丽卡，拖着曳地的白色晨衣，脸抵在沙发背上。远处的角落里、弗劳莱恩小姐的另一边，坐着格特鲁德，穿着她那件灰色的乌尔斯特长外套（ulster），膝盖惬意地翘着，外套下摆底下露出一只绗缝的猩红绸卧室拖鞋。

雷就在头顶上炸响、捶击。人人都一惊一乍。爱玛把双臂甩起来挡住脸，向后跌进椅子里，发出一声呜呜的哭喊。沙发那边传来掩不住的抽噎和喘咽。“Ach Himmel（啊天哪）！Ach Herr *Jé*-sus（啊主*耶*——稣）！Ach du *lie*-ber, *lie*-ber Gott（啊，亲*爱*的、亲*爱*的上帝）！”

===Original===
Miriam wished they could see the lightning and be prepared for the crashes. If she were alone she would watch for the flashes and put her fingers in her ears after each flash. The shock of the sound was intolerable to her. Once it had broken, she drank in the tumult joyfully. She sat tense and miserable longing to get to bed. She wondered whether it would be of any use to explain to Fräulein that they would be safer in their iron bedsteads than anywhere in the house. She tried to distract her thoughts.﻿﻿ … Fancy Jimmie’s name being Christina.﻿﻿ … It suited her exactly sitting there in her little striped dressing gown with its “toby” frill. How Harriett would scream if she could see them all sitting round. But she and Harriett had once lain very quiet and frightened in a storm by the sea﻿—the thunder and lightning had come together and someone had looked in and said, “There won’t be another like that, children.” “My boots, I should hope not,” Harriett had said.

For a while it seemed as though cannon balls were being thumped down and rumbled about on the floor above; then came another deafening crash. Jimmie laughed and put up her hand to her loosely-pinned topknot as if to see whether it was still there. Outcries came from all over the room. After the first shock which had made her sit up sharply and draw herself convulsively together, Miriam found herself turning towards Solomon Martin who had also stirred and sat forward. Their eyes met full and consulted. Solomon’s lips were compressed, her perspiring face was alight and determined. Miriam felt that she looked for long into those steady, oily half-smiling brown eyes. When they both relaxed she sat back, catching a sympathetic challenging flash from Gertrude. She drew a deep breath and felt proud and easy. Let it bang, she said to herself. I must think of doors suddenly banging﻿—that never makes me jumpy﻿—and she sat easily breathing.

Fräulein had said something in German in a panting voice, and Bertha had stood up and said, “*I’ll* get the Bible, Fräulein.”

“Ei! Bewahre! Ber*tha*!” shouted Clara. “Stay only here! Stay only here!”

“Nein, Bertha, nein, mein Kind,” moaned Fräulein sadly.

“It’s really perfectly all right, Fräulein,” said Bertha, getting quietly to the door.

As Fräulein opened the great book on her knees the rain hissed down into the garden.

“Gott sei Dank,” she said, in a clear childlike voice. “It dot besser wenn da regnet?” enquired the housekeeper, looking round the room. She began vigorously wiping her face and neck with the skirt of the short cotton jacket she wore over her red petticoat.

Ulrica broke into steady weeping.

Fräulein read Psalms, ejaculating the short phrases as if they were petitions, with a pause between each. When the thunder came she raised her voice against it and read more rapidly.

As the storm began to abate a little party of English went to the kitchen and brought back milk and biscuits and jam.

===Chinese===

米丽亚姆巴不得她们能看见闪电，好对炸雷有个准备。她若是独自一人，就会盯着闪，每闪过一次就把手指塞进耳朵。那声响的震荡让她无法忍受。可雷一旦炸过，她便快活地痛饮那喧腾。她绷得紧紧的，可怜兮兮地盼着上床睡觉。她纳闷，向弗劳莱恩小姐解释一句——她们待在各自的铁床架上，比在这宅子里的任何地方都更安全——会不会有用。她试着岔开自己的念头。……想想看，吉米的名字竟是克里斯蒂娜。……真再合适她不过了，穿着那件小小的条纹晨衣、领口一圈“托比”（toby）褶边坐在那里。哈丽特要是看得见她们这满屋子的人，非尖叫不可。可她和哈丽特也曾在海边的雷雨里安安静静、提心吊胆地躺着——雷和闪电一起来，有人探进头来，说“不会再有这样的了，孩子们。”“我的靴子，我希望不会，”哈丽特当时说。

有一阵子，仿佛一颗颗炮弹在头顶的楼板上砸得咚咚滚跳；接着又是一声震耳欲聋的炸响。吉米笑了，抬手摸了摸她那松松别着的发髻，像要看看它还在不在。满屋子响起惊叫。最初那一下惊得她猛地坐直、整个人抽紧之后，米丽亚姆发现自己转向了同样惊动起来、坐直了身子的所罗门·马丁。她们的目光满满相接，彼此商量。所罗门的嘴唇抿得紧紧的，冒着汗的脸发着光，透出决断。米丽亚姆觉得自己朝那双镇定的、油亮的、含着半笑的棕色眼睛望了很久。两人一同松弛下来时，她向后靠回椅背，接住了格特鲁德投来的一个会意的、挑衅的闪光。她深深吸了一口气，觉得又骄傲又安然。炸吧，她对自己说。我得想成是门突然被撞上——那种事从来吓不着我——于是她安安稳稳地呼吸着坐定了。

弗劳莱恩小姐曾用喘息的嗓音用德语说了句什么，贝莎便站起身来说：“*我*去拿《圣经》，弗劳莱恩。”

“Ei！Bewahre（哎！使不得）！贝*莎*（Ber*tha*）！”克拉拉喊道。“就待在这儿！就待在这儿！”

“Nein，贝莎，nein，mein Kind（我的孩子）。”弗劳莱恩小姐悲切地呻吟道。

“真的完全没事的，弗劳莱恩。”贝莎说着，静静走到门边。

弗劳莱恩小姐把那本大书在膝上翻开时，雨嘶嘶地泻进花园。

“Gott sei Dank（感谢上帝）。”她用清亮的、孩子般的嗓音说。“It dot besser wenn da regnet（下雨了，就好些了吧）？”女管家环视着房间问道。随即拽起她罩在红衬裙外面的短棉袄的衣襟，起劲地擦起脸和脖子来。

乌尔丽卡放声匀净地哭了起来。

弗劳莱恩小姐诵读《诗篇》，短句一句句迸出，像一句句祈愿，句与句之间有停顿。雷声一起，她便提高嗓音压过去，读得更快。

暴风雨开始减弱时，一小队英国人去了厨房，端回了牛奶、饼干和果酱。

===Original===
“You will be asleep, Miss Hendershon.” Miriam started at the sound of Ulrica’s wailing whisper. Fräulein had only just gone. She had been sitting on the end of Emma’s bed talking quietly of self-control and now Emma was asleep. Ulrica’s corner had been perfectly quiet. Miriam had been lying listening to the steady swishing of the rain against the chestnut leaves.

“No; what is it?”

“Oh, most wonderful. Ich bin so empfindlich. I am so sensible.”

“Sensitive?”

“Oh, it was most wonderful. Only hear and I shall tell you. This evening when the storm leave himself down it was exactly as my Konfirmation.”

“Yes.”

“It was as my Konfirmation. I think of that wonderful day, my white dress, the flower bouquet and how I weeped always. Oh, it was all of most beautifullest. I am so sensible.”

“Oh, yes,” whispered Miriam.

“I weeped so! All day I have weeped! The all whole day! And my mozzer she console me I shall not weep. And I weep. Ach! It was of most beautifullest.”

Miriam felt as if she were being robbed.﻿﻿ … This was Ulrica.﻿﻿ … “You remember the Konfirmation, miss?”

“Oh, yes, I remember.”

“Have you weeped?”

“We say *cry*, not weep, except in poetry﻿—weinen, to cry.”

“Have you cry?”

“No, I didn’t cry. But we mustn’t talk. We must go to sleep. Good night.”

“Gute Nacht. Ach, wie empfindlich bin ich, wie empfindlich.﻿﻿ …”

Miriam lay thinking of how she and Harriett on their confirmation morning had met the vicar in the Upper Richmond Road, having gone out, contrary to the desire expressed by him at his last preparation class, and how he had stopped and greeted them. She had tried to look vague and sad and to murmur something in spite of the bull’s-eye in her cheek and had suddenly noticed as they stood grouped that Harriett’s little sugar-loaf hat was askew and her brown eye underneath it was glaring fixedly at the vicar above the little knob in her cheek﻿—and how they somehow got away and went, gently reeling and colliding, moaning and gasping down the road out of hearing.

===Chinese===
“您还没睡着吧，亨德森小姐（Miss Hendershon）。”乌尔丽卡哀哀的耳语一响，米丽亚姆一惊。弗劳莱恩小姐刚走不久。她方才坐在爱玛床尾，静静谈论着自制，此刻爱玛睡着了。乌尔丽卡那角落一直悄无声息。米丽亚姆一直躺着，听雨均匀地刷着栗子叶。

“没有；怎么了？”

“哦，最不可思议了。Ich bin so empfindlich（我这么多愁善感）。I am so sensible（我好敏感）。”

“是sensitive（敏感）吧？”

“哦，那真是最最不可思议了。只要您肯听，我就讲给您听。今天晚上，暴风雨落下来的时候，跟我的坚信礼（Konfirmation）一模一样。”

“是啊。”

“就跟我的坚信礼一样。我想起那个奇妙的日子，我的白裙子，花束，还有我怎么一直哭。哦，一切都美到了极点。我这么多愁善感。”

“哦，是啊。”米丽亚姆悄声说。

“我哭得那么凶！一整天我都在哭！整整一整天！我妈她安慰我，叫我别哭。可我还是哭。Ach（啊）！真是美极了。”

米丽亚姆只觉得自己像在被洗劫。……这才是乌尔丽卡。……“您还记得坚信礼吧，小姐？”

“哦，记得，我记得。”

“您weeped（哭过）没有？”

“我们说*哭*（cry），不说weep（泣），除了在诗里——weinen，就是to cry。”

“您cry过吗？”

“没有，我没有cry。可我们不能再说话了。得睡了。晚安。”

“Gute Nacht（晚安）。Ach，wie empfindlich bin ich（我多么多愁善感），wie empfindlich。……”

米丽亚姆躺着，想起她和哈丽特在行坚信礼的那个早晨，怎样在上里士满路（Upper Richmond Road）上遇上牧师——她们出门了，正违背了他在最后一次预备课上表达的心愿——他怎样站住同她们打招呼。她当时竭力想显得恍惚而哀伤，想含含糊糊咕哝点什么，偏偏腮帮子里含着那颗圆形硬糖（bull's-eye），而站作一堆时又忽然发现，哈丽特那顶小小的糖锥帽歪在一旁，帽子底下那只棕色的眼睛越过她腮上的糖球，直勾勾地瞪着牧师——后来她们不知怎么脱的身，一路走去，轻轻地打着晃、你碰我撞，又是呻吟又是喘气，直到走出牧师的听力之外。

===Original===
Early next morning Judy came in to tell Emma and Ulrica to get up at once and come and help the housekeeper make the rooms tidy and prepare breakfast. Miriam lay motionless while Emma unfolded and arranged the screens. Then she gazed at the ceiling. It was pleasant to lie tranquil, open-eyed and unchallenged while others moved busily about. Two separate, sudden and resounding garglings almost startled her to thought, but she resisted, and presently she was alone in the strange room. She supposed it must be cooler after the storm. She felt strong and languid. She could feel the shape and weight of each limb; sounds came to her with perfect distinctness; the sounds downstairs and a low-voiced conversation across the landing, little faint marks that human beings were making on the great wide stillness, the stillness that brooded along her white ceiling and all round her and right out through the world; the faint scent of her soap tablet reached her from the distant washstand. She felt that her short sleep must have been perfect, that it had carried her down and down into the heart of tranquillity where she still lay awake, and drinking as if at a source. Cool streams seemed to be flowing in her brain, through her heart, through every vein, her breath was like a live cool stream flowing through her.

She remembered that she had dreamed her favourite dream﻿—floating through clouds and above treetops and villages. She had almost brushed the treetops, that had been the happiest moment, and had caught sight of a circular seat round the trunk of a large old tree and a group of white cottages.

She stirred; her hands seemed warm on her cool chest and the warmth of her body sent up a faint pleasant sense of personality. “It’s me,” she said, and smiled.

“Look here, you’d better get up, my dear,” she murmured.

She wanted to have the whole world in and be reconciled. But she knew that if anyone came, she would contract and the expression of her face would change and they would hate her or be indifferent. She knew that if she even moved she would be changed.

“Get up.”

She listened for a while to two voices across the landing. Millie’s thick and plaintive with her hay fever and Bertha’s thin and cold and level and reassuring.﻿﻿ … Bertha’s voice was like the morning, clean and cool.﻿﻿ … Then she got up and shut the door.

The sky was a vivid grey﻿—against its dark background the top of heavy masses of cloud were standing up just above the roofline of the houses beyond the neighbouring gardens. The trees and the grey roofs and the faces of the houses were staringly bright. They were absolutely stiff, nothing was moving, there were no shadows.

A soft distant rumble of thunder came as she was dressing.﻿﻿ … The storm was still going on﻿﻿ … what an extraordinary time of day for thunder﻿﻿ … the excitement was not over﻿﻿ … they were still a besieged party﻿﻿ … all staying at the *Bienenkorb* together.﻿﻿ … How beautiful it sounded rumbling away over the country in the morning. When she had finished struggling with her long thick hair and put the hairpins into the solid coil on the top of her head and tied the stout doubled doorknocker plait at her neck, she put on the rose-madder blouse. The mirror was lower and twice as large as the one in the garret, larger than the one she had shared with Harriett. “How jolly I look,” she thought, “jolly and big somehow. Mother would like me this morning. I *am* German-looking today, pinky red and yellow hair. But I haven’t got a German expression and I don’t smile like a German.﻿﻿ … She smiled.﻿﻿ … Silly, baby-face! Doll! Never mind. I look jolly. She looked gravely into her eyes.﻿﻿ … There’s something about my expression.” Her face grew wistful. “It isn’t vain to like it. It’s something. It isn’t me. It’s something I am, somehow. Oh, *do* stay,” she said, “do be like that always.” She sighed and turned away saying in Harriett’s voice, “Oo﻿—crumbs! This is no place for *me*.”

===Chinese===
第二天一早，朱迪进来叫爱玛和乌尔丽卡立刻起床，去帮女管家收拾房间、预备早饭。爱玛把屏风一扇扇展开、摆好的时候，米丽亚姆一动不动地躺着。随后她凝望天花板。别人忙忙碌碌地走动，她却安然躺着，睁着眼，无人来扰，多惬意。两声各别的、突兀而响亮的漱喉咙声几乎惊得她要思索起来，她抵住了，不多时这间陌生的房间里就只剩她一人。她想，暴风雨过后一定凉快些了。她觉得又强健又慵懒。她能觉出每条肢体的形状和分量；种种声音清清楚楚地传到她这里：楼下的声响，平台那头一段低低的交谈，那是人们正在这浩大宽广的静默上留下的小小的、淡淡的印记——那静默沿着她白色的天花板铺展，四围将她裹住，一直漫出到世界之外；她那块香皂的淡淡气味从远处的洗脸架上飘到她这里。她觉得她那一小觉必定睡得极完美，把她一路带到了安宁的中心，她此刻还醒着躺在那里，像就着泉眼饮水。清凉的水流似乎正在她脑中流、流过她的心、流过每一条血脉，她的呼吸也像一道活的清凉的水流，流过她的身体。

她记起自己做了那个心爱的梦——飘过云层，飘在树梢和村庄之上。她几乎擦到了树梢，那是最幸福的一刻，还望见一棵大老树的树干周围一圈圆椅，和一小片白色的农舍。

她动了动；双手搁在凉凉的胸口上，显得温热，身体的暖意向上送出一缕淡淡的、愉快的自我之感。“这是我。”她说，笑了。

“我说，你最好起床了，亲爱的。”她喃喃道。

她想把整个世界都请进来，同它和解。可她知道，万一真有人来，她就会缩起来，脸上的表情就会变，人家就会恨她，或者毫不在意。她知道，哪怕她只是动一动，她也会变掉。

“起床。”

她听了一会儿平台那头的两个声音。米莉的嗓音厚厚的、哀哀的，带着她的枯草热，贝莎的嗓音细细的、冷冷的、平平的、令人安心。……贝莎的嗓音像这个早晨，干净、凉爽。……然后她起了床，关上门。

天空是一种鲜明的灰——在深暗的天幕上，大片大片的云团在邻家花园之外那一排房屋的屋脊线正上方耸立着。树、灰色的屋顶和房屋的正面亮得刺眼。它们全然僵直，没有一样东西在动，没有影子。

她穿衣的时候，远处传来一声低低的、柔和的雷声。……暴风雨还在继续……这样的时辰打雷，多么不寻常……这场兴奋还没有完……他们仍是一伙被围困的人……全都一起待在“蜂巢”（*Bienenkorb*）里。……雷声在早晨的乡野上滚滚远去，听上去多么美。等她同那头又长又密的头发搏斗完，把发夹插进头顶那结实的发髻，把脖颈上那根粗粗的对折门环辫（doorknocker plait）扎好，她穿上那件玫瑰茜红色的衬衫。这面镜子比顶楼那面低，也足足大了一倍，比她同哈丽特合用的那面还大。“我今天看上去多痛快，”她想，“痛快，而且不知怎么显得高大了。妈妈今天早上会喜欢我的。我今天*真*是副德国长相，粉红的脸，黄头发的。可我没有德国人的表情，笑起来也不像德国人。……她笑了。……傻乎乎的娃娃脸！洋娃娃！不要紧。我看上去痛快。她郑重地望进自己的眼睛。……我的表情里有种什么东西。”她的脸变得怅惘起来。喜欢它并不是虚荣。它是某种东西。那不是我。那是某种我所是的东西，不知怎么的。哦，*千万*留着吧，她说，千万永远这样。她叹了口气，转过身，用哈丽特的嗓音说：“哎呀——老天！这地方可*不*适合*我*。”
